it sends; where else should this little work turn, if not to **YOU, AUGUST CAESAR**? <note>original: "Auguste Caesar" – a title used here for the Holy Roman Emperor Charles VI to evoke the prestige of the Roman Empire.</note> For it is yours by right of ownership, being the offspring of your servant. It is yours by origin, because under the auspices of your illustrious ancestors, my music took its beginning and drew its growth. You, exalted Monarch, have given me courage; you have supplied the expenses; and by the most merciful nod of your kindness, it now comes forth into the light. Therefore, whatever benefit the studious youth shall take from it, the whole must be credited to **YOUR MAJESTY**. For this reason, you will not disdain to look with kind eyes upon that which is yours in every respect. You have approved the labors of my past life with such merciful assent—and how great a task it is to satisfy your exquisite and select taste—that I have no doubt you will embrace this, nearly my last work, like a swan song, with that same kindness. Relying on this hope, prostrate at your sacred feet, I remain until death

# PREFACE
->TO<-
## THE READER.

Some will perhaps wonder why—since there exist so many monuments by most excellent men who have written about **Music** <note>original: "Musica"</note> quite learnedly and abundantly—I have applied myself to this kind of writing. This is especially true at this time when, music having become almost arbitrary, **Composers** <note>original: "Compositores"</note> wish to be bound by no precepts and no rules, and dread the very names of "Laws" and "School" as if they were death itself. I wish my intentions were known to these people: certainly, there have existed very many writers, famous for both their learning and authority, who have left behind most rich writings on **speculative music** <term>speculative music: the theoretical, philosophical, and mathematical study of music, focusing on how sound works rather than how to write it</term>, but regarding **active music** <term>active music: the practical application of music, specifically the craft of composition and performance</term>, their writings are quite meager and not at all clear. For the most part, they were content with certain examples, not at all concerned with finding an **easy method** <note>original: "methodo facili"</note> by which beginners might climb step-by-step, as if by a ladder, toward the attainment— <meta>The text cuts off here at the end of the page; the catchword "nem" completes the word "adeptionem" (attainment) on the following page.</meta>

<vocab>Music, Composers, speculative music, active music, easy method</vocab>

<summary>Johann Joseph Fux introduces his preface by explaining that while music theory is well-documented, there is a lack of clear, practical instruction for composition. He critiques contemporary composers for rejecting traditional rules and sets out to provide a "step-by-step" method for students.</summary>
<keywords>Johann Joseph Fux, music theory, speculative music, active music, musical pedagogy, composition rules, counterpoint, 18th-century music</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Preface to the Reader" from the previous page. The catchword "blica" from the bottom of this page connects to "publica" (the Republic or the common good) on the next. The author is addressing the pedagogical void in music education and defending the need for rules in composition.</meta>

->❧( ❦ )❧<-

...might reach the top. Neither the harshest haters of the School <note>original: "Scholæ oſores." Here, "School" refers to the traditional, rigorous study of counterpoint and musical theory.</note>, nor the corruption of the times, deter me.

For **medicine** <note>original: "medicina"</note> is prepared for the sick and for those not enjoying good health. Although my labor does not aim so far, nor do I claim so much strength for myself, as to believe I could check the course of a torrent rushing headlong out of its banks, or recall **Composers** <note>original: "Compoſitoreſque"</note> from their licentious **heresy** <note>original: "hæreſi." Fux uses religious language to describe the trend of ignoring musical rules.</note> of writing back to their senses. As far as I am concerned, let everyone follow their own counsel. My purpose is to bring aid to the **youth** <note>original: "adoleſcentibus"</note> aspiring to this art, many of whom I have known—and still know—who are endowed with a wonderful talent and are burning with a supreme zeal for this **discipline** <note>original: "diſciplinæ"</note>; yet, being destitute of both money and a **Teacher** <note>original: "Magiſtro"</note>, they are unable to quench their desire, which is like a long-standing and desperate thirst for excellence. For this reason, many years ago I began to search my mind, sparing no labor or study in finding an **easy method** <note>original: "methodo facili"</note>, exactly like that by which a child is first taught to recognize letters, then to syllable, then to join several syllables, and finally to read and write; and not without good reason. For when I had observed, while using this method in giving lessons, that students made marvelous progress in this art in a short span of time, I thought I would bring no small help to this profession if I were not only to make this method public for the sake of studious youth, but also share whatever I have acquired through nearly thirty years of continuous practice while serving three **August Emperors of the Romans** <note>original: "A. A. Romanorum Imperatoribus." Fux served under Leopold I, Joseph I, and Charles VI.</note> (of which alone I may be allowed to boast) with the Repub—

<summary>Fux defends his pedagogical method against contemporary critics, comparing his systematic approach to music to the way children learn to read. He expresses a desire to help talented, less fortunate students by sharing the expertise he gained from thirty years of service to the Imperial court.</summary>
<keywords>music education, counterpoint, pedagogy, music theory, musical rules, Imperial patronage, Johann Joseph Fux</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's preface and begins the Appendix. It picks up from the previous page's discussion on sharing musical knowledge for the common good. The catchword "blica" from the previous page is here completed as "publica" (public/commonwealth).</meta>

<image-desc>A horizontal row of decorative fleurons and ornaments at the top center of the page.</image-desc>

...commonwealth of Music <note>original: "blica Muſica." This completes the word "publica" from the previous page's final line.</note> faithfully. Furthermore, as Plato reminds us in the works of Cicero: we are not born for ourselves alone; our country claims a part of our birth, our parents a part, and our friends a part. Moreover, you will easily notice, beloved **lover of music** <note>original: "Philomuſe." From the Greek 'philos' (loving) and 'mousa' (music/muse).</note>, that in this **Treatise** <note>original: "Tractatu"</note>, I have given very little space to the speculative side of the art, but more effort to the practical side, as it is more necessary—for all the praise of virtue consists in action.

Finally, for easier understanding and so that the truth might shine more brightly, I have chosen to present the second part as a **dialogue** <note>original: "dialogicè." A common educational format in the 18th century where a master and student converse.</note>. By **Aloysius**, the **Teacher**, I mean that most famous light of Music, the man from **Palestrina** <note>original: "Præneſtinum." Referring to Giovanni Pierluigi da Palestrina (c. 1525–1594), whom Fux considered the supreme master of polyphony.</note> (or as others prefer, Praestinus), to whom I owe everything I know in this science, and whose memory, as long as I live, I shall never cease to honor with the greatest duty of piety. By the name **Joseph**, I address the **Student** <note>original: "Diſcipulum." Joseph is Fux's own first name, representing himself as a humble student of the great master's style.</note> who is eager to learn the musical art. Lastly, do not let the simple style of my writing offend you; for I claim no mastery of Latin except by the **right of return** <note>original: "jure poſtliminii." A legal term referring to the recovery of rights by someone returning from exile; Fux suggests his Latin is a functional tool he has reclaimed, rather than a polished, oratorical display.</note>; I would rather be understood than seem a great orator. Farewell, enjoy, and be kind.

# APPENDIX.

If, kind Reader, you find anything that strays from the proper order of writing, I trust you will bear it with a calm mind, once you understand that [this was written] amidst frequent illnesses, sometimes lasting for several months, and indeed...

# OF THE FIRST BOOK
## CHAPTER I.

<image-desc>A large historiated woodcut initial 'M' depicting a man on the left playing a lute and a landscape scene on the right with figures and a tree.</image-desc>
Music is a generic name, extending very broadly and containing many species: such as celestial, terrestrial, natural, artificial, historical music, etc. <note>In the 18th century, music was still often categorized into ancient divisions like *musica mundana* (the music of the universe/spheres) and *musica humana* (the harmony of the human body and soul).</note> For my part, serving brevity and utility rather than curiosity, I have proposed to treat only artificial music <note>original: "artificiali"; music created by human skill and artifice.</note>. It is twofold: *speculative* <note>original: "speculativa"; the theoretical study of mathematical ratios and musical laws.</note> and *practical*. Regarding the first, namely the *speculative*, we shall speak in this Book, and I will briefly touch upon only those things which seem necessary for a fuller attainment of Practice; the second part, namely the *practical*, I shall leave to be treated more extensively in the Second Book. Since our Music is concerned with sound as its object, we must first deal with that. Let it be, therefore:

## CHAPTER II.
### Concerning Sound.

Mersenne <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a French mathematician and music theorist known as the "father of acoustics."</note>, in the first book of his *Harmonie* <note>original: "Harmon." referring to his work *Harmonie Universelle*.</note>, proposition 2, following the mind of Aristotle, says that sound is the motion of the air. This certainly cannot be understood of just any air, because air left to itself undoubtedly moves, and yet according to the same Aristotle, cited by Mersenne in the same place: *air is devoid of sound*; from which it follows, what-

<sig>A</sig>
<meta>catchword: what-</meta>

<summary>Fux begins his treatise by defining music and narrowing his focus to the theoretical principles necessary for composition. He then introduces the study of sound, referencing the acoustic theories of Marin Mersenne and Aristotle.</summary>
<keywords>music theory, speculative music, practical music, sound, acoustics, Marin Mersenne, Aristotle, definition of music</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the physics of sound from the previous page, transitioning into the mathematical foundations of music theory. It establishes that while physicists study the nature of sound, musicians study the ratios between sounds.</meta>

header: Chapter III. concerning Numbers,

...that any movement of the air is not sound, but rather air affected by some external force, enclosed and compressed, is the efficient cause of sound. However, let this be the investigation of Physicists; for the Musician does not consider sound as such, or (as they say) in the abstract, but relatively, and taken under a certain comparison, by which sound differs from sound by reason of pitch and depth <note>original: "acuminis & gravitatis"; literally sharpness and heaviness, referring to high and low musical pitches.</note>. Since this comparison is made primarily with the help of numbers, it follows that:

# CHAPTER III.
## *Concerning Numbers, their Proportions, and Differences.*

**B**y Numbers, I mean the Elements of Arithmetic:
->1. 2. 3. 4. 5. 6. 7. 8. 9.<-

The Musician uses these as handmaidens to find their intervals, comparing number with number, and inquiring into their distances, differences, excesses, and deficits.

There are many kinds of numbers. However, having left aside others which do not serve our purpose, we shall mention only the following, namely: *rational, irrational; radical, and irradical.* Rational numbers are those which can be divided by a certain number so that nothing remains, such as 6 with 2 and 3; or 12 with 2, 3, 4, and 6. *Irrational* numbers are those which cannot be measured or divided in such a way, such as 3, 5, 7, and 9 with respect to 2 <note>In this context, "irrational" refers to numbers that are not evenly divisible by a specific divisor, rather than the modern mathematical definition of non-repeating decimals.</note>. *Radical* numbers are those which cannot be reduced into smaller numbers while preserving the proportion they constitute <note>These are what we would call "lowest terms" or "prime to each other" in a ratio, such as 2:3.</note>; *Irradical* numbers, on the other hand, are the opposite.

It is established that from *proportions*, or the comparison of one quantity to another, various distances and differences arise of their own accord. The question is now: what is this *Proportion*? It is defined by Euclid <note>Euclid (c. 300 BCE), the Greek mathematician whose "Elements" provided the foundational definitions for ratios and proportions used in music theory.</note> and others as *the relation of two quantities, one to the other, in the same proximate kind.* *Quantity*, which is mentioned here, is of two kinds: *continuous*, which exists in a physical body, such as a line, a board, a surface, etc.; and *discrete*, which is found in some multitude, such as a people, numbers, etc. By "relation" <note>original: "habitudinem"</note> is understood that connection which exists from one quantity to another; furthermore, it is said: *in the same proximate kind*; by which we are cautioned not to establish a proportion between a continuous quantity and a discrete one—such as from a physical line to a number—but rather from line to line, and from number to number.

<meta>catchword="Quam-"</meta>

<vocab>Arithmetic, Musician, rational, irrational, radical, irradical, Proportion, Euclid, continuous quantity, discrete quantity</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the mathematical basis of music theory, explaining how musicians use numbers and proportions to measure the distance between high and low sounds. It distinguishes between continuous quantities (like physical objects) and discrete quantities (like numbers), noting that proportions must compare like with like.</summary>
<keywords>arithmetic, musical ratios, Euclid, mathematical proportion, continuous quantity, discrete quantity, pitch relations</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter III regarding the mathematical nature of music. The previous page introduced types of numbers (rational, irrational, radical); this page moves into how those numbers form proportions, which are the basis for musical intervals like octaves and fifths.</meta>

# CHAPTER III. (Continued)
### *On Proportions and their Differences.*

<sig>A 2</sig>

**A**lthough the Musician may achieve his goal through continuous quantity <note>Physical measurement, such as the length of a string on an instrument.</note>, by measuring lines, strings, and so on, because that dimension must ultimately be reduced to numbers—namely, by how many parts one line exceeds another in length, and by how much it is shorter—therefore I have decided to discuss in this place only proportion, which usually arises from discrete quantity <note>Individual units or numbers that can be counted, rather than a continuous physical length.</note>, or from the comparison of number to number.

It is clear that the definition of proportion brought forward above is general and fits any kind of proportion; however, a specific one must be established that suits no discipline other than Music. It is as follows: *Musical Proportion is the condition or relation of a sounding number to a sounding number.* This definition is clear in itself from what has been said above and requires no further explanation.

This proportion can be of two kinds: *equality* and *inequality*. The proportion of *equality* arises from the comparison of an equal number to an equal number, such as 1 to 1, 2 to 2, 3 to 3, etc. The proportion of *inequality* is produced by the comparison of unequal numbers, for example, 1 to 2, 3 to 4, 5 to 8, etc.

It is established that musical intervals—that is, consonances and dissonances—are produced by the diversity of sounds; from this it is gathered that those proportions whose terms or extremes rely on the equality of numbers are neither suitable for producing intervals, nor can the unison be included under the name of "intervals" for that same reason <note>Since an interval requires a "gap" or distance between two different pitches, a unison (1:1 ratio) technically has no distance.</note>. A different judgment must be made about the unison in practice, which I shall carry out when I speak about each consonance and dissonance individually. Furthermore, from the comparison of an unequal number to an unequal number, many classes of proportions can exist, which the Musician is accustomed to restricting to five: *the Multiple, Superparticular, Superpartient, Multiple Superparticular, and Multiple Superpartient genera.* The following chapters will demonstrate that each of these genera encompasses almost infinite species.

---

### CHAPTER IV. On the Multiple Kind.

When the smaller number is contained by the larger exactly twice, it will be called *double*; if three times, *triple*; if four times, *quadruple*; and so on to infinity. See, if you please, examples of some of these species <note>original: "specierum"; in this context, "species" refers to the specific types of mathematical ratios within a broader category or "genus."</note>.

# CHAPTER V.
## On the second Kind of Proportions.

Which is called <term>superparticular</term> <note>A ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number once, plus one single part of the smaller number (e.g., 3:2 or 4:3). These ratios are the basis for the most important musical intervals like the fifth and fourth.</note>. Before I approach the explanation of this kind, it must be explained what an <term>aliquot part</term> and an <term>aliquant part</term> are. 

An *aliquot* part, which is also called *multiplicative*, is that which, when multiplied, always perfectly restores its whole. For example: 3 is an aliquot part of the numbers 6, 9, 12, 15, and 18, because 3 taken twice adequately makes 6, its whole; likewise, the same number three multiplied by itself <note>The author uses "multiplied by itself" loosely here to mean "taken three times" to reach 9.</note> produces 9, its whole; and so with the others. 

An *aliquant* part, or *additive* <note>original: "aggregativa"</note>, is that which, taken in any way, does not equal its whole; it must either exceed it or fall short of it. For example: 3 is an aliquant part of the number 5; because 5 contains 3 once, and in addition two of its parts—that is, the number two—which, taken once, is not enough to restore the three, and taken twice, exceeds it. This is what is required for the essence of an aliquant, or additive, part.

These things having been established, I say: the *superparticular kind* is a proportion whose larger number contains the smaller once, and in addition its...

...an aliquot part <note>original: "partem aliquotam"; a part that divides into the whole perfectly, like 2 into 4.</note>. For example, in this proportion: 3/2. In the **sesquialtera** <note>The 3:2 ratio, which in music produces the interval of a Perfect Fifth.</note>, the larger number contains the smaller once, and at the same time contains one aliquot part of it; which is clearly evident through the division of the larger number by the smaller: $^3/_2. | 1 \text{ \textonehalf}$. Here, the number three contains the number two, and additionally its half part, which when taken twice makes exactly two, its whole.

Infinite species of this kind can exist, which also receive different names according to the diversity of the numbers: such as *Sesquialtera, sesquitertia, sesquiquarta, sesquiquinta, sesquisexta, etc.*

Before I prepare myself for the method of forming this kind, it is necessary to know what the little word *Sesqui* means: namely, that this word signifies the half of some whole. From this it is clear that this particle can be applied to no other species of this kind except the sesquialtera, in which alone the larger number includes the smaller once, and additionally its half part; for in the **sesquitertia** proportion $^4/_3.$ <note>The 4:3 ratio, which in music produces the interval of a Perfect Fourth.</note>, the larger number contains the smaller once indeed, but not a half, but only a third part of it: $^4/_3. | 1 \text{ \textonefrac13}$. In this proportion $^5/_4.$ <note>The 5:4 ratio, known as a Major Third.</note>, the part remaining from division is not a half, but a fourth part: $^5/_4. | 1 \text{ \textonefrac14}$. , and so in all other species. However, because it is already accepted by use—or rather by misuse—that this particle is prefixed to all species of this kind, I have judged it more advisable to change nothing to avoid confusion.

This kind is formed in this easy way. Let two numbers be taken, leaving aside unity, in the order they follow each other in the <term>Pythagorean table</term> <note>The standard multiplication table used since antiquity.</note>, placing the larger above and the smaller below, as: $^3._2, ^4._3, ^5._4, ^6._5, ^7._6, ^9._8$ etc. Behold six ratios, or proportions, differing in species; the first of which is called *sesquialtera*, the second *sesquitertia*, the third *sesquiquarta*, the fourth *sesquiquinta*, the fifth *sesquisexta*, the sixth *sesquioctava* <note>The 9:8 ratio, a Whole Tone.</note>, and each of these takes its name from its denominator, or the smaller term placed below.

All ratios consisting of their <term>root terms</term> <note>original: "terminis radicalibus"; the simplest form of a ratio (e.g., 3:2 is the root of 6:4).</note> can also exist outside of root terms, which is achieved by doubling the terms of the ratio. For example: let the root terms of the sesquialtera ratio be doubled (unless one prefers to say *sesquialterae* <note>A grammatical correction from the masculine to feminine form.</note>, neglecting the laws of Grammarians as we are accustomed to do with technical terms) $^3/_2.$ in this way $^6/_4.$, which ratio is of the same sesquialtera kind outside its root terms. # CHAPTER VI.
## On the third Type of Proportion.

The third type of proportions is called **superpartient** <term>superpartient: A ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number once, plus more than one "part" of the smaller number (for example, 5:3, where 5 contains 3 once plus two-thirds of 3).</term>, *whose larger term contains the smaller once, and additionally some parts of it that are not aliquot parts, but "aliquants," or collective parts.* <note>original: "non aliquotas, sed aliquantas, seu aggregativas." An aliquot part (like 2 in 6) divides the whole exactly; an aliquant part (like 4 in 6) does not.</note>

# CHAPTER VI.
## On the third Type of Proportion.

...the difference will be two, and the proportion must be called **superbipartiens** <term>superbipartiens: A ratio where the larger number contains the smaller once, plus two additional parts (e.g., 5:3).</term>, as in this proportion $5:3$, where the difference is two.

If the difference is three, as in this proportion $8:5$, it is called a **supertripartiens** proportion <note>original: *proportio supertripartiens*; literally "three parts over."</note>.

If the difference is four—for example, $9:4$ <note>The author identifies the difference here as four, though $9-4$ is five; he likely intended the ratio $9:5$ to fit the "superquatripartiens" definition.</note>—the proportion will be called **superquatripartiens**, and so on for other numbers.

In forming this type of proportion, the following rule should be observed: take the third number from the **Pythagorean table** <term>Pythagorean table: A grid used to study multiplication and the relationships between numbers.</term>, which is 3, to serve as the smaller term of the proportion. Then, skipping the number 4 (the next in the natural order), take the number 5 for the larger term of the proportion, in this manner: $5:3$. This will be called **superbipartiens thirds**, which is the first species of this class.

The prefix *super* indicates that the smaller number is contained once within the larger, plus some additional parts of it. What is meant by the prefix *bi* <note>meaning "two"</note> is evident from what was said just above. Furthermore, for a fuller understanding of the different species and names of this class, it is helpful to divide the larger term of the proportion by the smaller. For example: $5 \div 3 = 1 \frac{2}{3}$. Here, the numerator of the fraction (the two) indicates the **difference** <note>original: *terminum differentialem*</note> of the proportion—showing that the larger number contains the smaller once, plus two parts. The denominator of the fraction (the three) shows what those extra parts are, namely two **thirds**. Therefore, the ratio just cited, $5:3$, is correctly called "superbipartiens thirds." From this, it is clearly seen that the numerator of the fraction indicates the difference of the proportion (how many parts of the smaller term the larger contains in addition to the whole), and the denominator indicates the quality of those parts remaining from the division. Let us now look at the various species and names of this class, so that the truth of what has been said may shine forth more clearly.

->The same proportions outside of their root terms <note>These are multiples of the 5:3 ratio.</note>.<-

->10. 15. 20.<-
->6. 9. 12.<-

# Chapter VI. On the third Type of Proportion.

Perhaps it might seem to someone that these proportions—specifically 10:6 with a difference of 4—are not at all equivalent to the previous 5:3 with a difference of 2, since in the former the differential term <note>The "differential term" is the number resulting from subtracting the smaller term from the larger.</note> is two and the parts remaining from division are thirds, whereas in this one (10:6) the differential term is four and the parts remaining from division are sixths. However, this does not at all prevent these proportions existing outside of their **radical terms** <term>radical terms: The lowest possible whole numbers that express a ratio; what we would today call a "simplified fraction."</term> from being of the same species as the first (5:3), as is demonstrated by the **golden rule of reduction** <note>original: *auream reductionis regulam*; a method for simplifying fractions by finding a common divisor.</note>.

Let a number be sought which, by dividing each term of the proportion, exhausts the whole with no remainder. For example, in this proportion 10:6, the common divisor is the number 2, because it divides both the number ten and the number six so that nothing remains; from ten it produces 5, and from six it produces 3. This proportion is **superbipartiens thirds** <term>superbipartiens thirds: A ratio of 5:3, where the larger number (5) contains the smaller (3) once, plus two-thirds of the smaller number (2).</term> consisting of radical numbers, which is the same result found in the others.

This is the rule by which a test is conducted to see whether a certain proportion consisting of terms outside of the radical ones is of the same species as its original proportion. This rule certainly has its place in proportions where both terms consist of **rational numbers** <note>original: *numeris rationalibus*.</note>, or those which can be measured together by a certain common divisor. However, because it happens that some proportions outside of the radical terms consist of **irrational numbers** <note>In historical mathematical texts, "irrational" was sometimes used to describe numbers that do not share an obvious common factor, or to describe complex relationships where the underlying ratio is obscured.</note>, and for that reason they cannot be reduced to their origin through a common divisor—as in these proportions: <gloss>22. 33. / 14. 21.</gloss> **superquatripartiens sevenths**—another rule of testing, which is usually called the **cross-rule** <note>original: *regula cruciata*; known today as cross-multiplication.</note>, will have to be applied.

# CHAPTER VII.
### *On the fourth Type of Proportion, called the multiple superparticular.*

This composite category contains as many species as its simple superparticular counterpart <note>The "superparticular" category involves ratios like 3:2 or 4:3. The "multiple" version adds a whole number to that, resulting in ratios like 5:2 (2 and 1/2).</note>. It is a **Proportion** where the larger term includes the smaller *twice*, *three times*, *four times*, etc., and simultaneously one aliquot part <term>aliquot part: a part that, when multiplied, exactly equals the whole (e.g., 2 is an aliquot part of 4).</term> of it. It is constructed from the species of its simple superparticular category in this manner:

Let us take, for example, the first species of the superparticular category in its radical terms, namely the **sesquialter** 3/2 <term>sesquialter: A ratio of 3:2, where the larger number is 1.5 times the smaller.</term>. Add the smaller number to the larger, and you will have 5; then place the smaller number of that same sesquialter (which is 2) beneath the larger number. The resulting proportion is 5/2, which is the first species of this category. It is called the **double sesquialter** because its larger number contains the smaller twice, plus one aliquot part, as is clear from the division: 5 / 2 = 2 1/2. See the examples of this species:

# Chapter VII. On the fourth Type of Proportion, etc.

<header>Chapter VIII. On the fifth Type of Proportion, etc.</header>

### Triple sesquiquintal. <note>This ratio (3 1/5 or 16:5) occurs when the larger number contains the smaller three times plus one-fifth part of it.</note>

Here it should be noted that the first example of any triple proportion placed here consists of <term>radical terms: the lowest possible whole numbers that express a ratio, similar to a simplified fraction.</term>; the remaining two immediately following the first are outside the radical terms. I will not linger here any longer, for whoever has well grasped in their mind what has been said so far will have very little trouble in constructing Fourfold, Fivefold, Sixfold proportions, and so on, and finding their species, both within and outside of radical terms.

# CHAPTER VIII.
## On the fifth Type of Proportion, called multiple superpartient.

In this Type, the larger term contains the smaller *twice, three times, four times, etc.*, along with some <term>aliquant part: a part that does not divide the whole exactly, such as 2/3 or 3/4.</term> of it. It is composed by taking the radical terms of its simple superpartient proportion and adding them together to create the new larger term. For example, take the ratio 5/3 <note>original: "5/3," which is the superbipartient proportion (1 2/3).</note>. These, through addition, make 8. By then placing the smaller term of said proportion (3) beneath the larger (8), they constitute this proportion: 8/3. This is the first species of this type, and it is called **double superbipartient thirds** <note>This represents the ratio 2 2/3.</note>. Several examples follow to clarify this species.

### Double superbipartient thirds.

## Second Species.

This is called **double supertripartient**, taking the rest of its name from the remaining parts <note>A "supertripartient" ratio involves a remainder of three parts, such as 2 3/4 or 2 3/5.</note>.

<header>Chapter VIII. On the fifth Type of Proportion, etc.</header>

->Double supertripartient fourths. <note>A ratio of 11:4. The larger number contains the smaller twice, with three parts of the smaller (three-fourths) remaining.</note><-

| **11.** | **22.** | **33.** |
|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| **4.** | **8.** | **12.** |

->Double supertripartient fifths. <note>A ratio of 13:5. The larger number contains the smaller twice, with three parts of the smaller (three-fifths) remaining.</note><-

| **13.** | **26.** | **39.** |
|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| **5.** | **10.** | **15.** |

The same judgment applies to the remaining species, such as: triple, quadruple, quintuple, etc., superbi- <note>meaning a two-part remainder</note>, supertri- <note>meaning a three-part remainder</note>, superquatripartient <note>meaning a four-part remainder</note>, by recalling what was said above regarding the third type of proportion, the superpartient.

Here it must be noted that all proportions mentioned so far are of **greater inequality**, because in all these numbers the larger is found placed above, and the smaller below; however, if the numbers are inverted, placing the larger below and the smaller above, the proportion will be of **lesser inequality**. For example: 1/2, 2/3, 3/4; and by adding the particle *sub*, it will be called *subdouble*, *subsesquialter*, *subsesquitertian*, etc. Furthermore, it should be observed that proportions inverted in such a way also invert the value of the notes. For example, in the sesquialter proportion of greater inequality (3/2), three <term>minims: a musical note value, similar to a half note in modern notation.</term> constitute the <term>tactus: the basic beat or pulse in Renaissance and Baroque music, often directed by a hand motion.</term> and a triple time; but when the numbers are inverted, namely in the subsesquialter (2/3), two minims constitute the tactus and a duple time: a fact which I assume is known here from <term>notated music: specifically "Musica signatoria," the practice of reading and writing musical signs.</term>.

Furthermore, since proportions should not only be viewed in themselves, but also insofar as they can be added, subtracted, multiplied, and divided—and since the logic of proportions is different from that of simple numbers—we must now see what method is to be used in adding, subtracting, multiplying, and dividing proportions. Before we proceed to this operation, it must be known beforehand that the relationship between the two <term>extremes: the two numbers that form the ratio.</term> constituting a proportion is usually called a *ratio* <note>original: "ratio"</note>. For example, in this proportion: 1 to 2, which is called a double ratio, and is commonly placed in this way: 2/1. However, if it is written with a dividing line as 1/2 or 2/3, it is no longer called a ratio, but a fraction, commonly known as a **Bruch** <note>original: "Bruch," which is German for 'fraction'.</note>; it indicates not the relationship of a whole to another whole, but rather the parts remaining from the division of some whole. *If a ratio is placed in this way, 3 to 2, the first number is called the **antecedent**, and the second the **consequent**. Likewise, if a ratio is found in this way, 3/2, the upper number is called the **antecedent**, and the lower the **consequent**.*

<summary>This page concludes the definitions of multiple superpartient ratios and explains the difference between proportions of greater and lesser inequality. It also introduces the mathematical distinction between a ratio (comparing two wholes) and a fraction (parts of a single whole) in a musical context.</summary>
<keywords>multiple superpartient, greater inequality, lesser inequality, sub-proportions, tactus, minims, ratio, fraction, antecedent, consequent, music notation</keywords>

<header>Chapter IX. On Division.</header>
<sig>D 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: then <note>original: "da," likely leading into "deinceps" or similar in the original Latin</note></meta>

Since all musical intervals have their origin, either immediately or mediately, from the division of the octave as if from a mother, order demands that we begin with it; let it be, therefore:

# CHAPTER IX.
## On Division.

The duty of Division is to divide the whole into its parts; this is what common division does with simple numbers. In dividing ratios, or related numbers, another type of division must be applied, as I have already said, whose task is to produce two or more proportions from one. This can be done in a threefold manner; therefore, a threefold Division must also be established: *Arithmetic*, *Harmonic*, and *Geometric*.

### On Arithmetic Division.

*Arithmetic* Division is perhaps so called because the numbers produced by it follow each other in natural order, with no interval cast between them. It is achieved by placing a middle term that is equally distant from both extremes, so that it produces equal differences and unequal proportions, establishing a larger proportion between the smaller numbers and a smaller proportion between the larger numbers. A paradigm <note>an illustrative example</note> will make the matter clearer. Let there be a double proportion <note>a 2:1 ratio, which defines the octave</note> in this ratio: 4 to 2. The number three <note>original: "ternarius"</note>, which can enter between these two extremes, is the arithmetic divisor; for it divides the extremes so that they are equally distant from each, and it creates equal differences: 4, 3, 2. For by the same interval that four is distant from three—namely, by a unit—three is distant from two. It exhibits unequal proportions: the sesquitertian <note>a ratio of 4:3, forming a musical fourth</note> and the sesquialter <note>a ratio of 3:2, forming a musical fifth</note>. The sesquitertian is between 4 and 3, which is a smaller proportion than the sesquialter; and the sesquialter is between 3 and 2, which is larger than the sesquitertian.

->Sesquitertian. Sesquialter.<-
->4. 3. 2.<-
->1. 1.<-
->Equal differences.<-

What must be done if the extremes of some ratio are found so joined that they cannot receive a middle term? For example, 2 to 1. <note>The number 1.5 would be the middle, but the author is working with whole numbers.</note> The response is that the extremes must be doubled, in this ratio: 4 to 2. If someone desires to make more middle terms and proportions, they should be tripled...

<header>Chapter X. On Harmonic Division.</header>

...the extremes will be, for example, 2 and 1. Let a tripling of those extremes be applied, and the same double proportion <note>A 2:1 ratio, or octave.</note> will be maintained, established in this ratio: 6 to 3. Within this, two middle terms can enter, five and four, resulting in: 6, 5, 4, 3. Thus, three different proportions are produced: a *sesquiquinta* <note>original: "sesquiquinta"; a 6:5 ratio, known in music as a minor third.</note> between 6 and 5; a *sesquiquarta* <note>original: "sesquiquarta"; a 5:4 ratio, or a major third.</note> between 5 and 4; and a *sesquitertia* <note>original: "sesquitertia"; a 4:3 ratio, or a perfect fourth.</note> between 4 and 3. From this, it is gathered that the greater the distance between the extremes, the more middle terms can enter, as in a sixfold proportion: 6 to 1. This is fit to receive four middle terms—5, 4, 3, 2—and produces five different proportions in this ratio: 6, 5, 4, 3, 2, 1, as is clear from what has already been said. There is also another way of dividing arithmetically; but I, striving for brevity as I previously mentioned, think this suffices for my purpose.

# CHAPTER X.
## *On Harmonic Division.*

Harmonic Division is achieved through a middle term, or divisor, which produces unequal proportions by establishing larger proportions among larger numbers and smaller proportions among smaller numbers; so that the differences are unequal in a similar way. The definition will become clearer by example: Let us take an octave already arithmetically divided in this ratio: 4, 3, 2. Let the two extremes be multiplied by the middle term: 4 multiplied by 3 gives 12; 2 multiplied by the same middle term of 3 produces 6, which is the other extreme of the harmonic proportionality: 12 and 6. Furthermore, let the extremes of the arithmetic proportionality be multiplied together—namely, 4 by 2—and the result is 8. This 8 is the true harmonic divisor, dividing the double proportion in this way: 12, 8, 6. It produces two dissimilar proportions: a *sesquialtera* <note>original: "sesquialtera"; a 3:2 ratio, or a perfect fifth.</note> between 12 and 8, and a *sesquitertia* <note>original: "sesquitertia"; a 4:3 ratio, or a perfect fourth.</note> between 8 and 6. By exhibiting the larger proportion among the larger terms and the smaller among the smaller, it makes the differences dissimilar in the same way, as in the following example.

| **Sesquialtera** (3:2) | | **Sesquitertia** (4:3) |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| 12 | 8 | 6 |
| \ | / | \ | / |
| **4** | | **2** |
| | **Dissimilar Differences** | |

<note>In Harmonic Division, the larger interval (the fifth, 12:8) is placed below the smaller interval (the fourth, 8:6) when considering string length. This is the mathematical inverse of Arithmetic Division.</note>

By the same method, the matter must be established in dividing the proportions of other types and species. Let a har-

<header>Chapter XI. On Geometric Division.</header>

...harmonically, a ratio of the *superparticular* genus <note>original: "generis superparticularis"; a category of ratios where the larger number contains the smaller number plus one part of it, such as 3:2 or 4:3.</note>, specifically the *sesquialtera* <note>original: "sesquialtera"; a 3:2 ratio, which in music defines the interval of a perfect fifth.</note> 3 to 2, let it first be divided arithmetically in this way: 6, 5, 4. Let the two extremes be multiplied by this arithmetic mean: six times 5 gives 30; again, 4 times 5 will give 20. We will then have the two extremes of harmonic proportionality in this ratio: 30 to 20. To find the harmonic middle, let the extremes of the arithmetic proportionality be multiplied together: 6 times 4 gives 24, which is the sought-after harmonic divisor, in this ratio: 30, 24, 20; and so it goes for the others.

# CHAPTER XI.
## On Geometric Division.

**G**eometric Division is that in which a middle term, placed between two extremes, creates two equal ratios <note>original: "proportiones"; here meaning the relationship between the numbers.</note> but unequal differences. From this, it is clear that this division cannot take place except within the *multiple genus* <note>original: "genere multiplici"; ratios where the larger number is a perfect multiple of the smaller, such as 2:1, 3:1, or 4:1.</note>. This division is established in the following way: take a certain ratio from the multiple genus consisting of its *radical numbers* <note>original: "numeris radicalibus"; the lowest possible integers that express the ratio.</note>—for example, a double ratio, 2 to 1. Let each of the extremes be multiplied by itself—namely, 2 times 2, which gives 4, and 1 times 1, which remains 1. From this multiplication arises the following quadruple ratio: 4 to 1. 

Furthermore, to find the middle term, or Geometric divisor, one must know what a square number is and what its root is. I say, therefore, that a simple number is the root of itself multiplied by itself. For example, two is the root of four, because four is born from two multiplied by itself. From this, it is inferred that two, as the root of four, is the true divisor of the said quadruple ratio to be divided geometrically: 4, 2, 1. From this division, two equal ratios arise—namely, double ratios—with unequal differences, for the difference between 4 and 2 is two, while 2 differs from 1 by a unit.

From this I conclude that in any geometrically divided ratio, the larger number is a square and its root is the geometric divisor; for greater clarity, it pleases me to set down here several square numbers with their roots.

| Roots. | 2. | 3. | 4. | 5. | 6. |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Squares. | 4. | 9. | 16. | 25. | 36. |

From this, it is further gathered that any species of the multiple genus can be divided geometrically in the said manner: such as triple, quadruple, quintuple, etc.

<header>Chapter XII. On the Multiplication of Ratios.</header>
<meta>catchword: effi-</meta>

# CHAPTER XII.
## On the Multiplication of Ratios.

**M**ultiplication of Ratios <note>original: "Multiplicatio Rationum"; in this historical context, "multiplication" refers to combining ratios to find a common numerical ground, rather than simple arithmetic multiplication.</note> is nothing other than the translation of two or more proportions consisting of radical terms <note>original: "terminis radicalibus"; the lowest possible integers used to express a ratio, such as 2:1.</note> into remote terms <note>original: "terminos remotos"; the same ratios expressed using larger numbers, such as 4:2 or 6:3.</note>, while the types of proportions remain the same. It does not matter whether the ratios are of different or the same genus, provided that one is not of greater inequality and the other of lesser inequality, in which case the operation could not be carried out. <note>In the mathematics of this period, a ratio of "greater inequality" is one where the first number is larger (e.g., 3:2), and "lesser inequality" is where the first number is smaller (e.g., 2:3). You cannot combine "upward" and "downward" intervals in this specific way.</note> Observe the method that must be followed in multiplying two proportions. For example, let a double ratio <note>ratio dupla; 2:1, which represents an octave.</note> and a sesquialtera ratio <note>ratio sesquialtera; 3:2, which represents a perfect fifth.</note> be multiplied, arranged in this way:

->2. 3.<-
->1. 2.<-

Multiply the antecedent <note>the first number in a ratio</note> of the double ratio with the antecedent of the sesquialtera: 2 times 3, and 6 will emerge. Furthermore, multiply the antecedent of the sesquialtera ratio with the consequent <note>the second number in a ratio</note> of the double ratio: 3 times 1, and you have 3. When this is compared with the 6 that arose from the first multiplication, it produces a double ratio consisting of more remote terms—namely, 6 to 3. Afterward, multiply the antecedent of the double ratio with the consequent of the sesquialtera: 2 times 2, and the result is 4. When this is compared with 6, it creates the sesquialtera ratio in this manner:

->6. 6.<-
->3. 4.<-

By this, it is established that this multiplication does not change the proportions, but transfers them from radical terms to more remote terms. <note>The final set of numbers 6, 4, 3 contains both the 2:1 ratio (6:3) and the 3:2 ratio (6:4).</note> However, for someone wishing to multiply three proportions in a single operation, another method must be employed. Let there be multiplied a double ratio (2:1), a sesquialtera (3:2), and a superbipartient ratio of thirds <note>original: "superbipartiens tertias"; a 5:3 ratio, which represents a major sixth.</note> (5:3).

->2. 3. 5.<-
->1. 2. 3.<-

Multiply the antecedent of the double with the antecedent of the sesquialtera, and you will have 6. Furthermore, multiply the consequent of the double with the antecedent of the sesquialtera, and 3 will emerge. Afterward, multiply the consequent of the double with the consequent of the sesquialtera: 1 times 2, and 2 will be produced. These numbers are placed in this order:

->6. 3. 2.<-

Now, multiply these three numbers by the antecedent of the superbipartient ratio of thirds: 6 times 5 gives 30; 3 times 5 produces 15; 2 times 5 results in 10. Finally, multiply again the smallest of the three numbers produced from the first multiplication with the consequent of the superbipartient ratio of thirds—namely, 2 times 3—and we will have 6. All these remote numbers, when placed in this order:

->30. 15. 10. 6.<-

<vocab>Multiplication of Ratios, double ratio, sesquialtera ratio, superbipartient ratio of thirds, radical terms, remote terms</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the mathematical process of combining multiple musical ratios into a single string of numbers by "multiplying" them. This allows different intervals, like octaves and fifths, to be expressed using a common set of larger integers without changing their proportional value.</summary>

<keywords>multiplication of ratios, radical terms, remote terms, double ratio, sesquialtera, superbipartient ratio, musical mathematics, proportion theory</keywords>

## CHAPTER XIII.
### On the Addition of Ratios.

**F**rom what has been said so far, it is clear that through Division, several proportions are made out of one; through Multiplication, the same proportions are indeed retained, yet translated to remote terms <note>original: "terminos remotos"; larger numbers that represent the same proportion.</note>. Next, it must be seen how one single proportion is made through the addition of two or more proportions. <note>In historical music theory, "adding" ratios is equivalent to multiplying the fractions together to find the resulting interval.</note> Let us first establish the addition of two proportions: for example, a sesquialtera with a sesquitertia <note>ratio sesquitertia (4:3), representing a perfect fourth.</note>. Let the larger terms of both proportions be placed on the left, and the smaller on the right, in this manner:

| 3. | 2. |
|----|----|
| 4. | 3. |

Multiply the smaller term of the sesquialtera with the smaller of the sesquitertia, and you will have 6; then multiply the larger term of the sesquialtera with the larger of the sesquitertia, and you will have 12. Once this is done, one single proportion will be made from the two stated proportions: namely, the double <note>Adding a fifth (3:2) and a fourth (4:3) results in an octave (12:6, which simplifies to 2:1).</note>:

->12.<-
->6.<-
---
->2.<-
->1.<-

The same method is to be held in adding the ratios of several proportions. For example, let there be two sesquialteras and two sesquitertias to be added, arranged in the manner described above.

| 3. | 2. |
|----|----|
| 3. | 2. |
| 4. | 3. |
| 4. | 3. |

Multiply the smaller terms of these proportions placed on the right among themselves: 2 by 2 gives 4; 4 by 3 results in 12; 12 by 3 produces 36. This final product will be the smaller term of the proportion arising through addition—

# CHAPTER XIV.
## On the Subtraction of Ratios.

Since the container must be greater than what it contains, it is certain that this operation neither takes place between two proportions of equal quantity, nor can a larger proportion be subtracted from a smaller one. From this, it follows that it is necessary to know by what rule one proportion is made larger or smaller than another. I say, therefore, that in the superparticular <note>original: "genere superparticulari"; ratios where the numerator is the denominator plus one, such as 3:2 or 4:3.</note> and superpartient <note>original: "superpartiente"; ratios where the numerator exceeds the denominator by more than one, such as 5:3.</note> genera, the larger the terms of a certain proportion, the smaller that proportion is, and the smaller the interval produced from it. For this reason, the sesquialtera <note>3:2, a perfect fifth.</note> is larger than the sesquitertia <note>4:3, a perfect fourth.</note>; this is larger than the sesquiquarta <note>5:4, a major third.</note>, and so on for the other proportions of this kind, whose terms always increase as the numbers ascend. The same rule must be held in the superpartient genus. The superbipartient-third ratio <gloss>5/3</gloss> <note>A major sixth.</note> is larger than the supertripartient-fifth, namely, <gloss>8/5</gloss> <note>A minor sixth.</note>, because the former yields a larger sixth, and the latter a smaller one. On the contrary, it happens in the multiple genus <note>original: "genere multiplici"; ratios where the larger number is a direct multiple of the smaller, like 2:1, 3:1, etc.</note> that the larger the terms, the larger the proportion: hence a triple ratio is larger than a double, a quadruple larger than a triple, and so on.

From what has been said, it is easily apparent which proportion can be subtracted, and of what nature that one must be from which one wishes to subtract. The function of subtraction is understood from its very etymology: namely, to diminish the interval from which the subtraction is made. Now, the method of subtracting is this: let the larger proportion be placed collectively on the left, and the smaller on the right, arranged in the same manner; multiply the antecedent <note>The first or larger term of a ratio.</note> of the larger proportion by the consequent <note>The second or smaller term of a ratio.</note> of the smaller proportion,

<meta>catchword: [smaller] proportion,</meta>

<summary>This page concludes an example of adding ratios to form a quadruple (4:1) proportion and introduces the rules for subtracting ratios. It explains that in music theory, as the numbers in "superparticular" ratios (like 3:2, 4:3) grow larger, the actual musical interval becomes smaller.</summary>

<keywords>subtraction of ratios, superparticular, superpartient, multiple genus, sesquialtera, sesquitertia, ratio reduction, interval size</keywords>

[...of the] minor [terms], and the consequent <note>The second number in a ratio.</note> of the larger with the antecedent <note>The first number in a ratio.</note> of the smaller proportion; the product will yield the proportion reduced through subtraction, and will show the difference intervening between those two proportions. An example <note>original: "Paradigma"</note> will explain the matter more clearly. For instance, if a sesquialtera <note>The ratio 3:2, representing a perfect fifth.</note> is to be subtracted from a dupla <note>The ratio 2:1, representing an octave.</note>; let the antecedent of the dupla be multiplied by the consequent of the sesquialtera, and conversely, the antecedent of the sesquialtera with the consequent of the dupla, and thus the operation will be completed. For greater ease, let two small lines be drawn in the form of a cross from the antecedent of one ratio to the consequent of the other, and vice versa, in this manner:

But to what end are there so many genera and species of proportions? Of what use are those triple Divisions? What do Multiplication, Addition, and Subtraction intend? Although the previous remarks can largely satisfy this question, I believe it will be more fully answered by what is to be said when I discuss each musical interval in particular. I shall begin with the Octave, demonstrating that from it, as if from a Mother, all consonances and dissonances are generated.

# CHAPTER XV.
## On the Octave.

The Octave is so named because it consists of eight sounds; it is the largest of all simple intervals. In Greek it is called the *Diapason* <note>original: "Diapason"; from the Greek *dia pason*, meaning "through all [strings/tones]."</note>, a word that signifies universality or "the whole," because the Octave encompasses all possible intervals; simple in actuality, but composite in potential. Truly, since the Octave, as it contains...

<meta>The previous page concluded the technical demonstrations of how to subtract musical ratios (such as subtracting a perfect fifth from an octave to find a perfect fourth). # Chapter XV. On the Octave.

...[since the rest] of the intervals cannot be understood as contained within it, and the Octave is, as it were, nothing other than a repetition of the unison, it is therefore established that only seven distinct sounds are contained within the Octave. Marcus Tullius Cicero, in *The Dream of Scipio* <note>original: "somnio Scipio." This is a famous text from Cicero's *De re publica* describing the structure of the cosmos.</note>, most elegantly expressed this by drawing a comparison from the sounds of the celestial spheres: *"What, I ask, what is this sound, so great and so sweet, that fills my ears?"*

> *"This is that sound," he replied, "which, though joined by unequal intervals, yet distinct according to a fixed proportion, is produced by the impulse and motion of the spheres themselves; and by tempering high tones with low ones, it creates various uniform harmonies. For such great movements cannot be stirred in silence, and nature demands that the extremes on one side sound low, and on the other, high. For this reason, that highest course of the starry sky, whose revolution is more rapid, moves with a high and sharp sound; while this lunar and lowest one moves with the deepest sound. For the Earth, the ninth sphere, remaining motionless, always clings to the lowest seat, occupying the central place of the universe. Those eight courses, however—in which the power of two, Mercury and Venus, is the same—produce seven sounds distinct by their intervals; a number which is the measure <gloss>measure</gloss> <note>The gloss "n" likely refers to "notandum" or "nota," or perhaps "numerus" (number/measure).</note> of almost all things. Learned men, by imitating this with strings and songs, have opened for themselves a return to this place, etc."*

We shall experience the truth of this matter through the division of the Octave, once we have first examined the nature of the Octave itself.

The Octave has its existence in the **multiple genus** <term>multiple genus: a category of musical ratios where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller, such as 2:1 (dupla) or 3:1 (tripla)</term>; its terms are established as **1 to 2**. For this reason, it is to be judged the most perfect of all intervals, since its first term is the very beginning—namely **unity** <term>unity: the number one, often symbolizing God or the source of all things</term>—and the second is the **binary number** <note>the number two</note>, which is closest to unity. One can clearly observe that this order was maintained by God in all His wonderful works and created things. Hence those celestial Spirits <note>The various ranks of Angels</note>, being closest to their Source in the excellence of their intelligence, surpass all other created things in perfection. Closest to these in nobility comes man, a participant in reason, whom God—the builder and architect of all things—made "a little lower than the Angels." The other living creatures, lacking reason, follow him. These are succeeded by the plants, and finally by inanimate objects. Who does not see that as these things recede further in nobility from their maker or their source, the more imperfect they are? In like manner, the same holds true for musical intervals and their perfection or imperfection; for the larger the terms of a certain pro-

<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion of the cosmic harmony of the spheres as described by Cicero. This page shifts from that celestial metaphor to the practical, mathematical demonstration of musical intervals on Earth, beginning with the physical properties of the Octave.</meta>

...of the ratio, and the further they are from unity, their starting point, the more imperfect the intervals arising from them will be. 

To return the discussion to where it digressed somewhat, perhaps someone will ask why the terms of this ratio, 1:2, sound an Octave? The reason is obvious; for since this ratio is double, and one string is twice as long as the other, it must necessarily produce a sound twice as deep. However, so that this truth may be clear not only to reason but also to the ears, an experiment can be performed on an instrument of the following shape, which is called a Rule <note>original: "Regula," referring to a measuring rod or straight-edge.</note> by the Latins, and a Monochord <note>original: "Monochordon," from the Greek for "single string."</note> by the Greeks—that is, a one-stringed instrument. Yet, a Polychord <note>original: "Polychordon," an instrument with multiple strings.</note>, or an instrument of two strings as shown in the following figure, provides a much easier opportunity for conducting the experiment:

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a musical instrument, identified in the text as a polychordon. It consists of a long, rectangular wooden soundbox with two parallel strings stretched across the top. On the left side are several tuning pins, and on the right is a fixed bridge. A small movable bridge (equuleus) is shown positioned beneath one of the strings to divide its length for measuring proportions.</image-desc>

It would not be worth the effort to linger longer on describing the method of making this instrument, which I trust can be easily constructed by any instrument maker <term>Organopæus: a craftsman who builds musical instruments, such as organs or lutes.</term> simply by looking at this figure. Therefore, I proceed to the demonstration of its use. Let both strings of this instrument, which should be equal in thickness, be accurately tuned in unison, and afterward let either of them be measured with a compass and divided into as many parts as the larger term of the ratio one intends to test; the smaller term of the ratio shows where the movable bridge <term>equuleus: literally a "little horse," this is a small block of wood moved under a string to change its vibrating length and pitch.</term> should be placed—a type of support on which the strings rest, commonly called a Saddle <note>original: "Ephippium" (Latin) or "Sattel" (German).</note>. For example, if the sounds of a double ratio (1:2) are to be tested, let either string be divided into two parts according to the larger term of the ratio, while the other string is left at its full length. The smaller term of the ratio, 1, indicates at which part—namely the middle—the string is to be divided by placing the movable bridge underneath. Once this is done, the string of one part will sound an Octave compared to the string of two parts. In a similar way, one must proceed in testing the sounds of the 3:2 ratio <term>sesquialtera: a ratio where the larger number is 1.5 times the smaller, creating the interval of a Perfect Fifth.</term>: let it be divided according to...

<meta>The previous page established the physical construction of a polychord or monochord as a tool for measuring musical proportions. This page continues the practical instruction on how to manipulate these strings to produce specific intervals, specifically the Fifth.</meta>

...the larger number of this proportion into three parts. One of these two strings will produce the first part, and the second part—indicated by the smaller term—will produce the desired sound, namely the Fifth <note>original: "quintam"</note>. This method is to be used in seeking out the sounds of all other proportions, with this observation: one of the strings should be divided into as many parts as the larger number of the ratio indicates, and the other string should be shortened by placing a movable bridge <term>equuleus: a small, sliding bridge used to change the vibrating length of a string</term> at the point indicated by the smaller term of that same ratio.

From what was said above in Chapters nine and ten regarding Arithmetic and Harmonic Division, it can be easily understood how the Octave <note>original: "Octava, sive Diapason"</note> is divided. Specifically, it is divided harmonically in this ratio: 6, 4, 3, because it presents a Fifth in the lower part and a Fourth in the higher part. However, when divided arithmetically in this way: 4, 3, 2, it gives a Fourth in the lower part and a Fifth in the higher part. These two intervals, therefore, arise from the first division of the Octave, and each of them must be examined individually.

# CHAPTER XVI.
## Concerning the Fifth, or Diapente.

The Fifth, called *Diapente* <note>original: "Diapente," from the Greek "dia" (through) and "pente" (five)</note> in Greek, is so named because it occupies the fifth place in the natural order of the Diatonic scale. It has its place in the superparticular <term>superparticular: a ratio where the numerator is exactly one greater than the denominator, such as 3:2 or 4:3</term> class of ratios, specifically in this "one-and-a-half" ratio <note>original: "sesquialtera"</note>: 3:2. It is indeed a perfect consonant, though less perfect than the Octave. This is because the terms of the Octave, namely 1 and 2, when joined by addition, result in 3; whereas the terms of the Fifth, namely 2 and 3, joined in the same way, result in 5. This number 5 is further removed from unity <note>The number 1, seen as the divine source of all numbers.</note>, its starting point, than the number 3. It is not out of place to investigate how far this privilege of perfection extends.

In my opinion, the number six <note>original: "senarius"</note> can be established as the limit, not so much because it is considered the first "perfect number" <note>In mathematics, a number equal to the sum of its divisors, like 1+2+3=6.</note>, but because nature herself inclines toward producing such consonances whose terms, when added together, do not exceed the number six. This can be tested in the unison (1 to 1), the octave (2 to 1), the fifth (3 to 2), the twelfth (3 to 1), and the double octave (4 to 1). We find by experience that if one of these strings is struck, the other—even if untouched—resonates of its own accord or certainly vibrates in sympathy. This occurs for no other reason than that their terms stay below the number six...

The author argues that the "perfection" of a musical interval is determined by how close the sum of its ratio's terms is to the number one, with six serving as the natural limit for sympathetic vibration.</summary>

# CHAPTER XVII.
## Concerning the Fourth, or Diatesseron.

The second interval arising from the division of the Octave is the *Fourth*, in Greek called the *Diatesseron* <note>original: "Diatesseron," from the Greek "dia" (through) and "tessares" (four)</note>. It has its existence in the superparticular <term>superparticular: a ratio where the numerator is exactly one greater than the denominator, such as 3:2 or 4:3</term> class, specifically in these terms: 4:3, which is the sesquitertial <term>sesquitertial: a ratio of 1 1/3 to 1, or 4:3</term> proportion. A famous question, and one full of difficulty, is whether the fourth is a consonant interval? All the Pythagoreans <note>followers of Pythagoras who believed the universe and music were governed by mathematical ratios</note> and other authors, most celebrated for the glory of their learning and authority, affirm it; but before I reveal my own mind on this matter, I would wish them to declare which Fourth they mean: the one arising from the harmonic division of the Octave, or the arithmetic? For example, if it is from the harmonic division:

<image-desc>A musical staff with three diamond-shaped notes arranged vertically to show an interval division of an octave into a fifth and a fourth.</image-desc>

Who does not see that this paradigm contains not a fourth at all, but rather a fifth and an octave? For intervals must be measured from the base and foundation, not from the middle parts to the [higher] parts. Otherwise, by the same logic, they might say a major sixth is present in the following example:

seems to produce a major sixth. But if they mean the fourth produced from the arithmetic division in this manner:

From what has been said so far, it becomes clear that the fourth and fifth are produced from the division of the Octave. Now we must see what kind of intervals arise from the division of the fifth itself.

## CHAPTER XVIII.
### Concerning the Division of the Fifth, or Diapente.

From the division of the Fifth, or Diapente <note>original: "Diapente," from the Greek "dia" (through) and "pente" (five)</note>, two intervals are born: the sesquiquarta <term>sesquiquarta: a ratio of 1 1/4 to 1, or 5:4</term> (5:4), or major third <note>original: "ditonus," literally "two tones"</note>, and the sesquiquinta <term>sesquiquinta: a ratio of 1 1/5 to 1, or 6:5</term> (6:5), or minor third <note>original: "semiditonus," literally "a half-two-tones"</note>. Through harmonic division, the major third is placed in the lower <note>original: "gravi," the "heavy" or bass part</note> part, and the minor third in the upper <note>original: "acuta," the "sharp" or treble part</note> part; conversely, the opposite occurs through arithmetic division.

Hence it is evident that the Fifth consists of a major and minor third, which have their existence, as is clear, in the superparticular <term>superparticular: a ratio where the numerator is exactly one greater than the denominator, such as 5:4 or 6:5</term> class. They are consonances, but imperfect ones—either because their terms joined by addition exceed the number six, or because nature concurs very little in their production <note>The author refers to the "Senario" or the number six, which Renaissance theorists like Zarlino believed limited the "natural" consonant ratios</note>.

Order would now require us to mention the division of the Fourth, or Diatesseron; however, since this interval cannot be divided—because its divisor, or middle term, would have to be the number seven (8 : 7 : 6)—and as this is a mystical number <note>The number seven was often considered "septenary" or "virgin," as it does not factor into the other numbers within the basic musical deck of 1-10</note>, it cannot be used in dividing ratios (as will become manifest to anyone attempting the experiment), we must therefore proceed to the division of the major third, or ditone.

<header>Chapter XIX. On the Division of the Major Third, etc.</header>

# CHAPTER XIX.
->On the division of the Major Third, or Ditone <note>original: "Ditoni," meaning an interval of two whole tones</note>.<-

From the division of the Major Third (represented by the ratio 5:4), two intervals are produced: the <term>sesquioctava: the ratio 9:8, which defines a Major Tone</term>, and the <term>sesquinona: the ratio 10:9, which defines a Minor Tone</term>. These intervals are considered dissonant because they produce a sound that is unpleasant to the ears—perhaps because their combined numerical terms exceed twelve <note>The author refers to "senarium duplicatum," or the number six doubled. In early music theory, ratios involving numbers up to 6 (the "Senario") were often considered consonant; going beyond this was thought to increase complexity and dissonance.</note>, and therefore the "beats" or pulses of such sounds are very difficult to synchronize.

And these are the intervals that are generated through division. Others—such as the Major Sixth (or Major Hexachord), the Minor Sixth (or Minor Hexachord), and all composite intervals—are found through addition; meanwhile, the Major and Minor Semitones and the <term>comma: a very small interval resulting from the difference between two nearly identical tones</term> are found through subtraction, as we shall see below. Now follows:

# CHAPTER XX.
->On the formation of the Major and Minor Sixth.<-

The Major Sixth is so named because it occupies the sixth position in the <term>Diatonic scale: the standard seven-note scale used in Western music</term>. It is composed by joining together a Major Third and a Perfect Fourth <note>original: "quartæ minoris," literally "minor fourth," but referring to the perfect fourth in this context</note>, in this manner:

| | |
|---|---|
| 5. | 4. |
| 4. | 3. |
| 20. | 12. |
| 5. | 3. |

<note>The table shows the multiplication of the ratios for a Major Third (5:4) and a Fourth (4:3). When multiplied (5x4 over 4x3), they result in 20:12, which simplifies to the ratio 5:3, the mathematical definition of a Major Sixth.</note>

From this, it is apparent that the Major Sixth has its existence in the <term>superpartient genus: a category of ratios where the larger number contains the smaller number plus more than one remaining part</term>; specifically, it is the "superbipartient third" <note>original: "superbipartiente tertias," meaning the ratio 5:3, as 5 contains 3 once, with 2 parts left over</note>. This is the first species of this genus. It can also be formed in the way mentioned above in Chapter 7: by taking the third number from the Pythagorean table for the lower term and, skipping the four, taking the five for the upper term. For example: 5/3, a proportion which shows the Major Sixth. It can also be constructed by adding the ratio of a Minor Tone (10:9) to a Perfect Fifth (3:2), by this calculation:

|---|---|
| 3. | 2. |
| 10. | 9. |

| 5. | 3. |

<header>Chapter XX. On the formation of the Major Sixth, etc.</header>

Even though this interval has its place in the <term>superpartient genus: a class of ratios where the larger number contains the smaller number plus more than one remaining part, such as 5:3</term>, whose proportions all produced dissonant intervals according to the <term>Pythagoreans: ancient Greek mathematicians who believed only simple ratios like 2:1 or 3:2 were harmonious</term>, sixths have nevertheless been numbered among the <term>imperfect consonances: intervals that sound pleasing but less stable than "perfect" ones like the octave or fifth</term> over the course of time. Even today, they are used as such in musical composition. This much regarding the Major Sixth.

The Minor Sixth, as was said above, is constructed from the constituent terms of the Major Sixth <gloss>5/3</gloss> by adding a three to the five, from which 8 is generated for the larger term; if the larger term of the Major Sixth is taken as the smaller term, one will have this proportion <gloss>8/5</gloss>, which is the form of the Minor Sixth itself. Moreover, it is created through addition in this manner: the ratio of the <term>sesquitertia: the ratio 4:3, which defines a Perfect Fourth</term> is added to the ratio of the <term>sesquiquinta: the ratio 6:5, which defines a Minor Third</term>, by this calculation:

| | 4. | 3. |
|---|---|---|
| | 6. | 5. |
| (3. | | |
|---|---|---|
| | 24. | 15. |
|---|---|---|
| | 8. | 5. |

which is the ratio of the Minor Sixth <note>In early music theory, adding intervals involves multiplying their ratios: (4/3) x (6/5) = 24/15, which simplifies to 8/5.</note>. At this point, one might ask: since thirds and sixths, both major and minor, are imperfect consonances, which among them should be considered the more "imperfect"? To me, certainly, if we look at their constituent terms, it is clear that the Minor Third is more imperfect than its Major counterpart, because its terms (6 and 5) added together produce 11; whereas the terms of the Major Third (5 and 4), united in the same way, produce 9 <note>The author uses a common Renaissance theory that smaller sums in a ratio indicate a "purer" or more perfect sound.</note>. The same judgment is made regarding the Sixths. However, since in this matter we should consider not so much the ratio of the terms as the "genera" or classes of ratios, and since Thirds have their existence in the <term>superparticular genus: ratios of the form (n+1):n, like 5:4, which were considered more "natural" than others</term>—which is more excellent than the superpartient genus to which the Sixths are subject—therefore the Sixths must generally be held as more imperfect than the Thirds. This opinion is confirmed by the fact that the ears, fully satisfied by the harmony produced by Thirds, seem to desire nothing more,

as in: <image-desc>A musical staff with two intervals. The first interval is a major third (dyad C and E). The second interval is a minor third (dyad E and G). Below the staff, the first is labeled "Major 3rd" and the second "minor."</image-desc> but the same is not true

in the case of sixths, whose harmony—being, as it were, unfinished—that most certain judgment of the ears does not so...

<note>The musical staff repeats the Major and Minor Sixths from the previous example, but adds a low G note beneath each, showing that these intervals "rest" or find their foundation when heard against their root note an octave down.</note>

It remains in this Chapter to inquire about the Seventh, or the <term>Heptachord: an interval spanning seven notes of the scale</term>, both Major and Minor, which are found by a similar method of addition, using the following method: let the <term>sesquiquarta ratio: the ratio 5:4, which defines a Major Third</term> be added to the <term>sesquialtera ratio: the ratio 3:2, which defines a Perfect Fifth</term> in this manner:

| 3. | 2. |
|---|---|
| 5. | 4. |
| 15. | 8. |

and we obtain the <term>superseptempartiens octavas ratio: a "super-7-part" ratio where the larger number (15) contains the smaller (8) plus seven remaining parts; mathematically 15:8</term>, which is the form of the Major Seventh, a dissonant interval. To find the ratio of the Minor Seventh, however, let the <term>sesquiquinta ratio: the ratio 6:5, which defines a Minor Third</term> be added to the <term>sesquialtera ratio: the ratio 3:2, which defines a Perfect Fifth</term>:

| 3. | 2. |
|---|---|
| 6. | 5. |
| 18. | 10. |
| 9. | 5. |

and there arises the <term>superquatripartiens quintas ratio: a "super-4-part" ratio where the larger number (9) contains the smaller (5) plus four remaining parts; mathematically 9:5</term>, constituting the ratio of the Minor Seventh, which is likewise a dissonant interval.

Lastly, it remains here for us to inquire into the ratios of the Tritone and the <term>False Fifth: also known as the diminished fifth</term>, both of which are dissonant intervals. The ratio of the Tritone is found by adding two <term>sesquioctave tones: the "Major Tone" with a ratio of 9:8</term> together with one <term>sesquinone tone: the "Minor Tone" with a ratio of 10:9</term>, by this calculation:

| 9. | 8. |
|---|---|
| 9. | 8. |
| 10. | 9. |
| (9. | |
| 810. | 576. |
| (2. | |
| 90. | 64. |
| 45. | 32. |
