Now that all the intervals contained within the octave in the <term>Diatonic genus: the standard scale of whole steps and half steps</term> have been found—except for the major semitone, the minor semitone, and the comma, since these are to be sought not so much through addition as through subtraction—it seems a new Chapter should be established.

## CHAPTER XXI.

### On the formation of the Major Semitone, the Minor Semitone, and the Comma.

**T**he Major Semitone is the difference, or that interval, by which a <term>sesquitertia: the ratio 4:3, which defines a Perfect Fourth</term> exceeds a <term>sesquiquarta: the ratio 5:4, which defines a Major Third</term>. This is found by the subtraction of one ratio from the other, in this manner:

-> *Sesqui-* 4. 3. *-altera.* <note>The author uses "sesquialtera" here to refer to the broader class of ratios, though the numbers 4:3 specifically denote a sesquitertia.</note> <-
-> *Sesqui-* 5. 4. *-quarta.* <-
---
-> *Diffe-* 16. 15. *-rence.* <-
-> *Major Semitone.* <-

Which <term>sesquiquindecimal ratio: 16:15</term> constitutes the major semitone.

The Minor Semitone is that very difference found through subtraction between a <term>sesquiquarta: 5:4 (Major Third)</term> and a <term>sesquiquinta: 6:5 (Minor Third)</term>. For example:

-> *Sesqui-* 5. 4. *-quarta.* <-
-> *Sesqui-* 6. 5. *-quinta.* <-
---
-> *Sesquivi-* 25. 24. *-gesimal-fourth.* <-
-> *Difference.* <-

The Comma <note>specifically the Syntonic Comma</note> is that difference by which a <term>Major Tone: 9:8</term> exceeds a <term>Minor Tone: 10:9</term>, as is clear through the subtraction of the <term>sesquinona: 10:9</term> from the <term>sesquioctava: 9:8</term>:

-> *Major* 9. 8. *Tone.* <-
-> *Minor* 10. 9. *Tone.* <-
---
-> 81. 80. <-
-> *Comma.* <-

### Chapter XXI. On the formation of the Major Semitone, etc.

These, therefore, are all the simple intervals which arise either immediately or mediately from the division of the Octave made by <term>Ptolemy: Claudius Ptolemy, an influential 2nd-century scholar whose "Intense Diatonic" tuning system is the basis for the ratios used here</term> (which is without controversy the best of all), and which you may see in the following table, placed in the order in which they are produced, along with their constituent terms and names:

->*Simple intervals.*<-

| :--- | :---: | :---: | :--- |
| Octave: | 2. | 1. | *Diapason, or the double ratio.* |
| Fifth: | 3. | 2. | *Diapente, or the sesquialterate ratio (3:2).* |
| Fourth: | 4. | 3. | *Diatessaron, or the sesquitertial ratio (4:3).* |
| Major Third: | 5. | 4. | *Ditone, or the sesquiquartal ratio (5:4).* |
| Minor Third: | 6. | 5. | *Semiditone, or the sesquiquintal ratio (6:5).* |
| Major Tone: | 9. | 8. | *Sesquioctave ratio (9:8).* |
| Minor Tone: | 10. | 9. | *Sesquinonal ratio (10:9).* |
| Major Sixth: | 5. | 3. | *Major Hexachord, or the <term>superbipartient third: a ratio where the numerator is two parts greater than the denominator (5:3)</term>.* |
| Minor Sixth: | 8. | 5. | *Minor Hexachord, or the <term>superbipartient fifth: a ratio where the numerator is three parts greater than the denominator (8:5)</term>.* |
| Major Semitone: | 16. | 15. | *Sesquidecima-fifth ratio (16:15).* |
| Minor Semitone: | 25. | 24. | *Sesquivigesima-fourth ratio (25:24).* |
| Comma: | 81. | 80. | *Sesquioctogesimal ratio (81:80), the tiny difference between a major and minor tone.* |
| Tritone: | 45. | 32. | *The <term>super-thirteen-part thirty-two: a complex ratio of 45:32</term>.* |
| False Fifth: | 64. | 45. | *The <term>super-eighteen-part forty-five: a complex ratio of 64:45</term>.* |

---

Although some of these intervals may be said to be in some way "composite"—because they must either necessarily be constructed by joining intervals (such as the major sixth, from a major third and a fourth; or the minor sixth, from a minor third and a fourth) or at least they can be (such as the fifth from a major and minor third, etc.)—nevertheless, strictly speaking, only those are included under the name of **composites** which, when joined with an Octave, exceed it in extension: such as the ninth, tenth, eleventh, twelfth, etc. These differ from their **simple** counterparts neither in nature nor in use, with a few exceptions. Let us see further by what method they are to be composed.

# CHAPTER XXII.

## On composite intervals, and the method for composing them.

**I**t has been stated elsewhere that those intervals are properly called **composite** which are traditionally formed together with an **Octave**. This is done by joining a specific interval to the Octave through addition <note>In historical music theory, "adding" intervals is performed by multiplying their mathematical ratios.</note>. For instance, for one wishing to compose a **minor ninth**, the ratio of the **major semitone** (16:15) must be added to the ratio of the **Octave** (2:1), in this manner:

| 16. | 15 |
|-----|----|
| 2.  | 1. |
|-----|----|
| 32. | 15.|

To compose a **major ninth**, let a **major tone** (9:8) be added to the Octave, by this ratio:

| 2.  | 1. |
|-----|----|
| 9.  | 8. |
|-----|----|
| 18. | 8. |
|-----|----|
| 9.  | 4. |

In finding the **minor tenth**, a **minor third** (6:5) must be joined to the Octave in a similar way:

| 2.  | 1. |
|-----|----|
| 6.  | 5. |
|-----|----|
| 12. | 5. |

And so on for the others.

If, however, some interval is added to an **Octave placed twice** <note>Meaning a double octave or a fifteenth.</note>, as in this example:

| 2.  | 1. |
|-----|----|
| 2.  | 1. |
| 9.  | 8. |
|-----|----|
| 36. | 8. |
|-----|----|
| 18. | 4. |

The interval produced by the ratio 18:4 shall be called a **major ninth twice-compounded**.

In the same way, if any interval is added to an **Octave placed three times**, it shall be called "thrice-compounded"; for example, if one were to have...

It does not escape me that, given the breadth of the subject, I have scarcely touched upon the matter. Nor did I think more time should be given to this writing, lest I [incur] the censure of **Marcus Tullius Cicero** <note>The author likely refers to Cicero's warnings in *De Officiis* against wasting too much effort on difficult and obscure studies that are not of primary necessity.</note> in...

<meta>This page transitions from the mathematical calculation of compound intervals to a defense of modern musical practice. The author addresses a major debate of the time: the shift from strict mathematical ratios (the ancient Greek system) to "tempered" tuning, which relies on the ear's judgment to make keyboards playable across different keys.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>
<header>34 Chapter XXII. On composite intervals, and their [formation], etc.</header>

...I might run [into trouble], as he <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero</note> warns in Book 1 of *On Duties* <note>original: "de Officiis"</note>, that we should not bestow too much study and great labor upon obscure matters which are, furthermore, unnecessary. For with the introduction of the new system of modern Music, who is so ignorant as not to know that the ancient system has changed, and that those three Greek Genera—the **Diatonic**, **Chromatic**, and **Enharmonic**—have collapsed along with their **Tetrachords** <note>A "four-stringed" scale segment, the building block of ancient Greek music.</note>? Because of this, most musical intervals, though they consist of their own rational proportions, now rely on the judgment of the ears rather than on pure mathematical reason. 

What then? Perhaps someone will object: "Therefore, modern Music—lacking certain and never-failing principles and relying solely on the fallible judgment of the ears—will be unworthy of the name of a **Science**." To this I respond: this in no way opposes the dignity and infallibility of our Music. Indeed, if a separate key <note>original: "clavis"</note> were established on the keyboard for every individual interval—for both major and minor tones, for major and minor semitones, and for even smaller intervals—it would be a different matter. In fact, many experts in Music, both ancient and modern, have demonstrated such things with their specialized **Keyboards** <note>original: "Claviaturis"</note>. In that case, all intervals used today, especially following the division of **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), an influential theorist who argued for "Just Intonation" based on simple mathematical ratios.</note>, could indeed be held to their proper rational proportions. 

However, they saw that the use of such a keyboard was full of difficulty. Nevertheless, wishing to provide for the scarcity of intervals and having set their minds on increasing them and acquiring "imperfect" **consonances** <note>The sounds of thirds and sixths, which were mathematically complex in the old system.</note>, they attempted to divide the tone and the semitone into two equal parts. Once they discovered that this could not be achieved through whole numbers, they called upon the judgment of the ears for help. By taking away a certain almost imperceptible quantity from one interval and adding it to another, they—through this industry—removed the difficulty of the keyboard. They ensured that our Music, having escaped from the prison of a "poverty of intervals," is today able to run most joyfully into the vast field of **modulation** <note>Moving between different keys or musical "colors."</note>; provided that Composers and **Organists** <note>original: "Organœdi"</note> keep themselves within the limits of reason. They must not, through ignorance or an excessive greed for transposing, be driven off course and dare to wander too licentiously through **Chromatic** intervals, especially on the Organ. If that is done, it can hardly be avoided that instead of a pleasing and lovely harmony <note>original: "concentûs"</note>, a most **harsh howling** <note>original: "asperrimus ululatus"; this refers to the "wolf tone," a jarring dissonance that occurs when an instrument is out of tune with itself.</note>, full of offense to the ears, is thrust upon us.

How much benefit and splendor has come to the art of Music from this, and how much praise is therefore due to the first author (the glory of which is rightly attributed to the ancient Philosopher **Aristoxenus** <note>A student of Aristotle who famously argued that the ear, not math, should be the final judge of music.</note>)...

<meta>catchword: with [praiseworthy...]</meta>

<summary>The author argues that modern music is a valid science even though it uses "tempered" tuning (adjusting ratios for playability) instead of strict Greek mathematics. He defends the use of the ear's judgment to overcome the physical limitations of keyboards, while warning organists not to push these adjustments so far that they create discordant "howling."</summary>

<header>The final chapter, on the modern System of Music.</header>

...to be brought forth, I believe it is known to anyone even slightly acquainted with Music.

Although the things I will add below—namely, concerning the modern **System of Music**—seem rather to belong to the second book (which is the practical part), I have thought it more advisable to insert them at the very end of this book, so that the beginning of the next book may lead directly into the prescribed lessons. Therefore, let it be:

## THE FINAL CHAPTER.
### On the Modern System of Music.

With the inequality between Tones and Semitones removed <note>This refers to "tempering" the scale, where the small mathematical differences between different types of steps were smoothed out to make instruments like keyboards easier to play in all keys.</note>, the three Greek genera were abolished—as has been commonly noted above—and it is helpful to recall that here. For this reason, necessity has introduced a different System, which has restricted those three genera <note>The "three genera" were the Diatonic, Chromatic, and Enharmonic scales of ancient Greece.</note> to two: namely, the **Diatonic** and the **Chromatic**. This system has taken the place of the Greeks' system, and I will now demonstrate it expressed in the following layout:

->Modern Diatonic Genus.<-

<image-desc>A single five-line musical staff displaying a diatonic scale in white diamond-shaped notes (semibreves). It begins with a C-clef on the fourth line and shows an ascending and descending sequence of notes spanning an octave and a sixth.</image-desc>

->Modern Chromatic Genus.<-

<image-desc>Two five-line musical staves displaying a chromatic scale using white diamond-shaped notes. The notation includes sharp accidentals represented by a cross-shaped symbol. A C-clef is used on the fourth line of each staff. The first staff shows an ascending chromatic sequence, and the second continues with further chromatic intervals and a descending portion, ending with a final long note.</image-desc>

However, since the insatiable human desire for variety and novelty (which is always looming) was not yet satisfied by either of these Genera, **Composers** <note>original: "Compositores"</note> became accustomed to using both indiscriminately in their **Compositions**, so that today a "mixed genus" is generally accepted in practice. This, I do not at all disapprove of (since one must adapt to the times);

<sig>I 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: Com-</meta>

<summary>The author explains the shift from ancient Greek musical systems to the modern Diatonic and Chromatic systems, noting that contemporary composers often mix both to satisfy the public's desire for novelty.</summary>
<keywords>Modern System of Music, Diatonic Genus, Chromatic Genus, Temperament, Composition, Musical Notation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's transition from mathematical theory to practical music application. Having established the "modern" (tempered) system on the previous page, the author now warns against using complex chromaticism in choral music and begins a catalog of musical intervals, starting with the unison and the second.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>The final chapter, on the modern System of Music.</header>

However, I wish to warn composers not to use this "mixed genus" <note>This refers to the mixing of the Diatonic and Chromatic scales mentioned on the previous page.</note> in compositions intended to be sung *a cappella* <term>a cappella: vocal music performed by a choir without instrumental accompaniment</term> without an organ. Otherwise, they should be certain that they will never achieve the intended effect; for nothing besides the pure **Diatonic genus** <note>The standard seven-note scale, equivalent to the white keys of a piano.</note> is suitable for this style. Having been taught by many instances of practice and experience, I greatly wish for this important warning to be recommended to everyone.

Now, assuming the equality of tones and semitones <note>A reference to "equal temperament," where the mathematical differences between steps are smoothed out to allow instruments to play in all keys.</note>, let us proceed to the intervals in use today, beginning with:

### On the Unison.

It is clear that the **Unison** <note>original: *unisonum*</note> cannot technically be included under the name of "intervals," because its existence consists in a ratio of equality (such as 1 to 1), between whose extremes there is no difference in pitch. However, because its extremes clearly harmonize more perfectly than all other consonances <note>sounds that are stable and pleasing to the ear</note>, and because it has outstanding use in practice, far from being removed from the list of consonances, no practical musician could doubt that it should be established as the most perfect of all consonances. The word's origin itself explains the Unison: that it is the same sound of two strings or voices, expressed in musical notes like this:

<image-desc>Two musical staves displaying unison notes using diamond-shaped semibreves. The upper staff features an alto C-clef with four notes on the middle line. The lower staff features a bass F-clef with four notes on the fourth line.</image-desc>

We will explain the use of this, as well as of the other consonances and dissonances <note>sounds that create tension or "clash"</note>, in the following book. For now, let us contemplate their essence, beginning with the **Second** and ascending in natural order.

### On the Second.

The **Second** is twofold: major and minor. The minor consists of a semitone; the major of a whole tone, as follows:

<margin>minor...</margin>

The **third** is likewise twofold: **major** and **minor**; the latter consists of a tone and a semitone. For example <note>original: *V. G.*, an abbreviation for *verbi gratia*</note>: *re fa*. The major [third consists] of two tones, for example: *ut mi*:

->minor.   major.<-

The **fourth** is threefold: the **perfect fourth**, the **diminished**, and the **augmented**, or tritone. The perfect fourth <note>original: *quarta vera*</note> consists of two tones and a semitone. For example: *ut fa*. The diminished <note>original: *minuta*</note> consists of two semitones and a tone, for example: *mi fa, mi fa* <note>The repetition suggests a chromatic alteration, where one note is raised or lowered by a half-step</note>. The **tritone** consists of three tones, for example: *fa mi*. The examples <note>original: *paradigmata*</note> will show the matter more clearly:

->perfect.   diminished.   Tritone.<-

The third is labeled "Tritonus" above and "fa mi" below, featuring a chromatic alteration to widen the distance to three whole tones.</image-desc>
->*ut fa*.   *mi fa*.   *fa   mi*.<-

The **fifth** is twofold: **perfect** and **diminished**. The perfect [fifth] <note>original: *vera*</note> consists of three tones and a semitone. For example: *ut sol*, *re la*. The diminished fifth <note>original: *falsa*, literally "false fifth"</note> consists of two semitones and a tone <note>The author's mathematical description here is unusual; in modern theory, a diminished fifth consists of two tones and two semitones. This may reflect a specific tuning system of the era</note>. For example: *mi fa*, *re mi fa*.

->*Major 13th* &emsp; *Augmented 13th* <note>original: *superfl.*</note> &emsp; *Minor 14th* &emsp; *Major 14th* &emsp; *Double Octave* <note>original: *bisdiapason*</note><-

Up to this point, consonant and dissonant intervals have been designated together indiscriminately; it remains for these to be described separately, so that which ones they are, and of what kind, may be better understood. What the matter of consonance and dissonance actually is need not be explained at great length, since it is clear from the names themselves: consonance is that which affects the ears pleasantly without any discord and soothes the mind. On the other hand, dissonance is that which, by thrusting something harsh and rough upon the ears, brings offense rather than delight.

# Last chapter on the modern system of music.

## The consonances are

->1st, 3rd, 5th, 6th, 8th, along with their compounds.<-

Of these, some are *perfect*, and some are *imperfect*. The perfect ones are the Unison, the Fifth, and the Octave. The imperfect ones are the Sixth and the Third. The rest—such as the Second, the Fourth, the False Fifth <note>original: *Quinta falsa*; the diminished fifth</note>, the Tritone, and the Seventh, along with their compounds—are dissonances.

And these are the elements by which the entire harmony <note>original: *concentus*</note> of Music is perfected. Its purpose is to delight; a variety of sounds gives birth to this delight. Variety is generated by moving from one interval to another. This transition is achieved through "motion"; therefore, it remains to investigate what this motion is.

*Musical motion* is that progression by which one moves from one interval to another toward a higher <note>original: *auctam*</note> or lower <note>original: *gravem*</note> part. Since this progression usually occurs in three ways, three types must likewise be established:

->*Direct*, *contrary*, and *oblique motion*.<-

*Direct motion* <note>original: *Motus rectus*</note> occurs when two or more parts follow each other in a straight path by ascending or descending, whether this happens by step or by leap, as is clear in the example:

### *Direct motion.*

<image-desc>Musical notation on two five-line staves with four measures demonstrating Motus rectus (direct motion). Diamond-shaped notes move in parallel directions: upwards in the first two measures and downwards in the last two measures.</image-desc>

*Contrary motion* <note>original: *Motus contrarius*</note> is when one part ascends while the other descends, and vice versa, whether by leap or by step. For example:

### *Contrary motion.*

<image-desc>Musical notation on two five-line staves with four measures demonstrating Motus contrarius (contrary motion). In each measure, the two voices move in opposite directions: up/down, down/up, up/down, and down/up.</image-desc>

*Oblique motion* <note>original: *Motus obliquus*</note> is when one part moves by leap or by step while the other remains stationary, as can be seen in the examples:

### *Oblique motion.*

<image-desc>Musical notation on two five-line staves with three measures demonstrating Motus obliquus (oblique motion). In each measure, one voice maintains a constant pitch while the other voice moves either by step or by leap.</image-desc>

<meta>catchword: Co-</meta>

<summary>This page classifies musical intervals into perfect and imperfect consonances versus dissonances, and introduces the three primary types of melodic motion used in harmony: direct, contrary, and oblique.</summary>
<keywords>consonance, dissonance, perfect intervals, imperfect intervals, musical motion, direct motion, contrary motion, oblique motion, music theory</keywords>

# The final chapter on the modern system of music.

Having understood these three types of motion, we must now see in what manner they are to be applied in practical use. This doctrine is contained within the following four fundamental *Rules*.

### *First Rule.*

From a perfect consonance to another perfect consonance, one proceeds by contrary or oblique motion. <note>The author is forbidding parallel perfect fifths and octaves here, a core rule of classical counterpoint.</note>

### *Second Rule.*

From a perfect consonance to an imperfect one, one may proceed by all three types of motion.

### *Third Rule.*

From an imperfect consonance to a perfect one, one proceeds by contrary or oblique motion.

### *Fourth Rule.*

From an imperfect consonance to another imperfect one, one may proceed by all three types of motion.

Where you will notice that oblique motion is permitted in all four types of progression.

Upon the knowledge and correct use of these three motions depends—as we are accustomed to say—the Law and the Prophet <note>original: *Lex, & Propheta*; a biblical allusion to Matthew 22:40, signifying that these rules are the essential foundation upon which the entire art of music rests</note>.

And these were the matters pertaining to theoretical music <term>Theoretica Musica</term> and the innermost secrets of the art which we thought should be briefly declared in the present book; now let us advance our step toward the matter itself and the second part of the work.

# BOOK TWO.
## DIALOGUE.

**Josephus.** I come to you, venerable Master, to be steeped in the precepts and principles of Music.
**Aloysius.** So, you desire to learn the art of musical Composition?
**Joseph.** Indeed I do.
**Aloysius.** Do you not know that the study of Music is an immense sea, not to be exhausted even in the years of Nestor? <note>original: "Nestoris annis"; Nestor was a mythological Greek king famous for his great age, living through three generations of men.</note> Truly, you intend to take up a difficult task, a burden heavier than Mount Etna. <note>original: "Ætnâ gravius"; a reference to the giant Enceladus, who was said to be buried under the Sicilian volcano, symbolizing an immense weight.</note> For if choosing a path in any part of life is full of difficulty—since upon a choice made either rightly or poorly depends the course of one’s fortune for the rest of one's life, whether good or ill—how much more cautious foresight must be used by him who thinks to enter the path of this Discipline, before he dares to take counsel and decide for himself. For the Musician and the Poet are born <note>original: "Musicus enim, & Poëta nascuntur"; a variation of the Latin proverb "Poeta nascitur non fit," meaning that true artistic talent is an innate gift rather than a purely learned skill.</note>. You ought to look back and consider whether you felt yourself driven toward this study from a tender age by a certain singular impulse of nature, and whether you find yourself powerfully moved by the delight of harmony <term>concentûs</term>?
**Joseph.** Most powerfully indeed. For from a time when I did not yet have the full use of reason, I was seized by I know not what intensity of passion, casting all my thoughts and all my care into Music; even now I am so inflamed by a wonderful desire to learn it that I am swept along as if against my will, and day and night sweet melodies seem to echo in my ears; so much so that I seem to have no cause at all to doubt the truth of my calling. Nor does the weight of the labor <note>original: "laboris gravitas"</note> deter me...

<sig>L 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: ...weight [gravitas]</meta>

# Dialogue.

[difficul]ties, which I trust will not be difficult for me to overcome with the help of nature. For I have heard from a certain old man: Study is a pleasure rather than a labor.

**Aloysius.** I am wonderfully delighted to understand the inclination of your nature. I will raise only one more difficulty for you, and once it is removed, I shall enroll you among my students.

**Joseph.** Speak freely, respected Master. But I am certain that neither by this concern of yours, nor by any other motive, will I be deterred from following the plan I have conceived.

**Aloysius.** Perhaps you are tickled by the hope of future riches and an abundance of household goods to embrace this state of life? If that is the case, believe me, change your plan. For it is not Plutus <note>original: "Plutus"; the Greek god of wealth.</note>, but Apollo <note>original: "Apollo"; the god of music, poetry, and the sun.</note> who presides over Parnassus <note>original: "Parnasso"; a mountain in Greece sacred to the Muses and Apollo, representing the home of the arts.</note>. Those who are inflamed by the desire for riches and wish to find a way to them must walk a different path.

**Joseph.** Not at all. I would have you be persuaded that the aim of my desire is nothing other than the love of Music itself, set apart from any desire for gain. Furthermore, I remember being very often warned by my teacher that we should be content with a modest way of living and more eager for virtue, fame, and a good name than for worldly possessions, because virtue is its own reward.

**Aloysius.** I am incredibly glad to have found a youth so suited to my own temperament; but are you familiar with all those things said in the preceding Book concerning <term>Intervals</term> <note>The distance in pitch between two notes.</note>, the division of <term>Consonances</term> <note>Musical intervals that sound stable and harmonious.</note> and <term>Dissonances</term> <note>Musical intervals that sound tense or clashing.</note>, the variety of <term>Motions</term> <note>The way different melodic lines move in relation to one another (e.g., parallel or contrary motion).</note>, and the four <term>Rules</term>?

**Joseph.** I believe that none of those things are hidden from me.

**Aloysius.** Therefore let us proceed to the work, taking our beginning from God himself, three times greatest, the source of all Sciences.

**Joseph.** Before we enter upon the beginning of the lessons, allow me first to ask, venerable Master, what is to be understood by the name <term>Counterpoint</term> <note>original: "Contrapuncti"; the technique of combining two or more melodic lines so they sound harmonious together.</note>, a word so common that I hear it frequently on the lips not only of experts but even of those unskilled in the musical Art?

**Aloysius.** You ask rightly. For this will be the chief object of our study and our labor. You ought to know that in place of modern <term>Notes</term> <note>original: "Notularum"; the symbols used to write down music.</note>, <term>points</term> <note>original: "puncta"; the Latin word for dots or points.</note> were used in ancient times, so that <term>Composition</term> [was made] by points placed against points...

<summary>In this dialogue, Josephus expresses his pure intentions for studying music, leading Aloysius to begin his instruction. The master begins by explaining the etymology of "Counterpoint," noting that it derives from the ancient practice of placing "points" (notes) against one another.</summary>

->Exercises I. Lesson I. Concerning Note against Note.<-
<margin>M</margin>
<meta>catchword: ctas,</meta>

...it was customary for it to be called <term>Counterpoint</term> <note>original: "Contrapunctum"; literally "point against point," referring to the practice of placing one musical note (point) against another.</note>; which even today, regardless of the shapes of other notes, remains in use; and by the name "Counterpoint" is understood a composition worked out according to the rules of the Art. Just as a genus contains species, Counterpoint contains many species, which we shall see in succession. In the meantime, let us begin with the simplest species, namely:

# EXERCISE I.
## FIRST LESSON,
### Concerning Note against Note.

**Joseph.** My first question has been satisfied by your kindness. Now, do not be reluctant to teach what that first species of Counterpoint is: *Note against Note*.

**Aloysius.** I shall tell you. It is the simplest composition of two or more voices and of notes of equal value, consisting of pure <term>Consonances</term> <note>original: "Consonantiis"; intervals like the third, fifth, or octave that sound stable and harmonious to the ear.</note>. Nor does it matter what kind of notes they are, provided they are all of the same value. However, because the <term>semibreve</term> <note>original: "Nota semibrevis"; in modern notation, this is a "whole note."</note> provides an easier way of understanding, I have decided that we should use it in these exercises. Let the beginning be made, therefore, with God as our guide, from a composition of two voices, by placing a certain <term>Cantus firmus</term> <note>original: "Cantum firmum"; literally "fixed song," a pre-existing melody that serves as the foundation for the exercise.</note>, either invented by one’s own judgment or taken from some choral book as a foundation. For example:

<image-desc>A musical staff with a mezzo-soprano clef (C-clef on the second line). It displays a sequence of eleven semibreves (whole notes) representing a Cantus Firmus. The melody begins on C, rises step-by-step to C an octave above, and then descends: C, D, E, F, G, A, B, C, B, A, G. The staff ends with a double bar line.</image-desc>

To each of these notes, let its own particular consonance be given above in the melody, having observed the method of motions and rules expressed at the end of the preceding book, employing for the most part <term>contrary motion</term> <note>original: "motum contrarium"; voices moving in opposite directions.</note> or <term>oblique motion</term> <note>original: "motum obliquum"; one voice stays the same while the other moves.</note>, by the use of which we cannot easily fall into error. There will be need for greater caution in the progression from one note to another by <term>direct motion</term> <note>original: "motum rectum"; voices moving in the same direction.</note>, where, because there is a greater danger of erring, greater attention must also be paid to the rules.

**Joseph.** From my understanding of the motions and rules, everything you have just said seems known to me; but I remember that you made a distinction, that some consonances are perfe- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; the catchword "ctas" indicates the word is "perfectas" (perfect).</note>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the master Aloysius and the student Josephus. ### EXERCISE I. LESSON I.
->Concerning Note against Note.<-

<meta>catchword: Con-</meta>

...fect <note>original: "ctas"; the end of the word "perfectas" from the previous page.</note>, and some imperfect; it seems to me necessary to know whether, in using them, a certain distinction must be made?

*Aloys.* Have patience; I shall tell you everything. While there is indeed a great disparity between perfect and imperfect <term>Consonances</term> <note>original: "Consonantias"; the musical distance between two notes. "Perfect" intervals like the fifth or octave sound very stable, while "imperfect" intervals like thirds or sixths sound more colorful and melodic.</note>, which we shall discuss in another place, in this context—aside from the principle of motion and the fact that more imperfect ones should be used than perfect ones—their use is essentially equal, except for the beginning and the end, both of which must consist of a perfect consonance.

*Joseph.* Dear Master, would you mind explaining the reasons why more imperfect consonances than perfect ones should be used here, and why the beginning and end must consist of a perfect consonance?

*Aloys.* Your eagerness, which I nevertheless praise, makes it so that I must speak of certain things somewhat out of order. I will tell you, though not everything, so that your mind is not overwhelmed and confused by such a variety of things at the start. Know this: imperfect consonances are more <term>harmonious</term> <note>original: "harmonicas"; here implying a richer, more pleasing sound.</note> than perfect ones, for a reason I will explain later. Therefore, if a composition of this species—which has only two parts and is otherwise very simple—were filled only with perfect consonances, it would necessarily be thin and devoid of all harmony. Regarding the beginning and the end, consider this reason: the beginning indicates perfection, and the end indicates rest. Since imperfect consonances lack this sense of total perfection and cannot create a final conclusion, the beginning and the end must consist of perfect consonances. Finally, it should be noted that if the <term>Cantus firmus</term> <note>original: "Cantus firmus"; the pre-existing "fixed song" that serves as the basis for the exercise.</note> is in the lower part, the penultimate note should be a <term>major sixth</term>; if it is in the upper part, it should be a <term>minor third</term>.

*Joseph.* Are these all the requirements for this species of counterpoint?

*Aloys.* Not all, indeed, but enough to lay the foundations. The rest will become clear through the process of correction. Prepare yourself for the work, then: with the *Cantus firmus* laid down as your foundation, try to build a counterpoint in the <term>treble clef</term> <note>original: "clave Cantûs"; the soprano or high voice clef.</note> according to the rules I have given you so far.

*Joseph.* I will do what I can.

<summary>Aloysius explains the foundational rules for the first species of counterpoint, emphasizing the balance between stable perfect consonances and melodic imperfect consonances. He provides specific instructions for starting and ending a musical exercise correctly.</summary>
<keywords>counterpoint, perfect consonances, imperfect consonances, Cantus firmus, major sixth, minor third, music theory, musical harmony</keywords>

->Concerning Note against Note.<-

*Aloys.* You have hit the nail on the head <note>original: "Rem acu tetigisti"; a Latin idiom meaning to touch a matter with a needle, or to describe something with precise accuracy.</note>. I admire both your insight and your attention; but for what purpose did you place numbers both above the <term>Fixed Melody</term> <note>original: "Cantum" (referring to the Cantus firmus); the pre-existing melody used as the foundation for the exercise.</note> and above the Alto?

*Joseph.* By the numbers placed above the Alto, I intended to designate the <term>Consonances</term> <note>original: "Consonantias"; the musical distance or "intervals" between the two voices.</note> that I used, so that by having the movements from one consonance to another before my eyes, I might deviate less from the correct method of proceeding. However, those placed over the Fixed Melody—for example, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, etc.—indicate nothing other than the numbering of the notes, so that I might show you, venerable Master, that if I have succeeded, it happened not by chance, but by reason. You commanded that a beginning be made from a perfect consonance, which I have done by placing a Fifth. From the first note to the second—that is, from a Fifth to a Third, or from a perfect to an imperfect consonance—I proceeded by <term>oblique motion</term> <note>original: "motu obliquo"; when one voice stays on the same note while the other moves.</note>, although this could be done through all three types of motion. From the second note to the third—namely, from a Third to a Third, or from an imperfect to an imperfect consonance—I employed <term>direct motion</term> <note>original: "motum rectum"; when both voices move in the same direction.</note>, according to the rule which says: from imperfect to imperfect through all three motions. From the third note to the fourth, or from a Third (an imperfect consonance) to a Fifth (a perfect one), I moved by <term>contrary motion</term> <note>original: "motum contrarium"; when voices move in opposite directions, one up and one down.</note>, according to the rule saying: from imperfect to perfect by contrary motion. From the fourth note to the fifth, or from perfect to imperfect, I proceeded by direct motion, as the rule permits. From the fifth to the sixth note—that is, from imperfect to perfect—I employed contrary motion, as the rule commands. From the sixth note to the seventh, I proceeded by oblique motion, which is subject to no error. From the seventh to the eighth—that is, from imperfect to imperfect—I continued by direct motion, as the rule permits. From the eighth note to the ninth, as it is from imperfect to imperfect, any motion may be used. From the ninth to the tenth note, the same reasoning of correctness applies, where the tenth note (that is, the <term>penultimate</term> <note>original: "penultima"; the second to last note of the piece.</note> note), as you commanded, forms a major sixth, because the Fixed Melody in the lower...

# EXERCISE I. LESSON I.
->Concerning Note against Note.<-

If you had begun on **G (sol-re-ut)** <note>Solmization syllables used in the Guidonian hand system to identify specific pitches and their functions across different scales.</note>, it is clear that you would have started outside the mode; therefore, in the correction, I have placed an **Octave** in place of a **Fifth**, which is consistent with the <term>Fixed Melody</term> <note>original: "cantu firmo"; a pre-existing melody that serves as the foundation for a counterpoint exercise.</note> of that same mode.

*Joseph.* I am glad that this error—which I shall now keep in my memory—happened through my ignorance rather than my carelessness; but what is the reason for the second error marked at the second note?

*Aloys.* It is not an error of the second note with respect to the first, but of the second note with respect to the third. For you proceeded from a **Third** to a **Fifth** by **direct motion** <note>original: "motu recto"; when both musical parts move in the same direction, either both up or both down.</note>, which is against the rule stating: move from an imperfect to a perfect consonance by **contrary motion** <note>A movement where one voice goes up while the other goes down.</note>. This error is easily corrected by having the second note remain below on **D (la-sol-re)** through **oblique motion** <note>original: "motu obliquum"; where one voice stays on the same note while the other moves.</note>, thereby creating a **Tenth**. In that case, in moving from the second note to the third—namely from a Tenth to a Fifth, or from an imperfect to a perfect consonance—it happens that you proceed by contrary motion, just as the rule prescribes. I do not want you to be troubled by this small error; for it is hardly possible for a beginner to be so attentive as to fall into no error at all. *Experience is the master of all things.* Meanwhile, I bid you be content that you did the rest correctly; especially that you gave a **minor Third** to the penultimate note when the Fixed Melody was in the upper part, as I advised a little while ago.

*Joseph.* So that I may apply myself to this rule with greater attention hereafter and commit it more firmly to memory, would you please explain the reason why it is not permitted to move from an imperfect consonance to a perfect one by direct motion? For the matter does not seem of such great importance.

*Aloys.* I shall tell you. Because in that case, two **Fifths** follow one another consecutively, one of which is expressed or "open," while the other is "covered" or "hidden." I will now show in an example how this hidden one is made manifest through **diminution** <note>original: "diminutionem"; the process of breaking down long notes into shorter, faster notes to reveal the underlying melodic movement.</note>.

The numbers 5 and 5 are printed above specific notes in the fourth and fifth measures to highlight the consecutive fifths that are revealed when the motion is filled in.</image-desc>

*Joseph.* I completed this **Counterpoint** <note>original: "Contrapunctum"</note> with the greatest attention I could muster; nevertheless, I see two marks anew, indicating errors: namely, from the ninth note to the tenth, and from the tenth to the eleventh.

*Aloys.* You seem impatient to me; yet I am greatly pleased by this eagerness of yours not to stray from the rules. But how do you expect to avoid those small details about which you have not yet received instruction? From the ninth note to the tenth, you used a leap of a **Major Sixth** <note>original: "Sextæ majoris"</note>, which is forbidden in **Counterpoint**—a genre of composition in which everything should be very easy to sing <note>Leaps of a sixth were traditionally considered difficult for singers to pitch accurately compared to thirds, fourths, or fifths.</note>. Then, from the tenth note to the eleventh, you moved from a Tenth to an Octave with the parts converging—that is, with the lower part ascending and the upper part descending by step. This Octave is called the **Beaten Octave** <note>original: "Octava Battuta"</note> by the Italians and the **Downbeat** <note>original: "Thesis"</note> by the Greeks, because it occurs at the start of the measure; it is forbidden. In truth, though I have searched long and hard for the cause of this, I have been able to find neither a reason for the fault, nor a logical difference as to why the Octave in this 

example <image-desc>A musical snippet showing a 10th (E in the top voice, C in the bottom) moving to an 8ve (D in both voices) by contrary motion.</image-desc> should be approved,

while the following <image-desc>A musical snippet showing a 10th (B in the top voice, G in the bottom) moving to an 8ve (C in both voices) by similar motion.</image-desc> should be rejected?

Since the Octave in both examples is achieved through contrary motion. Another consideration must be made for the **Unison** <note>original: "Unisoni"; two voices singing the exact same pitch.</note>, produced in the same way,

namely from a Third into a Unison, for example: <image-desc>A musical snippet showing a 3rd (E and C) moving to a Unison (D and D) by contrary motion.</image-desc> in which 

case the Unison—since it consists of a **proportion of equality** <note>original: "proportione æqualitatis"; a mathematical way of saying the frequencies are 1:1, or identical</note>—is heard very little, and seems almost absorbed and lost. For this reason, in this species of Counterpoint, except at the begin- <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "prin-", leading into the next page's discussion of the beginning and end of a piece.</note>

*Joseph.* What is the meaning of that **NB.** <note>an abbreviation for the Latin 'nota bene', meaning 'note well' or 'pay attention'</note> placed on the first note of the **Counterpoint**?

*Aloys.* It indicates that it is otherwise *incorrect* to move from a **Unison** to another **Consonance** by a **leap** <note>original: "saltum"</note>, nor into a **Unison** in the same manner, as was said a little while ago. But since that lea-

### Exercise I. Lesson I. Concerning Note against Note.

...arises from the part of the **Fixed Song** <note>original: "Cantûs firmi"</note>, which cannot be changed, and therefore it must be tolerated in this place. The situation would be different if you were not bound by the obligation of the choral melody <note>original: "Cantûs choralis"</note> and had the free will to do as you please. But why did you add a **sharp** <note>original: "Dieſin"</note> to the eleventh note? This is not particularly common in the **diatonic** genus <note>The diatonic system refers to the standard natural scales; sharps were technically "chromatic" additions used sparingly to improve harmony.</note>.

**Joseph.** It seemed appropriate to place a **sixth** <note>original: "Sextam"</note> at that spot. I have heard, while learning the art of singing, that *fa* <note>The syllable used for the fourth degree of the scale, which often tends to resolve downward.</note> tends toward descent, while *mi* <note>The syllable used for the third or seventh degree, which often acts as a "leading tone" tending upward.</note> tends toward ascent. Since the progression moves from that sixth up to a **third** <note>original: "Tertiam"</note> in an ascending motion, I added the sharp so that the ascent would be made more authentic <note>Meaning more musically convincing or following the natural rules of leading tones.</note>. Furthermore, if that F on the eleventh note were placed without the sharp, it would create a somewhat unpleasant relation <term>cross-relation: a clash that occurs when a note in its natural form and its altered form (like F and F-sharp) appear very close to one another in different parts.</term> with the F-sharp on the thirteenth note.

**Aloysius.** You have made an excellent observation. Now I believe every stumbling block has been removed. Proceed then to the two remaining modes <note>original: "Tonos"; here referring to the keys or scales of A and C.</note>, A and C.

<image-desc>A musical example in two staves. The top staff is labeled "Counterpoint" <note>original: "Contrapunctum"</note> with a C-clef on the third line (Alto clef). The bottom staff is labeled "Fixed Song" <note>original: "Cantus firmus"</note> with an F-clef on the fourth line (Bass clef). Both use diamond-shaped notes (semibreves) in common time. Between the staves, numerical intervals are written: 8, 3, 6, 3, 3, 6, 3, 5, 6, 6, 6 (with a sharp), 8. The eleventh note of the counterpoint has a sharp sign placed before it.</image-desc>

<image-desc>A second musical example in two staves. The top staff is labeled "Fixed Song" with a C-clef on the first line (Soprano clef). The bottom staff is labeled "Counterpoint" with a C-clef on the fourth line (Tenor clef). Both use diamond-shaped notes in common time. Between the staves, numerical intervals are written: 1, 3, 3, 6, 6, 8, 10, 10, 5, 3, 3, 1. The eleventh note of the counterpoint has a sharp sign placed before it.</image-desc>

From these last two examples, it is clear that you now know everything necessary for this **species** <note>First Species Counterpoint: the basic technique of writing one note in the new part for every one note in the original melody.</note>. Now we must follow with the second species.

# EXERCISE I.
## SECOND LESSON,
### Or the Second Species of Counterpoint.

Before I begin to explain this second species of counterpoint, it must be known beforehand that for the present we are working in duple time <note>original: "Tempore binario"</note>, the beat or measure of which consists of two equal parts, one of which consists of the downward motion and the other of the upward motion of the hand. The downward motion of the hand is called *Thesis* in Greek <note>Greek: θέσις</note>, while the upward motion is called *Arsis* <note>Greek: ἄρσις</note>; I have decided that we should use these two words from now on in this exercise for greater convenience. This second species occurs when two minims <term>minim: a half note, worth half the duration of a semibreve</term> are placed against one semibreve <term>semibreve: a whole note, the basic unit of the fixed melody</term>. Of these two notes, the one falling on the *Thesis* [the downbeat] must always be a consonance <term>consonance: an interval like a third, fifth, or octave that sounds stable</term>; the other on the *Arsis* [the upbeat], however, may even be a dissonance <term>dissonance: a clashing interval like a second or seventh</term>, provided it moves by step from the preceding note to the following one. But if it moves by a leap, it must necessarily be a consonance. In this species of counterpoint, a dissonance cannot occur in any other way than through diminution <term>diminution: the process of "diminishing" or breaking a long note into smaller notes to fill the melodic gap between two points</term>, that is, by filling that intermediate space which lies between two notes separated by the leap of a third. For example:

<image-desc>A musical notation example on a single staff using a soprano clef. It consists of four bars. The first two bars show first-species counterpoint with semibreves in two voices. The third and fourth bars demonstrate "Diminutio" (diminution) where a leap of a third in the upper voice is filled with passing minims. 
Bar 1: Semibreves C (lower) and E (upper).
Bar 2: Semibreves D (lower) and F (upper).
Bar 3: Semibreve C (lower), upper voice has semibreve E followed by minims F and G. Text "Diminution" is written below.
Bar 4: Semibreve D (lower), upper voice has semibreve F followed by minims E and D. Text "Diminut." is written below.</image-desc>

One does not need to consider whether that diminished note is a consonance or a dissonance; it is enough that it fills the empty space left between two notes separated by the leap of a third.

*Joseph.* Are those rules regarding motion and progression that were taught for the first species of counterpoint also to be observed here?

*Aloys.* Most certainly; except for the next-to-last measure <note>original: "penultimum tactum"</note>, in which the first note should have a fifth and the second note a major sixth, if the fixed song <note>original: "Cantus firmus"</note>, or choral melody, is in the lower part. But if the fixed song is in the upper part, the fir-

<keywords>Second Species, counterpoint, Thesis, Arsis, diminution, consonance, dissonance, minim, semibreve, duple time</keywords>

*Joseph.* My fear of making mistakes was not in vain; for I see two marks of correction <note>original: "reprehensionis signa"</note>: the first in the first note of the ninth measure, the second in the first note of the tenth measure, neither of which I understand. For in both places, I moved from one imperfect consonance <term>imperfect consonance: intervals like thirds and sixths, which are harmonious but less stable than "perfect" octaves or fifths</term> to another by contrary motion <term>contrary motion: when two musical parts move in opposite directions to avoid prohibited parallel intervals</term>.

*Aloys.* You reason correctly, for there are two errors, and the reason for both is one and the same—which I am not surprised you are ignorant of, as you have not yet been taught it. It must therefore be noted that a leap of a Third can save neither two Fifths nor two Octaves; because that intermediate note occurring on the upbeat <note>original: "Arhsi," referring to the Arsis or the second half of the measure</note> is regarded as if it were not there at all. This is because, due to the smallness of its value <note>i.e., the short duration of the half-note</note> and the smallness of the interval, it is unable to temper the sound enough to prevent the effect of two Fifths,

A different reasoning must be applied to a leap consisting of a larger interval <note>original: "majori spatio"</note>. For example: Fourths, Fifths, Sixths. In these cases, the distance from the first note to the second seems to bring about a kind of forgetfulness in the ears of the sound of the first note on the downbeat <term>downbeat: or "Thesis," the accented first half of the measure</term>, in relation to the second note existing in the same way on the following downbeat. ...mindful not only of the beat <term>tactus: the steady pulse or measure of the music</term> in which you work, but also of those that follow.

*Joseph.* Indeed it is so, venerable Master. For in composing the previous exercises of counterpoint, I would hardly have known what to do unless, before I decided to write, I had looked ahead by considering one or two beats to see what would fit there.

*Aloysius.* I am wonderfully glad to see you so perceptive, and I urge you again and again that you strive to overcome the weight of the difficulty with both attention and constant study. Do not allow yourself to be crushed by the harshness of the burden, nor let yourself be drawn away from diligent study by flatteries—as if you already knew much. By doing so, you will feel light gradually rising in the darkness, and the veil of gloom <note>original: "caliginis velum"</note> will be partially lifted from you, and you will rejoice. Here we should also speak of Triple Time <note>original: "Tempore Ternario"</note>, where three notes are placed against one. However, because it is of little importance due to the ease of the matter, I do not think it worth the effort to establish a special lesson for that cause. It will soon be found that a few examples are enough for the understanding of this matter.

<image-desc>A musical notation example on two staves in 3/2 time signature (Triple Time). The upper staff uses a C-clef and shows notes in triple rhythm (a dotted minim followed by crotchets, then minims). The lower staff uses an F-clef and shows sustained semibreve notes.</image-desc>

Where the middle note—because all three move by step <note>original: "gradatim"</note>—can be a dissonance; it would be otherwise if one or another of these three were to leap, in which case it is established from what has already been said that all three must be consonants.

# EXERCISE I.
## LESSON THREE,
### On the Third Species of Counterpoint.

The Third Species of Counterpoint is the composition of four crotchets <term>semiminimae: literally "half-minims," known today as quarter notes</term> against one semibreve <term>semibrevis: a whole note</term>. Where, first of all, it must be noted that if it happens that five crotchets follow one another continuously by step, either ascending or descending,

<sig>Q 2</sig>

# Exercise I. Lesson III. Concerning the Third Species of Counterpoint.

...it happens that the first note must be a <term>consonance: an interval such as a third, fifth, or octave that sounds stable</term>, while the second may be a <term>dissonance: a clashing interval like a second or seventh</term>. It is necessary that the third note becomes a consonance again. The fourth may be a dissonance, provided the fifth note <note>meaning the first note of the following measure</note> is a consonance; as in these examples:

<image-desc>Two lines of musical notation using diamond-shaped notes. The top line displays four individual measures, each showing a different four-note melodic pattern (minims) against a single long note (semibreve). The bottom line shows a continuous staff illustrating these principles in practice.</image-desc>

However, this rule first fails in a specific *Hypothesis* <note>a particular musical case or scenario</note> where the second and fourth notes happen to be consonances, in which case the third note may be a dissonance; as in the following examples:

<image-desc>A musical score for two voices. The upper voice consists of six groups of four minims, each separated by double barlines. Below each group are figured bass numbers indicating the intervals: 6 5 4 3, 5 6 7 8, 3 5 4 3, 8 6 7 8, 3 1 2 3, and 3 5 4 3. The lower voice holds a single long semibreve under each group.</image-desc>

In these, the third note of all these *Models* <note>original: Paradigmatum</note> is a dissonance <note>The OCR reads "Consonans" (consonant), but the context and the music—where the third notes are marked 4, 7, 4, 7, 2, and 4—confirm these are dissonances.</note>, and it is called a "filling in of a leap of a third" <note>original: saltûs tertiæ</note>. To make the truth of this matter clear, these examples must be reduced to their basic form <note>original: ad substantiam revocanda</note> in this way:

<image-desc>A musical score for two voices showing three short examples that simplify the previous melodic figures into a basic harmonic structure, showing how the moving notes relate to the underlying chord.</image-desc>

From these, it is clearly seen that this third note—namely, the dissonance—is nothing other than a <term>diminution: the process of dividing a long note into shorter ones to create melody</term> of a leap of a third, by which the intermediate space found between the second and fourth notes is filled; this space never refuses a diminution or being filled by an intermediate note.

The second *Hypothesis* is one where the common rule is set aside: the "changed note," called *Cambiata* by the Italians, where from a dissonant second note...

*Joseph.* It seemed to me that otherwise a harsh relation would arise from *mi* against *fa* <note>original: "mi contra fa"; the tritone or "augmented fourth," a dissonant interval that was strictly avoided in classical counterpoint</note>. *Aloysius.* You have observed most excellently; for the same reason, even sharps <note>original: "Diesis"</note> must sometimes be employed: but where and when they are to be used must be weighed with keen judgment. # EXERCISE I.
## LESSON FOUR,
### Regarding the Fourth Species of Counterpoint.

This Species consists of two minims <note>half notes</note> placed against a semibreve <note>whole note</note> in one and the same place, and joined by a curved line <note>a tie</note>; it is necessary that the first note falls on the Arsis <term>the upbeat or weak part of the measure</term> and the second falls on the Thesis <term>the downbeat or strong part of the measure</term>. This Species is customarily called *Ligature* <note>a note tied across a barline or beat</note>, or *Syncopation*; and it is of two types: Consonant and Dissonant.

A Ligature of *Consonance* is one where both minims—both on the Arsis and the Thesis—are a Consonant interval. The matter will become clear through examples.

->*Ligatures of Consonance.*<-

<image-desc>Musical notation featuring two staves. The top staff, using an alto clef, shows a series of minims (half notes) with ties across the barlines, representing ligatures. The bottom staff, using a bass clef, consists of semibreves (whole notes). Numeric interval markers are printed between the staves: 8, 5, 3, 5, 8, 5, 3, 5, 8.</image-desc>

A *Ligature of Dissonance* is one where the first note—namely on the Arsis—is indeed Consonant (which must always be the case), but the second note on the Thesis is dissonant, as may be seen in the following example.

<sig>S</sig>

### Dissonant Ligatures.

<note>The musical example shows two staves. The upper staff, using a C-clef on the first line, contains a series of tied half notes (syncopation) forming dissonant intervals against the lower staff. The intervals marked are a 10th (consonant start), followed by a series of 7-6 suspensions (the 7th is the dissonance, the 6th is the resolution), ending on an octave. The lower staff, in an F-clef, descends in whole notes.</note>

Furthermore, in this species, Dissonances do not appear accidentally or through diminution <term>melodic decoration using shorter note values</term>—as they did in the previous species—but appear substantially and on the Thesis <term>the strong beat or downbeat</term>. Because dissonances are by their nature harsh to the ears, they cannot offer anything pleasing on their own. Instead, it is necessary for them to borrow their sweetness from the Consonance that immediately follows, into which they are resolved. Therefore, we must now speak

->Regarding<-
### The Resolution of Dissonances.

Before I begin to describe the manner in which Dissonances are to be resolved, it is helpful to know that tied notes <note>original: "Notas ligatas"</note>, as if bound by chains, are nothing other than a retardation <note>a delay of a note's arrival</note> of the note that follows; these notes then seem to emerge into freedom, as if released from slavery. For this reason, dissonances must always be resolved by moving downward by step into the nearest Consonance, as is clearly seen in the following example:

<note>A musical example with two staves. The upper staff shows tied notes (ligatures) resolving downward by step. The numbers below the staff indicate the intervals: a 3rd (consonance), followed by three 4-3 suspensions (where the 4th is the dissonance and the 3rd is the resolution). The lower staff provides a steady bass in whole notes.</note>

->This example, with the retardation removed, looks like this:<-

<note>A musical example showing the same harmonic progression as above, but without the ties or delays. This reveals the underlying structure: a simple series of 3rd intervals (3, 3, 3, 3), showing that the "dissonance" is merely a delayed version of a consonant harmony.</note>

Example...

<summary>This page explains the theory of "ligatures" (suspensions), where a dissonance on a strong beat is treated as a "retardation" that must resolve downward by step to a consonant interval.</summary>
<keywords>Fourth Species, counterpoint, dissonance, resolution, ligatures, syncopation, retardation, Thesis, music theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Lesson IV on the Fourth Species of counterpoint (syncopation). Having established that dissonances must resolve downward, the text now explains the underlying harmonic logic: a tied note is simply a delay of a note that would otherwise appear on the beat. Therefore, the rules governing consonant progressions still apply behind the scenes.</meta>

Page 71
# Exercise I. Lesson IV. regarding the fourth Species of Counterpoint.

From this it is gathered that it will be an easy thing to know into which Consonance <term>a stable, harmonious interval</term> any dissonance must be resolved: namely, into that one which is found immediately on the Thesis <term>the strong beat or downbeat</term> of the following measure once the retardation <note>the delay of a note's arrival caused by the tie</note> is removed. For this reason, when the Canto Fermo <term>the fixed, pre-existing melody</term> is in the lower part, the Second must be resolved into a Unison, the Fourth into a Third, the Seventh into a Sixth, and the Ninth into an Octave. This reasoning is so grounded in truth that, for this very reason, one is not permitted to progress from a Unison to a Second, nor from an Octave to a Ninth by means of a Ligature <term>a tied note</term>; as can be seen in the following examples.

->*incorrect.* &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; *incorrect.*<-
<note>original: "male"</note>

Both use tied half notes in the upper voice against whole notes in the lower voice.</image-desc>

Once this retardation <note>the suspension</note> is removed, you will see the immediate succession of two Unisons in the first example, and of two Octaves in the second, for this reason:

<image-desc>Two musical examples showing the simplified harmonic structure of the previous examples without the suspensions. This reveals direct parallel unisons in the first case and parallel octaves in the second, explaining why the previous examples were considered incorrect. In traditional counterpoint, "parallel perfect intervals" like these are forbidden because they reduce the independence of the voices.</image-desc>

On the contrary, the progression is correct if it moves from a Third into a Second, or from a Tenth into a Ninth, in this manner:

->*correct.* &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; *correct.*<-

The second example shows a correct 10-9-8 suspension-resolution pattern, with the numbers "8 9 8" written below the staff. Because the underlying structure (3 to 1 or 10 to 8) does not involve parallel unisons or octaves, these are allowed.</image-desc>

These hypotheses are correctly established, because they would still be valid even if the retardation <term>the delay of a note's arrival</term> or the ligature <term>a tied note</term> were removed, as shown in the following way:

<image-desc>Two musical staves showing examples of two-part counterpoint in C-clefs. The top system demonstrates a series of tied notes (suspensions) in the upper voice resolving downwards. After it has been shown what kinds of dissonances <term>clashing intervals</term> can be used and in what manner they ought to be resolved if the Canto Fermo <term>the fixed, pre-existing melody</term> is in the lower part, it now remains to be said which dissonances have a place, and by what reasoning they should be resolved, when the Canto Fermo is in the upper part.

I say, therefore, that in this case the Second resolved into a Third; the Fourth resolved into a Fifth; and the Ninth resolved into a Tenth can be used. For example:

<image-desc>Two musical staves in two-part counterpoint. The upper staff contains the Cantus Firmus in whole notes. The lower staff contains counterpoint with tied notes. Three segments are marked with slurs and intervals: a resolution from a second to a third (labeled 1 2 3), a fourth to a fifth (labeled 3 4 5), and a ninth to a tenth (labeled 8 9 10).</image-desc>

*Joseph.* Why do you omit the Seventh in this case? Can it therefore not be used when the Canto Fermo is in the upper part? Please do not be annoyed <note>original: "non molestè feras," a polite request for an explanation</note> to tell me the reason.

*Aloysius.* I confess that the Seventh was intentionally omitted by me in this case. Indeed, hardly any other reason can be given than the heavy authority of the Classical Authors <note>This refers to the master composers of the "old style," such as Palestrina</note>, to whom much must be granted in practice; hardly any of them are found who used the Seventh in this manner, resolving into an Octave.

<image-desc>Two musical staves. The top staff has the Cantus Firmus. The bottom staff shows a counterpoint line where a seventh resolves to an octave, labeled with the numbers 6 7 8 under a slur.</image-desc>

Page 73
# Exercise I. Lesson IV. regarding the fourth Species of Counterpoint.

One might perhaps say that a Seventh <term>an interval of seven notes</term> resolved in this way is invalid because it resolves into a perfect consonance—namely, the Octave—from which it can derive very little harmonic variety. This would be true if we did not frequently find, among the same masters, the Second (which is an inverted Seventh) resolved into a Unison; from the Unison, as the most perfect of consonances, a dissonance <term>a clashing sound</term> can share even less harmony. In this matter, I believe we should consult the practice introduced by the great masters. Let us see an example of the Seventh inverted into a Second and resolved into a Unison:

-><image-desc>A musical score fragment featuring two staves in soprano (C) clef. The upper voice performs a sequence of three whole notes: C, D, and C, with a slur connecting the first two. The numbers 8, 2, and 1 are printed above these notes respectively. The lower voice consists of three whole notes on C. The word "well" <note>original: "bene"</note> is printed below the lower staff.</image-desc><-

*Joseph.* Before I proceed further with the lesson, may I ask, with your permission, whether a retardation <term>the delay of a note's arrival</term> or ligature <term>a tied note</term> in dissonance also has a place when ascending? For the following examples seem to follow a similar logic:

<image-desc>A musical score fragment with two staves in soprano (C) clef. The upper staff shows a series of ascending tied half notes (ligatures). The lower staff shows a series of descending whole notes. Between the staves, the intervals are marked as 3, 2, 3, 2, 3.</image-desc>

*Aloys.* You raise a question more difficult to untie than a Gordian knot <note>A metaphor for a complex or unsolvable problem; Aloysius suggests Joseph is not yet ready for this advanced theoretical debate</note>, which you, still lingering at the threshold of this discipline, cannot yet grasp; therefore, it must be resolved in another place. Although, if you were to remove the retardation, there seems to be a similar logic of Thirds in both the ascent and the descent, some difference nevertheless lies beneath, which will be explained in its proper place, as I said. Meanwhile, by my authority as Teacher <note>original: "jure Magistri"</note>, I tell you that all dissonances must be resolved by descending into the nearest consonance. Furthermore, the penultimate <term>second to last</term> measure of this Species must be concluded with a Seventh resolving into a Sixth, if the Canto Fermo is in the lower part; but if the Canto Fermo is in the upper—

<sig>T</sig>

# EXERCISE II.
## FIRST LESSON.
### *Regarding Note Against Note in Three-Part Writing.*

That Three-Part Composition <note>original: "Tricinii"</note> is the most perfect of all is evident from this: that in it, without the help of another part, a perfect <term>harmonic triad</term> <note>original: "Trias harmonica"</note> can be had, whereas the addition of a third <gloss>fourth</gloss> or more parts is almost nothing else than a repetition of some part already existing in the harmonic triad. From this, it has passed into a proverb, as it were, that for someone who has the power to correctly execute three-part writing, the way to the composition of many parts lies open as wide as can be.

*Joseph.* My soul burns with an incredible desire for knowing by what method this composition is to be crafted; but I fear in no small measure that the weight of the subject, overwhelming my own insignificance, might cause my wish to falter.

*Aloysius.* There is no reason for you to fear; for since you did not struggle greatly in crafting the Species of two-part writing <note>original: "Bicinii"</note>, rest on hope. Once you have understood those things I am about to tell you, and having taken the start from the simplest Species—namely, Note Against Note—and proceeding in that same order we observed in two-part writing, the labor of crafting three-part writing will not be so difficult for you.

This Species of three parts is therefore the simplest com-

### # EXERCISE II.

### Regarding Note against Note in Three-Part Writing.

...composition <note>original: "positio"</note> consisting of equal notes, specifically three <term>whole notes</term> <note>original: "semibrevibus"</note>, and composed of nothing but <term>consonances</term> <note>intervals that sound stable and pleasing, such as thirds, fifths, and octaves</note>.

In this, it must first be noted that in every underlying structure or measure <note>original: "Hypothesi, sive tactu"</note>, unless some other reason prevents it, the <term>harmonic triad</term> should be employed.

*Joseph:* What is the <term>harmonic triad</term> <note>original: "Trias harmonica"</note>?
*Aloys:* It is a system consisting of a third and a fifth. For example:

<image-desc>A musical notation example on three staves. Each staff contains a single semibreve (whole note) in one measure, together forming a C major triad in root position: G on the top staff (C-clef on the first line), E on the middle staff (C-clef on the fourth line), and C on the bottom staff (F-clef on the fourth line).</image-desc>

*Aloys:* Occasionally it is for the sake of a more melodious line <note>original: "concinnioris Cantûs"</note>, for which reason another consonance—either a sixth or an octave—is usually used instead of the triad. More often, the necessity of avoiding the immediate succession of two perfect consonances <note>such as two consecutive fifths or octaves, which were forbidden in classical counterpoint</note> requires that, leaving the triad behind, one must use either a sixth in place of a fifth, or an octave, or even both; as I shall show in the following example:

### # EXERCISE II.
## LESSON I.
### Regarding Note against Note in Three-Part Writing <note>original: "Tricinio"</note>.

<image-desc>Three musical staves arranged vertically. The top staff contains a single diamond-shaped note with the number 5 written below it. The middle staff contains a single diamond-shaped note with the number 3 written below it. The bottom staff (using a bass clef) contains a single diamond-shaped note.</image-desc>

That note C, then, when placed in its proper position, forms a **harmonic triad** <note>original: "Triadem harmonicam"; the basic three-note chord consisting of a root, third, and fifth</note>. If this note is shifted up an **octave** into the higher register <note>original: "translatum per Octavam in acutum"</note> while the other parts remain as they were, it must necessarily result in a **sixth**. However, this is only to be understood as the rule when that note *mi* <note>The third syllable in the hexachord system, often used for the leading tone or the third degree of a scale</note> is followed by *fa*,
->as in this example:<-

<image-desc>A single musical staff with two diamond-shaped notes, representing the interval of a second (mi and fa).</image-desc>

If, however, that *mi* happens to leap to another note, it calls for a **fifth** rather than a **sixth**,
->as in the following examples:<-

<image-desc>Three musical staves showing four vertical chords grouped into two pairs by a vertical bar line. This is because, in my version, the note A appears in the **Tenor** part only once, whereas in your example it is found twice,
->as can be seen here:<-

<image-desc>Two musical staves showing a sequence of notes. The top staff has five notes followed by a double bar line and then four notes. The bottom staff follows a similar pattern.</image-desc>

### # EXERCISE II.
## LESSON I.
### On Note against Note in Three-Part Writing <note>original: "Tricinio"</note>.

---

I want it told to you, for now and always, that great consideration must be given to this variety.

*Joseph.* Please do not take it ill if I ask why, in the example <note>original: "Paradigmate"</note> you brought forward a moment ago, you began with the parts separated so far from each other? For it seems to me that it could have been done in another way as well.

*Aloysius.* Your curiosity, full of promise, brings me the least bit of trouble and the greatest amount of delight. Did you not see that the **Bass** in that example moves by ascending continuously step by step? For this reason, so that the parts have space to move toward each other by **contrary motion** <note>original: "motu contrario"; when voices move in opposite directions to avoid forbidden parallel intervals or awkward spacing</note>, it is necessary to begin with the parts far removed from the fundamental part <note>The bass is considered the "fundamental" part because it provides the harmonic foundation</note>.

But by what examples do you demonstrate that the model could have been done in another way?

*Joseph.* I will provide two examples, not for the sake of demonstrating my point, but so that I may be taught.

<image-desc>Musical notation for two exercises in three-voice counterpoint (tricinio). The notation is spread across three staves per exercise, utilizing soprano, alto, and bass clefs. Numerical interval indicators (such as 10, 8, 6, 5, 3) are printed between the staves to show the harmonic relationships between the parts.</image-desc>

*Aloysius.* I do not entirely disapprove of these examples of yours. But you see, in the first example, from the first beat <note>original: "tactu"; the primary unit of time or "stroke" in early music conducting</note> to the second, all three parts move by ascending—partly by step and partly by leap—which can hardly happen without some awkwardness <note>In counterpoint, having all voices move in the same direction—especially by leap—is often discouraged as it can weaken the independence of the voices</note>. This occurs here in the **Tenor** part in relation to the **Alto** from the first to the second beat, which are arranged like this:

<image-desc>A short musical snippet showing two measures of counterpoint for two voices, likely the Alto and Tenor parts mentioned in the text, illustrating a specific motion between the first and second tactus.</image-desc>

<meta>catchword: Ex</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the spatial relationship between voices in three-part counterpoint, specifically why a teacher might choose widely separated starting pitches to allow room for contrary motion. It introduces the concept of the "tactus" (beat) and the potential "awkwardness" of all voices moving in the same direction simultaneously.</summary>
<keywords>three-part counterpoint, tricinio, contrary motion, motu contrario, voice spacing, bass, tenor, alto, musical pedagogy, tactus</keywords>
