### # EXERCISE II.
## LESSON I.
### On Note against Note in Three-Part Writing <note>original: "Tricinio"</note>.

---

From this it is clear that, once the fundamental part is removed, that progression is faulty, because not only is the movement made from an imperfect interval to a perfect one, so to speak, but—what is worse—that fifth is not a consonant fifth, but a **false fifth** <note>original: "Quinta falsa"; a diminished fifth, which was considered dissonant and forbidden in this style of counterpoint</note>. For the logic of the rules must be observed not only in respect to the **Bass**, but also between the other parts among themselves whenever possible; although in a composition of many parts, it is not to be observed so rigorously. Thus, even in three-part writing, for weighty reasons regarding the parts in relation to the **Bass**, it is sometimes permitted to withdraw from that rigor, which can be seen in the penultimate measure <note>original: "tactu"; literally 'touch' or stroke, referring here to the musical bar</note> of the previous example. There, a progression occurs by **direct motion** <note>original: "motu recto"; when two or more voices move in the same direction</note> from a fifth to a final octave—that is, from perfect to perfect—because it cannot be done otherwise. For example:

<image-desc>A musical example in three parts across three staves. The top staff (soprano) moves from G to high C; the middle staff (tenor) moves from E to G; the bottom staff (bass) moves from C to C. This represents a standard cadential progression where the voices move toward a final resolution.</image-desc>

*Joseph.* Could that awkwardness not be avoided by placing a **tenth** in the place of the **octave**?

*Aloysius.* It could indeed, but it seems that by using that imperfect interval <note>A tenth is a compound third, categorized as an imperfect consonance</note>, the sense of perfection and rest required for the final note is not sufficiently established. The reasoning is different for **four-part writing** <note>original: "Quadricinii"</note>, where with the addition of the fifth, those requirements seem to be satisfied despite the presence of the third.

*Joseph.* But what do you find worthy of reproach in my second example?

*Aloysius.* Nothing other than that **sixths**, when ascending on the **downbeat** <note>original: "Thesi"; from the Greek 'thesis', the strong beat of a measure where the foot was traditionally lowered</note>, have a certain harshness. Sixths occurring on the **upbeat** <note>original: "Arhsi"; from the Greek 'arsis', the weak beat where the foot was raised</note>—which have no place in this species—are more tolerable as they have less strength, as will be discussed more extensively elsewhere.

Now let us proceed to the exercise. So that the creation of this species <note>First species counterpoint: note against note</note> may be easier for you by following a certain formula, I will compose the first example for you, which, according to the nature of three parts, will also be threefold: first, with the **fixed melody** <note>original: "Cantus firmus"</note> in the upper part; second, in the middle part; third, in the foun- <meta>catchword: damen- (fundamental)</meta>

<summary>Aloysius explains the necessity of specific intervals for a sense of musical "rest" at a cadence and critiques the use of sixths on strong beats. He then prepares to demonstrate how to place a fixed melody (cantus firmus) in each of the three voices.</summary>

<keywords>three-part counterpoint, tricinio, direct motion, false fifth, thesis, arsis, cantus firmus, musical cadence, Joseph, Aloysius</keywords>

*Joseph.* It seems, however, that from the seventh to the eighth measure in the **Alto** part with respect to the **Bass**, you did not observe the rule which states: [move] from an imperfect interval to a perfect one by **contrary motion** <note>original: "motum contrarium"; when voices move in opposite directions to avoid forbidden parallel intervals</note>. You, however, proceeded by **direct motion** <note>original: "rectum"</note>.

*Aloys.* You observe correctly indeed; but you must remember that I said a little while ago that to avoid a greater inconvenience in a **three-part composition** <note>original: "Tricinio"</note>, where it could not be done otherwise, it is sometimes permissible to depart from that rigor.

*Joseph.* I remember indeed; but it seems to me that even while observing that rule, it could have been done correctly if the progression had been established from F in the **Bass** to the high C <note>original: "C. acutum"</note> by contrary motion, in this manner:

<term>Tricinium</term>: A composition for three voices.
<term>Cantus firmus</term>: A "fixed song" or melody that serves as the foundation for counterpoint.
<term>Trias harmonica</term>: The harmonic triad, the basic three-note chord of Western music.
<term>Motus contrarius</term>: Contrary motion; when one voice goes up and the other goes down.
<term>Motus rectus</term>: Direct (or parallel) motion; when voices move in the same direction.
<term>Bassus</term>: The Bass, or the lowest voice-part.
<term>Altus</term>: The Alto, or the middle voice-part in this context.
<term>Tactus</term>: The measure or beat of the music.

### # EXERCISE II. LESSON I.
## On Note against Note in Three-Part Writing <note>original: "de Nota contra Notam in Tricinio"</note>.
# 88

*Aloys.* That inconvenience would indeed be avoided by **contrary motion** <note>original: "motum contrarium"; voices moving in opposite directions</note>; but do you not see that by such a method two similar transgressions would immediately follow: from the ninth note into the tenth, and from the tenth into the last? Furthermore, in the ninth measure <note>original: "tactu"; literally a touch or stroke, referring here to the musical bar</note>, the **Tenor** part with the **Bass**, as you see, would create a **unison**, which contributes less to the harmony than an **Octave**—besides the fact that in this style of composition, one is not permitted to exceed the limits of the five staff lines without grave necessity.

*Joseph.* I am entirely convinced by the reasons you have brought forward, but perhaps it could be done in this way?

<image-desc>A musical score fragment in five measures for three voices. The top staff uses a soprano C-clef, the middle an alto C-clef, and the bottom a bass F-clef. The notes are diamond-shaped (rhomboid). The fourth measure of the middle staff contains a sharp accidental.</image-desc>

*Aloys.* Even less so. Do you not remember that a leap of a **major sixth** is forbidden? What then should be judged of a leap of a **seventh**? We must always consider the natural ease of the singing voice. Now, I shall provide an example with the **fixed melody** <note>original: "Cantu firmo"</note> placed in the middle part.

<image-desc>A three-part musical composition in eleven measures, with the middle staff labeled "Cantus firmus." Measures are numbered 1 through 11 above the top staff. *Aloys.* It is so; it could have been done that way. But looking a little more closely, you will find that the **Octave** serves the logic of the **Melody** <note>original: "Cantûs rationi"</note> better; to which, as I have often said and will have to say even more often, much weight must be given. <margin>Fixed melody <note>original: "Cantus firmus"</note></margin>
<image-desc>A musical score for three voices in note-against-note counterpoint. The composition is written on three staves using C and F clefs. The bottom staff is labeled in the margin as the "Cantus firmus." Numbers representing intervals are placed between the staves. *Aloys.* You judge correctly indeed. But what kind of **Third** do you think should have been placed: **major**, or **minor**? If minor, do you not feel that it is not strong enough to form a conclusion? If major, it is equally unsuitable: for since this **mode** <note>original: "tonus"</note> maintains its consistency with a minor third—specifically F without a **sharp** <note>original: "Diesi"</note>—the ears, having been accustomed to that melodic movement through the entire course of the fixed melody, would perceive a certain offense from hearing a sharp at the end. Therefore, it will be more prudent to omit the Third entirely.

Now proceed to compose examples for the remaining five surviving **modes** <note>original: "tonorum"; referring to the ecclesiastical modes like Dorian or Phrygian</note> in such a way that the fixed melody is placed in each of the three parts, as I have already said and done; observing everything that has been taught so far.

### # EXERCISE II. LESSON I.
## On Note-against-Note in Three-Part Writing <note>original: "de Nota contra Notam in Tricinio"; a tricinium is a composition for three voices</note>.
# 90

**Joseph.** I have labored not a little in crafting the **final cadence** <note>original: "clausula finali"</note>. For since, in the **premise** <note>original: "Hypothesi"</note> of the penultimate **measure** <note>original: "tactûs"</note> of a formal cadence—for example:

<image-desc>A small musical example containing three staves. The top and middle staves each show a sequence of two notes: a diamond-shaped note followed by a square-shaped note. The bottom staff contains a single diamond-shaped note.</image-desc>

—there is no place for it, nor does this **mode** <note>original: "tonus"</note> by its natural logic have a **perfect fifth** in that position, nor does it allow for a **sharp** <note>original: "Diesin"; here referring to an accidental to raise a note</note> by whose benefit a formal cadence could be formed, it therefore seemed to me that the end could not be concluded in any other way than how I have done it. However, I remain somewhat doubtful because of the **major third** placed in the **final note**. For I remember you saying above that in similar modes, the **third** should be omitted entirely, and a **fifth** should be placed in its stead.

**Aloys.** You have used very wise judgment regarding the **final cadence**; namely, that because of the peculiar location of the **semitone**, there is no place for a formal cadence there, and therefore a unique cadence had to be formed. Do not let the **major third** placed in the final note cause you any **doubt** <note>original: "scrupulum"</note>; for what I said about omitting the third is to be understood as applying where it is possible to do so. You have therefore judged correctly that in the final note of that example, a **fifth** could not have been placed without an immediate **succession** <note>original: "consecutione"</note> of two fifths; therefore, a **major third** had to be used in its place, because a **minor third** is less suited for concluding the end due to its greater degree of imperfection. Proceed.

melody <note>original: "Cantus firmus"</note>
</margin>

## EXERCISE II. LESSON I.
### On Note-against-Note in Three-Part Writing <note>original: "de Nota contra Notam in Tricinio"</note>.

<image-desc>Three musical staves displaying a three-part counterpoint exercise in mensural notation (diamond-shaped notes). The top staff has a C-clef on the third line. *Joseph.* Here are the remaining examples of the musical modes; in my judgment, they are done well enough, at least as far as the restriction of the <term>Fixed Melody</term> <note>original: "Cantus firmus"; a pre-existing melody used as the foundation for counterpoint</note> allows. Truly, I believe that if the necessity of the fixed melody had not restricted the freedom of composing, the composition could have been made richer with <term>Harmonic Triads</term> <note>original: "Triade harmonicâ"; a chord consisting of a root, third, and fifth</note> in many places.

*Aloys.* You judge correctly. Thus, in due time, when you are in <term>Free Composition</term> <note>original: "Compositione libera"; composing without the pedagogical constraint of a pre-existing melody</note>, you will not be burdened by the restriction of any obligation to a fixed melody. But it is incredible how much benefit this exercise—namely, building upon a fixed melody—brings to a student of this discipline. By this alone, being instructed by both practice and reason, can one be led to a true knowledge of this art. I recommend this exercise to you most highly, again and again (for it must be emphasized often).

Having completed the examples of the first species, we must now proceed to the second species.

# EXERCISE II.

## LESSON SECOND,

### On the Placement of the Half Note against the Whole Note in Three-Part Writing.

As a model, I present three examples to you, so that the completion of the rest may come more easily to you.

## EXERCISE II. LESSON II.
### On the Placement of the Half Note <note>original: "Minimæ"</note>, etc.

<image-desc>A musical score featuring three staves in C-clefs, representing a three-part counterpoint exercise (Tricinium) in the second species (two notes against one). 
- The top staff (Soprano clef) contains a counterpoint in half notes (minims). 
- The middle staff (Alto clef) is labeled "Fixed Melody" <note>original: "Cantus firmus."</note> and contains a melody in whole notes (semibreves). 
- The bottom staff (Tenor clef) contains a supporting part in whole notes (semibreves). 
The exercise consists of 12 measures. There are markers (※) at the end of the first and third staves. At the bottom right of the page, a small fragment of a new staff with a soprano clef is visible, serving as a "catch-notation" to guide the reader to the next page.</image-desc>

*Joseph.* I remember you said earlier, when you handed down the rules for this <term>Species</term> <note>a style of counterpoint; here, the "second species" where two notes move against one</note> in <term>Two-part Writing</term> <note>original: "Bicinio"</note>, that two <term>Half Notes</term> <note>original: "Minimas"</note> are never to be placed on the same line or space; therefore, there is no place for <term>Ties</term> <note>original: "Ligaturæ"; notes of the same pitch joined together to create a suspension</note> here. However, in the <term>final cadence</term> <note>original: "clausula finali"</note> of all three of these models, I see not only a Tie, but also that the final note of the last example ends with a <term>Major Third</term> <note>original: "Tertiâ majore"</note>, which I remember was no less forbidden.

*Aloysius.* You remember quite well. But since there is almost no rule without an exception, I think it must be understood that where the opportunity to do so is found—which can always happen in Two-part Writing, but not in <term>Three-part Writing</term> <note>original: "Tricinio"</note>—it may be used. This can be seen clearly in the preceding examples: if the two Half Notes were played separately instead of being placed in a Tie, it would necessarily result in either a faulty <term>Unison</term> <note>two voices playing the exact same pitch at the same time, which was often avoided in part-writing for lack of variety</note> or an <term>Octave</term> empty of harmony. Necessity excuses the Major Third placed in the final note of the third example, because there is no room for a <term>Fifth</term> in that part of the melody; if a Fifth were placed there, two Fifths following one another immediately would arise <note>this refers to "parallel fifths," a major error in classical counterpoint where two voices move in the same interval of a fifth</note>.

From these examples, it is clear that in one of the parts, Half Notes are to be used throughout the entire course of the <term>Fixed Melody</term> <note>original: "Cantus firmi"</note>, while in the other two parts, simple <term>Whole Notes</term> <note>original: "Semibreves"</note> are to be established. In this way, the Half Notes always harmonize properly with the two Whole Notes, while also taking the movement and the rules into account as much as possible.

Come now, if you please, complete the examples for the remaining <term>positions</term> <note>original: "tenorum"; referring to the different arrangements of the voices</note>, observing the three-fold variation I showed you.

->A<-

<summary>In this dialogue, Joseph questions the use of ties and major thirds in three-part counterpoint, which were forbidden in two-part writing. Aloysius explains that these exceptions are necessary in three-part harmony to avoid errors like parallel fifths or empty-sounding octaves.</summary>
<keywords>second species, counterpoint, half notes, minims, whole notes, semibreves, ties, ligatures, major third, parallel fifths, three-part writing, tricinium</keywords>

*Aloysius.* These examples suffice for the present; I leave the remaining three modes <note>original: "tonos," referring to the church modes like Dorian or Phrygian</note> to be pursued by your own private study in the same manner.
*Joseph.* I find this study full of difficulty, so much so that at times the progress from one beat <note>original: "tactu," the measure or stroke of the hand</note> to the next seems almost impossible to me.
*Aloysius.* I admit that the construction of these Species is not so easy, in which it is necessary for two <term>half notes<gloss>minimas</gloss></term> to harmonize correctly with the <term>whole notes<gloss>semibrevibus</gloss></term> placed in the two [other] parts, while observing all that must be observed. This is a difficult thing to do, and hardly possible, unless I have already conceived in my mind the following one or two beats in advance before I decide to write, just as I have often said. But it can hardly be expressed in words how much utility and how much facility in writing this exercise brings to students. Once they are well-versed in this, and the rigor of the <term>Fixed Melody<gloss>Cantûs firmi</gloss></term> is later removed, they will find themselves like those released from chains, able to complete freer compositions with exultant ease, as if in play.

## LESSON THREE.

Since the same order of progression is proposed to us in this Three-Part Composition <note>original: "Tricinii Compositione"</note> as we used in giving the Two-Part <note>original: "Bicinii"</note> Lessons, it is self-evident that this Lesson is a composition consisting of four <term>quarter notes<gloss>semiminimis</gloss></term> placed against a <term>whole note<gloss>semibrevem</gloss></term>; however, with this distinguishing observation: just as the quarter notes in the Two-Part composition had to harmonize with the whole note of only one part, so in this Three-Part composition they must harmonize with the whole notes of the two [other] parts. For the rest, <gloss>here</gloss> those things must be observed which were said not only about this Species in the Two-Part Lesson, but also those which have been taught up to now concerning the Three-Part Species.
*Joseph.* Does nothing else occur here that needs to be observed?
*Aloysius.* Nothing, except that just as in all species of <term>Counterpoint<gloss>Contrapuncti</gloss></term>, so also in this one, the greatest consideration must be given to those notes that consist in the <term>Downbeat<gloss>Thesi</gloss></term>.
*Joseph.* I will try, then, whether I have the ability to complete this Species without a provided example.
*Aloysius.* That pleases me. But take care that if the first quarter note <insert>of the</insert> <term>Upbeat<gloss>Arhsis</gloss></term>...

# EXERCISE II.
## LESSON FOUR,
### On the Ligature.

At this point, we must recall those things which were said concerning <term>Ligatures</term> in <term>two-part writing</term> <note>original: "Bicinio"</note>; the method of executing these is not to be changed at all in <term>three-part writing</term> <note>original: "Tricinio"</note>, but must be followed exactly. It will be relevant now to teach in what manner, with the addition of a third part, that social harmony <note>original: "socialis illius concordantia"; referring to how the three voices interact as a group</note> should be established. It is also important to remember here what was said above: namely, that a Ligature is nothing other than the <term>delaying of the following note</term> <note>original: "retardationem Notæ sequentis"</note>. From this arises a paradox: that the same harmony ought to be given to the third part as would have been used if the Ligature were omitted. The following <term>examples</term> <note>original: "Paradigmata"</note> will make this truth clear.

->Without Ligature.<-

<image-desc>A musical score for three voices in mensural notation labeled "Without Ligature." The notation uses diamond-shaped whole notes (semibreves) and square double-whole notes (breves) arranged in simple, consonant harmonic progressions across five measures.</image-desc>

->With Ligature.<-

<image-desc>A musical score for three voices labeled "With Ligature." It uses the same staves and melodic structure as the previous example, but the middle voice now incorporates suspensions. These are indicated by curved lines (ligatures) connecting notes across the bar lines, creating rhythmic delays and temporary dissonances against the other two voices before resolving downward.</image-desc>

Where it is clearly seen, in both examples, that the <term>concordance</term> <note>original: "concordantiam"; the harmonic agreement between the voices</note> of the third part is the same, with the <term>ligature</term> <note>A suspension where a note is held over from a previous beat to create a temporary dissonance</note> hindering nothing; I believe the same must be understood regarding ligatures placed in the low, or fundamental, part. For example:

<image-desc>A musical score in three parts. The top voice uses syncopation (ligatures) to create a series of suspensions (7-6 and 4-3 resolutions) against the middle and bottom voices. The notes are diamond-shaped in the style of 18th-century printing.</image-desc>

<image-desc>A musical score identical to the one above, but with the ties (ligatures) removed. This reveals that the voices are moving in parallel fifths, which is a fundamental error in composition.</image-desc>

I have placed this faulty example before your eyes, Joseph, for no other purpose than that you might come to understand that the nature of concordances is not changed at all by ligatures, but remains exactly the same.

*Joseph.* From this assertion of yours, Master, a <term>scruple</term> <note>original: "scrupulus"; a small doubt or hesitation</note> arises in me, which, if it does not bother you, I will reveal.

*Aloys.* Speak out boldly. Indeed, your long silence until now almost led me to doubt whether or not you understood what I have been saying without interruption.

*Joseph.* If ligatures, as has been said, change nothing, then the first example consisting of ligatures recently shown is just as faulty as the second. Therefore, just as in the second, with the ligatures re-

<summary>Aloys demonstrates that ligatures (suspensions) do not change the underlying harmonic structure of a piece; if the underlying structure contains errors like parallel fifths, the ligatures cannot truly "hide" them from a theoretical standpoint.</summary>

# Exercise II. Lesson IV. on the Ligature

...once the <term>ligatures</term> <note>original: "Ligaturis"; a suspension where a note is tied over from a previous beat to create a temporary dissonance before resolving</note> are removed from the example, an immediate succession of several <term>fifths</term> <note>A "perfect" interval that sounds very stable; having two in a row was strictly forbidden in this style of music</note> appears; for the same reason, if the ligatures are considered as if they had not been added, the first example provided with ligatures would not be free from the same fault.

**Aloys.** I am very pleased with this shrewd argument of yours, which is a sign of your great attention. However, besides the fact that much weight must be given to the authority of the famous masters of the art who approve the first example and disapprove the second, you ought to know that when I said, *Ligatures change nothing*, it was to be understood only regarding the nature and essence of the <term>concordances</term> <note>The harmonic agreement between the voices</note>, which are equal in both examples. Otherwise, who can doubt that ligatures have the greatest force and power to save and to change?

**Joseph.** My argument would be resolved by this distinction of yours, were it not for the example of a ligature previously brought forward in the <term>Bicinium</term> <note>A composition or exercise for two voices</note>, which you rejected as faulty because it was unable to save two octaves. For example:

<image-desc>A musical notation example for two voices. The top voice has a tie (ligature) creating an 8-9-8 sequence against the bottom voice's notes. The numbers 8 and 9 are written between the staves to show the interval distance.</image-desc>

Just as the ligature in this example is not able to prevent two faulty <term>octaves</term> <note>Moving from one octave to another between the same two voices was forbidden as it makes the voices sound like they have merged into one</note> from occurring; so for the very same reason, it will not be able to save two fifths in the following:

<image-desc>A musical notation example for two voices. The top voice has a tied note (ligature) creating a suspension over the bottom voice. The number 5 is marked to indicate the interval of a fifth.</image-desc>

**Aloys.** To resolve this, your far from insignificant objection, it is helpful to know that many things are forbidden in the high <note>original: "parte acuta"; the treble or soprano register</note> part because it is more perceptible and accessible to the ears, which are tolerated in the low <note>original: "parte gravi"; the bass register</note> part, being somewhat obscured by its depth and not striking the hearing quite so much. For sharpness <note>pitch height</note> distinguishes; depth...

<note>The text cuts off here as it reaches the end of the page.</note>

<summary>Joseph challenges Aloys by pointing out that if ligatures don't change the underlying harmony, then they shouldn't be able to "fix" illegal parallel fifths. Aloys begins to explain that the human ear perceives errors more clearly in the high registers than in the low ones.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion between Aloys (the master) and Joseph (the student) regarding why some suspensions (ligatures) are acceptable while others are not. Aloys is explaining that the "perfection" of the resolving interval determines how well a suspension disguises faulty parallel movement.</meta>

...suppresses the faults. Then, so that we may arrive at a more fundamental reason and understand the disparity between the examples, let us recall what we said elsewhere regarding the perfection of <term>consonances</term> <note>original: "Consonantiarum"; intervals that sound stable and pleasing to the ear</note>: namely, that the **fifth** is a perfect consonance, the **octave** is more perfect, and the **unison** is the most perfect of all. The more "perfect" these intervals are, the less <term>harmony</term> <note>In this historical context, "harmony" refers to the richness or variety of sound; perfect intervals sound so similar to a single note that they lack the "color" of thirds or sixths</note> they possess. Furthermore, it is established that <term>dissonances</term> <note>Unstable intervals that create tension</note> are in themselves devoid of all the pleasantness and grace of harmony; whatever charm they offer to the ears must be attributed entirely to the benefit of the following consonance into which they are <term>resolved</term> <note>The movement from a clashing dissonance to a stable consonance</note>. 

From this, it is clear that a dissonance resolved into a **fifth** is more tolerable than one that happens to resolve into an **octave**. Hence, it is not surprising that the first example is rejected as faulty by good authors, while the second is approved as being suited to the rules of counterpoint. Finally, the more a perfect consonance (into which a dissonance resolves) approaches the nature of an <term>imperfect consonance</term> <note>Intervals like thirds and sixths, which were considered more "harmonious" and colorful than fifths or octaves</note>, the more easily the resolution should be treated with excuse and indulgence. Now, if you are satisfied, prepare yourself for the exercise of this "species" of counterpoint.

*Joseph.* I shall do as you command.

### Exercise II. Lesson IV. On the <term>Suspension</term> <note>original: "Ligatura"; a technique where a note is held over from a previous beat to create a temporary clash before resolving</note>.

**Aloys.** That doubt of yours is very praiseworthy, as it serves as a testament to your great attention to detail. However, it is of no consequence that this <term>scenario</term> <note>original: "Hypothesis"; here referring to a specific musical arrangement or exercise</note> might seem less consistent with the strictness of the rules. For what has been said—that the first part of a suspension must be a <term>consonance</term> <note>original: "Consonantia"; a stable, pleasing interval</note>—should be understood to apply to those scenarios in which the fundamental part moves with every beat. # EXERCISE III.
## LESSON ONE.
### On Four-part Composition,
->*Or*<-
### The Composition of Four Parts.

It has already been stated above that the perfect ratio of the <term>harmonic triad</term> <note>Latin: *Triadis harmonicæ*; a three-note chord consisting of a root, a third, and a fifth.</note> is contained within <term>Three-part Composition</term> <note>Latin: *Tricinio*.</note>. From this, it is clear that there is no room for an arriving fourth part except through the doubling of some <term>consonance</term> <note>Latin: *Consonantiæ*; intervals like the third, fifth, or octave that sound stable and pleasing.</note> already placed in the three-part texture—except for certain cases of <term>dissonances</term> <note>Latin: *dissonantiarum Hypothesibus*; notes that create tension, such as the seventh or second, which require specific rules for resolution.</note>, which are to be demonstrated in another place. Even though there is a very large disparity between the <term>unison</term> <note>Latin: *Unisonum*; two voices on the exact same pitch.</note> and the <term>octave</term> <note>Latin: *Octavam*; the interval of eight notes, such as C to C.</note> in terms of interval and position, they are entirely the same regarding their common names; for a unison is called "C" just as an octave is, for example, and an octave is generally considered to be a repeated unison.

Therefore, the system of Four-part Composition will consist primarily of the <term>Third</term>, <term>Fifth</term>, and <term>Octave</term>. Where the Octave cannot be used due to faulty progressions (which happens frequently), the Third should be doubled more often in its place, or the <term>Sixth</term> more rarely. Furthermore, the rules of progressions and motions prescribed in the first Book, as far as...
<meta>catchword: [they can] be done</meta>

### Exercise III. Lesson I. On Four-part Composition.

...should be observed as much as possible <note>original: "fieri poterit, observentur"; this completes the thought from the previous page regarding the rules of the harmonic triad.</note>, having regard not only for the relation of the parts to the foundation <note>Latin: *fundamentum*; referring to the bass or the lowest voice upon which the harmony is built.</note>, but also for the relationship of the parts among themselves. I said "as much as possible" because sometimes necessity, the flow of the melody, the requirements of imitation, or the strictness of the subject will dictate that a hidden sequence of fifths or octaves <note>Latin: *Quintarum, vel Octavarum cooperta consecutio*; also known as "hidden" or "direct" fifths/octaves, which occur when two voices move in the same direction to land on a perfect interval.</note> must be tolerated.

Meanwhile, the less one happens to stray from the common rules, the more perfect the resulting four-part composition will be.

**Joseph.** I seem to perceive your words as if through a mist. Therefore, to understand the matter more clearly, I have need of one or two examples.

**Aloysius.** The mist in your mind will be easily dissipated by the following illustration <note>Latin: *Paradigmante*.</note>.

<image-desc>A musical notation example consisting of four staves in white mensural notation (diamond-shaped notes). The top three staves have numerical indicators (3, 5, 8) written above the notes to show the intervals created against the bass. Between the second and third staves, the label "Fixed Song" (Cantus firmus) is written. The staves use Soprano, Alto, Tenor, and Bass clefs to demonstrate how to voice a four-part harmony using the notes of a triad.</image-desc>

Do not wonder that the <term>consonances</term> <note>Sweet-sounding musical intervals such as thirds, fifths, and octaves.</note> or compound intervals <note>Intervals wider than an octave, such as a tenth.</note> are marked with simple numbers, contrary to the custom practiced until now; for I thought this should be done for no other purpose than that the doubling of the consonances might show itself more clearly to the eyes. Follow this model, then, in your imitation; and if any doubt still remains for you, speak up. If nothing remains, take up this very same <term>fixed song</term> <note>Latin: *Cantum firmum*; a simple, pre-existing melody used as the basis for a counterpoint exercise.</note> to be worked through with the usual variations <note>Latin: *mutatione*; referring to the practice of moving the melody to different voices.</note>, placing it successively in each of the parts.

**Joseph.** Does it matter which of the consonances is assigned to which part?

**Aloysius.** It matters very much indeed; this, I believe, has already become known to you from the exercises on three-part composition <note>Latin: *Tricinii*.</note> and what has been said before. For besides the fact that, in the natural order, where possible, every consonan-

### Exercise III. Lesson I. On Four-part Writing, etc.
<meta>catchword: Through-</meta>

...nance <note>Completing the word "consonantia" from the previous page.</note> claims its own proper place, it matters a great deal to look around and see whether, from the system of the first beat <note>Latin: *tactus*; the rhythmic unit or measure.</note> arranged in such a way, the progression to the second, third, or even fourth beat can be properly established. If not, the structure of the first beat must be changed, and the Consonances must be placed in such a way that a convenient and correct means of proceeding to the following beats is made possible.

**Joseph.** What is that proper place you spoke of, which the Consonances claim for themselves?

**Aloysius.** It is the order produced from the harmonic division of the Octave. It is clear from such a division that first the Fifth is generated, and from the further division of the Fifth, the Third is produced. This order is to be maintained in the placement of Consonances, unless another reason—namely, the method of proceeding into the following beats, which happens very frequently—stands in the way. I provide an example here, showing the natural order of the Consonances:

<image-desc>Musical notation on a five-line staff with a C-clef on the first line. A four-note chord is shown with notes stacked vertically: C (on the first line), G (on the third line), C (the octave above), and E (the tenth above). The notes are diamond-shaped.</image-desc>

You see the Fifth placed in the first position, generated from the division of the Octave; in the second position, the Octave existing by itself; and in the third position, the Third (or the Tenth) produced through the division of the Fifth.

**Joseph.** According to the system of our modern keyboard <note>Latin: *Claviatura*.</note>, it seems the Third should be placed in the first position, even before the Fifth; therefore, the system for four-part writing should be arranged in the following order:

<image-desc>Musical notation on a five-line staff with a C-clef on the first line. A four-note chord is shown: C (on the first line), E (on the second line), G (on the third line), and C (the octave above). The notes are diamond-shaped.</image-desc>

**Aloysius.** It certainly seems so, but in reality, it is not. For besides the fact that the judgment of order must be taken from the Division <note>Referring to the mathematical division of a vibrating string.</note> and not from the layout of the keyboard, there is the added fact that a Third placed in a low position, close to the fundamental part <note>The bass.</note>, produces a confused and muddy sound. Furthermore, the larger the terms or numbers constituting an interval are, the higher the sound of that same interval will be; hence, it also claims a higher place for itself. Moreover, the constitutive terms of the Fifth are 2 and 3, which when joined by addition give 5. But the terms of the Third are 4 and 5, which joined together make 9. Thus, by natural reason, it is established that the Fifth must be placed in a lower position, and the Third in a higher position.

<summary>Aloysius explains the "natural" vertical arrangement of chords in four-part writing, arguing that the fifth should be placed below the third based on acoustic clarity and mathematical ratios.</summary>
<keywords>four-part writing, counterpoint, harmonic division, fifth, third, octave, acoustic clarity, musical ratios, Fux, Josephus, Aloysius</keywords>

### Exercise III. Lesson I. On Four-Part Writing.

Proceed now, if no doubt remains for you, to the remaining labors of this and the other tones, by surveying the workshops <note>Latin: *ergastula*; here referring to the structured measures or "work-spaces" of the composition.</note> of all the beats <note>Latin: *tactuum*; the rhythmic unit or measure.</note>, and comparing the individual parts with one another, lest anything contrary to the rules happens to creep in. Great circumspection is needed to accomplish this; here it must be observed that not only the parts with the Bass (the fundamental part), but also the middle parts among themselves, must correctly accord according to the tenor of the rules.

**Joseph.** From the preceding example of four-part writing <note>Latin: *Quatricinium*.</note>, I found that a Fourth sometimes occurs between the middle parts. You established that this is a dissonance, and you affirmed it to be forbidden in composition consisting of mere semibreves <note>A "semibreve" is what we now call a whole note.</note>; I could not pass this over without a scruple <note>A feeling of doubt or hesitation.</note>.

**Aloysius.** Rightly so. But it must be remembered that the nature of Consonances and dissonances is to be measured with respect to the fundamental part <note>The Bass.</note>, whatever may result between the middle parts—provided that other absurdities, such as the succession of two Fifths or Octaves, are avoided. Furthermore, it should be noted that the more the parts are contracted into a narrow space, the more perfect the resulting Harmony, according to the saying: *Strength united is stronger* <note>Latin: *Vis unita fortior*.</note>. If anything difficult is set before you, as can hardly be avoided, think that one must ascend to virtue through hardships <note>Latin: *per aspera*; referring to the proverb that excellence requires struggle.</note>; and that athletes do not enter the Olympic games without an opponent. Untiring study, constancy, and the shield of patience are necessary in the struggle.

->Fixed Melody <note>Latin: *Cantus firmus*.</note><-

The third staff is specifically designated as the "Fixed Melody" (Cantus firmus).</image-desc>

## SECOND LESSON,
### Concerning Minims against the Semibreve <note>In modern musical terms, this refers to "Second Species" counterpoint, where two half-notes (minims) are written against every one whole-note (semibreve).</note>.

Here it is necessary to recall to memory what we said above regarding this species of Counterpoint in Three-Part Composition <note>Latin: *Tricinio*.</note>; all of those rules must also be observed in this four-part composition. There is no difference other than that there [in three parts] two Minims <note>Half notes.</note> must harmonize with two semibreves <note>The notes of the other two voices.</note>, whereas here they must harmonize with three <note>The notes of the three other voices in the four-part texture.</note>. For the rest, those things which were said regarding four-part note-against-note counterpoint should be observed, as much as the restricted constraints <note>Latin: *ligamen*, referring to the "binding" or strict rules of the counterpoint species.</note> of this species allow.

Proceed to the exercise examples.

# Exercise III. Lesson II. Concerning Minims against the Semibreve <note>original: *de Minimis contra Semibrevem*. This refers to "Second Species" counterpoint, where one voice moves in half-notes while the others move in whole-notes.</note>.

### [Musical Exercise]
<note>The image description indicates musical staves for four voices. In these exercises, one voice—the soprano, alto, tenor, or bass—moves in minims (half notes) while the other three voices, including the "Fixed song" (Cantus firmus), move in semibreves (whole notes).</note>

*Joseph.* I find this Species of Counterpoint <note>original: *Contrapuncti Speciem*</note> full of difficulty; indeed, none has seemed more difficult to me until now, so much so that <gloss>not even</gloss> the second-to-last note <note>original: *penultimam*</note> could I manage according to that rule.

*Aloysius.* It is so. Truly, the weight of this difficulty arises from the obligation of placing two Minims <note>half notes</note> against three Semibreves <note>whole notes, which constitute the other three voices of the four-part texture</note>: it was established for no other purpose than that you might acquire the science of harmony <note>original: *concordandi scientiam*</note>, and to make you cautious, circumspect, and attentive in arranging harmonies. Hence it is no wonder that in certain situations the ability to proceed is not found: nor will it happen in any free style of composing that such a long series of measures <note>original: *tactuum*</note> must be drawn out by this law. For these lessons were invented not for the sake of common use, but for the sake of exercise: just as spelling <note>original: *Syllabizzatio*</note> is no longer practiced by one who knows how to read; so also the Species of Counterpoint are prescribed solely for the purpose of learning. I leave the remaining examples in the various modes <note>original: *tonorum*</note> to your private study.

*Aloysius.* I respond that it could indeed have been done that way; but besides the fact that when a unison is placed on the strong beat <term>Thesis; the downbeat or accented part of a measure</term> the fullness of the composition is reduced by no small amount, there is also the fact that the third—or tenth <note>An interval of a tenth is a third plus an octave; in counterpoint, they are functionally the same.</note>—in the moving voice part would be missing <note>original: *manca*; meaning "maimed" or "defective"</note> as it wanders, because it would not be heard continuously. What does the second mark of doubt, **B**, indicate?

*Joseph.* That progression between the Alto and Tenor seems faulty, because it moves from one perfect interval to another perfect interval by direct motion <term>Motu recto; when two voices move in the same direction to land on a perfect fifth or octave, which was generally avoided in strict counterpoint</term>.

<column-break/>

Fixed song. <note>original: *Cantus firmus*</note>

<summary>The master Aloysius explains why certain technical "faults" in counterpoint, such as direct motion to perfect intervals or doubling, are sometimes tolerated for the sake of harmonic fullness or when restricted by the slow-moving voices. # Exercise III. Lesson III. Concerning Crotchets against etc. <note>original: *de Semiminimis contra &c.*</note>

*Aloysius.* Excellent, Joseph! With this example, you have given an outstanding demonstration of your progress. From it, you show that nothing of what has been discussed throughout the course of so many lessons has slipped from your memory. This is because you placed the first crotchet <term>Semiminima; a quarter note, representing the "Third Species" of counterpoint</term> to ensure a complete harmony, and then, with the remaining three notes wandering here and there, you entered the following measure <term>Tactus; the primary beat or the measure unit itself</term> by a clear path. Furthermore, you have correctly observed the other rules of harmony and the various species of counterpoint. This success occurs when the **Thesis**—which is the beginning of any measure—is provided with a complete harmony; that is, when it is supplied with a third and a fifth. Proceed.

*Aloysius.* What does that letter **A**, placed in the second measure <term>Tactus; the unit of time or measure</term> of the melody, signify?

*Joseph.* That progression from a perfect consonance to another perfect consonance by direct motion <term>Motu recto; when two voices move in the same direction—up or down—to reach an interval</term>—that is, from a fifth to an octave—causes me no small amount of concern. It worries me all the more because it happens to occur in the outer voices <note>The "outer voices" are the highest and lowest parts, where musical errors are most audible to the listener.</note>.

*Aloysius.* I have already mentioned this a little while ago, and I repeat it once more: such progressions must occasionally be forgiven due to the necessity of the whole notes <term>Semibreves; whole notes, which move slowly and restrict the melodic choices available to the composer</term>. Indeed, they cannot always be avoided even in a freer style of composition. However, as you rightly observed, they are more tolerable in the middle parts than in the outer voices.

### Exercise III. Lesson III. Regarding Crotchets <note>original: *Semiminimis*</note> against etc.

Furthermore, that specific flaw is even harder for the ear to perceive because it occurs in a middle voice. Now, while I am here, you shall complete the next mode, *E la mi* <term>The note E; named according to the medieval system of solmization</term>. This one is the most difficult of all to compose because of the "defect of the fifth" <note>In the mode starting on E (Phrygian), the relationship between certain notes can make it difficult to form the perfect fifths required by traditional harmony.</note>. I leave the remaining exercises for you to work on at home <note>original: *lucubrandos*; literally, to work by lamplight.</note>.

*Joseph.* Between the parts of the Soprano <note>original: *Cantûs*</note> and the Violin, from the first to the second note, the interval is found to move from a third to a fifth, which is against the common rule. For the reasons previously mentioned, I did not wish to correct it, nor did I find a way to do so.

*Aloys.* I have already emphasized many times that in this context, many things must be granted to necessity which ought to be avoided in a free style of composition. Similarly, that transition marked **B.** from an octave to a fifth—relative to the lowest part and moving in similar motion <term>Similar motion (motu recto): when two or more musical parts move in the same direction, either up or down, at the same time</term>—should not be considered a fault, given the difficulty of this Species. Now, since you should complete the remaining tones at home, let us proceed to another Species.

# EXERCISE III.
## LESSON FOUR,

Having completed the Species of Crotchets <note>original: *Semiminimarum*</note>, we know from the lessons on Two-part <note>original: *Bicinii*</note> and Three-part <note>original: *Triciniique*</note> writing that Ligatures <term>Ligatures: also known as suspensions, these occur when a note is sustained or tied from a previous beat into a new chord where it is often dissonant, before resolving downward to a stable tone</term> follow next. I assume that their nature is already well understood from those previous lessons. It now remains to be explained which companion harmonies <note>original: *Concordantiarum comites*</note> the ligatures should take for themselves in Four-part <note>original: *Quatricinio*</note> writing. Something was already said regarding this in three-part composition: namely, that they require the same harmonies they would seek if the ligature were removed. This is for the reason stated there: that a ligature is nothing other than a delay of the following note, which changes nothing regarding the underlying harmonies. Examples will make the matter clear.

Therefore, the strict rule of three whole notes in this species cannot be held very strictly.

*Joseph.* A seventh accompanied by an octave would not be subject to the division of the whole note at all: as can be seen in your previous example, in this manner:

### Exercise III. Lesson IV. regarding Crotchets <note>original: *Semiminimis*</note> against [the other parts], etc.

*Joseph.* I find nothing to doubt in this example, except for the progression from the fourth to the fifth beat <term>tactum: the pulse or "touch" used to measure time in early music</term> between the Tenor and Alto parts.

<image-desc>A musical snippet consisting of two staves with C-clefs, showing a two-measure progression in white mensural notation.</image-desc>

*Aloys.* Do not let this progression trouble you. You must know that a fourth between the middle parts is either considered of no consequence, or it performs the duty of an imperfect consonance. <note>In traditional counterpoint, the interval of a fourth is considered a dissonance when it occurs against the lowest voice (the bass), but it is often treated as a "consonance" when it occurs between upper or middle voices.</note> Hence, that progression is to be held in the same way as if you were proceeding from a perfect consonance to an imperfect one by direct motion: which should be well noted. Now to the remaining examples.

### Exercise III. Lesson IV. regarding Crotchets against etc.

Then, in the fifth measure of the last example, the second <note>original: *Secunda*</note> is doubled while the sixth <note>original: *Sextâ*</note> is missing, which is desired for a full harmonic account, as the following example demonstrates.

*Aloys.* It is not so much a matter of the second or the fourth as it is a matter of complete harmony. For since the fullness of harmony consists in the combination of the third, the fifth, and the octave; and in the said example, the fifth is found doubled in place of the octave, there the har-

Similar defects of no great importance must be granted to the rigor of this Species <note>the specific "species" or category of counterpoint being studied</note>, because of the exceptional benefit this exercise brings to students. It not only teaches the correct method of composition, but also how much one is permitted to deviate from the rigor of the rules when necessity demands. I have placed a pattern before your eyes; I leave it to you to handle the remaining five tones <note>original: *tonos* — referring here to the musical modes</note> in the same manner and with similar variation by imitating it. # EXERCISE III.

Thus, nothing new needs to be added here, except for the fourth part, which consists of a whole note <term>Semibrevi: a whole note, the standard unit of time in this system</term>, to be woven in according to the rules of four-part writing <term>Quatricinii: a composition for four voices</term>. # EXERCISE III. LESSON FIVE.

*Joseph.* I see that just as in the species of ties <note>original: *ligaturarum* — referring to the fourth species of counterpoint where notes are tied across bar lines to create syncopation</note>, here too one or two of the whole notes <note>original: *Semibrevium*</note> have been divided.

*Aloysius.* When I said that whole notes <note>original: *Semibreves*</note> ought to remain whole, I intended this to be understood as a matter of preference where possible. In the meantime, however, you will notice that wherever the opportunity was given, that rule was observed with great precision. I leave the remaining exercises of the five modes <note>original: *tonorum* — the church modes or scales used in Renaissance and Baroque music</note> to be pursued by you with equal zeal.

Having now completed the five species, each practiced separately, I now most strongly encourage you to perform them together. Keeping the same Fixed Song <note>original: *Cantu firmo* — the foundational melody around which other parts are composed</note>, you should join, for example, half notes <note>original: *Minimas*</note>, quarter notes <note>original: *Semiminimas*</note>, and ties <note>original: *Ligaturas*</note>. Once this is done, with each of the parts having gained its own unique movement, a wonderful variety will be added to the composition. As shown in the example:

<page-num>139</page-num>
# EXERCISE III. LESSON FIVE.

With equal zeal, I task you with completing both this and the remaining five modes <note>original: *tonos* — the church modes or scales</note> by varying the position of the **Fixed Song** <note>original: *Cantûs firmi*</note> fourfold, so that it may happen to be placed in all four parts. You shall give your effort to grasp as a whole those things which were said about each **Species** <note>the five categories of counterpoint rules</note> separately; and you should observe precisely what the duty of the parts is toward one another (a matter which I think is no longer hidden from you). It can hardly be expressed how much benefit these properly instituted exercises bring to students: for indeed, almost no difficulty will arise later that will not be familiar to you once these Species are mastered. Therefore, if you desire to make progress in this Discipline, I exhort you with the greatest intensity to spend no small amount of time exercising these five Species. By always choosing new Fixed Songs at your own discretion, you should spend at least a year or two insisting upon this exercise. Nor should you let yourself be swept away by a premature greed for proceeding to *ideal* or *free* composition <note>original: *Compoſitionem idealem, arbitrariamque* — writing music without the strict rules of counterpoint</note>; enticed by its sweetness, you might waste time wandering here and there, never reaching the attainment of a true foundation.

*Joseph.* You command me to walk a rough and very thorny path, Venerable Master. For it is hardly possible that I should not be affected by weariness, spending so much time on this tedious labor.

*Aloysius.* I forgive your complaint, Joseph, and I feel for you. Truly, the Muses are said to inhabit a mountain which cannot be reached except by a steep path. There is no apprentice of even the most humble mechanical art <note>trades involving manual labor or craftsmanship</note> who is not bound to at least a three-year apprenticeship <note>original: *tyrocinio*</note>. What shall I say of **Music**? It not only far excels the unskilled and manual arts in genius, difficulty, and wealth of subject matter, but it is second to none of the **liberal arts** <note>the higher academic disciplines of the classical world, including grammar, logic, and astronomy</note>. Let the future usefulness of your sweat stimulate you. Let the hope of glory entice you. Let the ease of writing that will come from it incite you, along with a certain confidence in the correctness of whatever you shall write. Once this is done, and the Fixed Song is removed for a time, I shall lead you to the knowledge of imitations and **fugues**. First, however, it must be mentioned that outside the restriction of the Fixed Song, certain **dissonances** <note>original: *diſſonantiarum*</note> can also be resolved in another way: for example, the Ninth into the Sixth, and the Tenth into the Third; the Fourth into the Sixth, and the Third in the manner written below. For example:

<image-desc>A musical notation fragment in a bass clef illustrating three types of dissonance resolutions described in the text. The first measure shows a ninth resolving to a sixth (notes G and A moving to G and F), labeled "9 6" above the staff. The second shows a tenth resolving to a third (notes G and B moving to B and D), labeled "10 3". The third shows a fourth resolving to a sixth (notes G and C moving to G and E), labeled "4 6".</image-desc>

<header>Exercise IV. Sole Lesson on Imitation.</header>

<image-desc>Two systems of musical notation illustrating complex resolutions. The first system shows a suspension where a 9th resolves to a 6th, and a 9th to a 10th. The second shows a 7th to a 10th, and a 4th to a 6th. The figured bass includes "5 falsa" (a diminished fifth).</image-desc>

*Joseph.* Why do these resolutions <note>The way a dissonant note moves to a stable, consonant one.</note> occur outside of the **Fixed Song** <note>original: *Cantum firmum* — the pre-existing, slow-moving melody used as a foundation.</note>, and not also with the **Fixed Song**?

*Aloysius.* Do you not see that both parts move during the resolution? However, can that happen with the **Fixed Song**, which is immobile? There is, therefore, a disparity: and it is clear that in these resolutions, where **oblique motion** <note>original: *motus obliquus* — when one voice stays on the same note while the other moves.</note> is indispensable, there is no place for them at all. Now I shall lead you back to the composition of a **Bicinium** <note>original: *Bicinii* — a musical composition for two voices.</note>, restricted by no bond of a **Fixed Song**.

# EXERCISE IV.
## SOLE LESSON,
->On Imitation.<-

**Imitation** <note>original: *Imitatio* — a technique where a melodic idea is presented in one voice and then repeated by another.</note> occurs when a following part follows the leader after some pause, preserving the same intervals which the leader traversed, without regard for Mode, Tone, or Semitone; <note>This means the imitation follows the shape of the melody rather than staying strictly within a specific scale or key.</note> this can be done at the **Unison**, **Second**, **Third**, **Fourth**, **Fifth**, **Sixth**, **Seventh**, and **Octave**; which will appear more clearly through examples.

*At the Unison.*

*Joseph.* From this model <note>original: *paradigmate*</note>, I gather that not all the notes of the preceding part must be repeated in the following one.

*Aloysius.* You have gathered correctly. For that is the duty of **Canons** <note>original: *Canonum* — a strict form of composition where voices must follow each other exactly throughout.</note>, not of imitations, in which it is enough to follow only certain notes.

Furthermore, imitation is usually employed not so much at the beginning of a **Composition** <note>original: *Concentûs* — a "singing together" or harmonious piece.</note> as in the middle. There, the strictness of the mode is not much considered.

<page-num>143</page-num>
<header>Exercise V. First Lesson. On Fugues in general.</header>

# EXERCISE V.
## FIRST LESSON,
->*On Fugues in General.*<-

Many noteworthy authors affirm that the word "Fugue" is derived from the words *to flee* and *to chase* <note>original: "à fugere, ac fugare"</note>; it is as if the preceding part were fleeing while being chased by the following part. This would indeed be true, were it not for the fact that "imitation" (as we have already discussed) does exactly the same thing. Therefore, we must establish another definition to distinguish the Fugue from imitation. I say, then: A Fugue is the repetition by a following part of certain notes previously placed in the preceding part, while taking into account the mode and, for the most part, the arrangement of tones and semitones. To understand this definition, it is clearly necessary to know what a "Mode" is. By **Mode** <term>Mode: in this context, the ancestor of our modern musical scales (major and minor), used to organize the melody</term>, I mean that which is commonly called a "tone." Thus, while people say "first tone," "second tone," and so on, it is better to say "first Mode," "second Mode," etc. This is to avoid confusion with the interval of the **sesquioctave** <term>Sesquioctave: the mathematical ratio 9:8, representing a "major second" or a whole tone</term> and the **sesquinone** <term>Sesquinone: the mathematical ratio 10:9, representing a "minor second" or a slightly smaller whole tone</term>, which are also both called by the name "tone." However, since the subject of the Modes is perhaps the most intricate of all, and not easily grasped by a beginner, we shall be content for now to explain only as much as is sufficient for our present purpose; we believe the full explanation should be deferred to the end of this work. A Mode, therefore, is a series of intervals contained within the limits of an Octave, defined by the varied and differing placement of the **Semitones** <term>Semitone: the smallest interval in traditional Western music; the "half-step" found between E-F and B-C on a piano</term>. To this point belongs the famous saying of Horace:

> *There is a measure in things; there are, finally, fixed boundaries,*
> *Beyond and short of which the right cannot find a place.*
> <note>original: "Est modus in rebus, sunt certi denique fines, Quos ultrà, citraque nequit consistere rectum." The author is using a play on words, as the Latin *modus* means both "musical mode" and "limit" or "measure."</note>

Since the variety of semitone placements is found to be six-fold, six Modes must also be established. These are shown in the following systems of Octaves, namely: D, E, F, G, A, and C.

<image-desc>Two musical staves using 18th-century notation. The first staff shows a scale from D to D (Dorian mode), with the syllables "mi fa" marking the half-steps between E-F and B-C. The second staff shows a scale from E to E (Phrygian mode), likewise marking the "mi fa" semitones. A small musical fragment appears at the bottom right indicating the continuation of the lesson.</image-desc>

<sig>Nn 2</sig>

<vocab>Fugue, imitation, Mode, tone, sesquioctave, sesquinone, Semitone, Octave, Horace</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the "Fugue" as a specific type of melodic repetition governed by musical Modes, distinguishing it from general imitation. The author introduces the six classical Modes based on the placement of semitones within an octave scale.</summary>
<keywords>Fugue definition, musical modes, etymology of fugue, Horace, music theory intervals, church modes, counterpoint basics</keywords>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page's introduction to the definition of a Fugue and the concept of Modes, this page explains the structural limitations of the octave and how the placement of semitones defines the relationship between the leading and following voices in a Fugue.</meta>

<page-num>144</page-num>
<header>Exercise V. First Lesson. On Fugues in general.</header>

<image-desc>Four musical staves presenting short sequences of diamond-shaped notes in mensural notation style. Each sequence highlights a semitone step using a combination of hollow (white) and filled (black) notes. Solfege syllables are printed beneath the notes: the first staff has "fa", "mi fa", "mi fa"; the second "mi fa", "re mi fa sol"; the third and fourth both show "mi fa", "mi fa".</image-desc>

You see the existence of **semitones** <term>semitones: the smallest musical interval in traditional Western music, such as the distance between B and C</term> in six different locations, counting from the first note of each **Mode** <term>Mode: a system of melodic organization, like a precursor to modern scales</term>. This diversity is indicated by the blackness of the notes <note>In the musical example, the filled-in black notes highlight where the semitone (the "mi-fa" step) occurs within different melodic contexts</note>. As for which of these is the first, second, or third, etc., we will pass over that for the present; meanwhile, we shall number them according to the order in which they are placed here. Furthermore, a Mode is bounded by the limits of the **Fourth** and **Fifth** <term>Fourth and Fifth: intervals encompassing four and five letter-names respectively; they are the building blocks that make up an eight-note octave</term> existing within an **Octave**. The themes of Fugues must be regulated according to these limits. For example: <note>original: "E. C." (Exempli Causa)</note>

<image-desc>A single musical staff containing six measures of vertical intervals or chords. The first three measures are written in a C-clef, and the last three in an F-clef, demonstrating harmonic relationships within the octave.</image-desc>

Thus, if the beginning part makes a **leap** <term>leap: a melodic movement across an interval larger than a single step</term> of a Fifth, it is necessary that the following part make a leap of a Fourth, so that it does not exceed the limit of the Mode or the Octave; and vice versa. For example: <note>original: "E. G." (Exempli Gratia)</note>

<image-desc>Two musical staves showing a two-part contrapuntal passage. The upper voice begins with an ascending fifth (C to G), and the lower voice responds by imitating the theme but adjusted to a fourth (G to C), illustrating the rule of tonal fugue mentioned in the text.</image-desc>

To this rule **imitation** is not bound; for it, it is sufficient to follow the same steps and leaps. For example: <note>This distinction is crucial: in simple "imitation," the intervals are copied exactly, but in a "fugue," the intervals are often adjusted (from a fifth to a fourth) to stay within the proper musical "key" or Mode.</note>

<image-desc>Two musical staves showing another example of two-part musical imitation where the lower voice follows the melodic contour of the upper voice exactly.</image-desc>

<vocab>semitones, Mode, Fugues, imitation, leap, C-clef, F-clef</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the technical construction of a fugue, specifically how a melody must be adjusted (from a fifth to a fourth) to stay within the boundaries of a Mode. It distinguishes between a "Fugue," which follows these structural rules, and "Imitation," which simply copies the melody exactly.</summary>
<keywords>tonal fugue, musical modes, semitones, melodic leap, counterpoint, fourth and fifth intervals, imitation</keywords>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the definition of Fugue and Imitation on page 144, this page discusses the specific tonal requirements for constructing a Fugue subject within the various musical modes.</meta>

<page-num>145</page-num>
<header>Exercise V. Lesson I. On Fugues in general.</header>
<columns>2</columns>

Lastly, a **Fugue** <term>Fugue: a musical technique where a short melody is introduced by one part and then taken up by others in succession</term> cannot be established outside the intervals that constitute a Mode—for example, outside the **Unison**, **Octave**, and **Fifth**. **Imitation**, however, as has been said, can occur at any interval.

*Joseph.* Up to this point, I believe I have understood what has been said about Modes, Imitation, and Fugues. Now, I ask that you teach me what is required in choosing subjects, and in what way Fugues should be established and conducted; for I have very often heard that the matter of Fugues is not known even to all professors of this discipline.

*Aloys.* It is so; talk of them is heard more frequently than actual knowledge. So that nothing about them remains hidden from you, I will explain what you have asked. It is established from the demonstration given before that six different **Modes** are possible. Therefore, each of the Modes must be fitted with its own proper subject. From this it follows that a theme proper to the first Mode will by no means suit the second, third, fourth, fifth, or sixth, because of the placement of tones and semitones, which is found differently in each Mode, as the examples will show.

->*A subject proper to the first Mode.*<-

<image-desc>Two short musical staves in soprano clef. The first shows the notes D, F, E, D labeled with solmization syllables "re, fa, mi, re." The second is similar.</image-desc>
<column-break/>
This theme, when taken up by the following part at the **Fifth** <term>Fifth: the interval between the first and fifth notes of a scale</term>, preserves the same tones and semitones. For example: <note>original: "E. G." (Exempli Gratia)</note>

This is something that cannot happen with this subject in any of the other Modes. Let us look at the Mode of *A, la, mi, re* <note>The Aeolian mode, beginning on A, which is the relative minor to C major</note>, which alone is closest in equality to the Mode of *D, la, sol, re* <note>The Dorian mode, beginning on D</note>:

---

<image-desc>A single wide musical staff showing two four-note segments. The first is D, F, E, D (re, fa, mi, re). The second is E, G, F, E (mi, sol, fa, mi).</image-desc>

Where, in the second-to-last note of the following part, *mi, fa* <note>The solmization syllables indicating a semitone step</note> occurs instead, and the placement of the tone and semitone is inverted. Nor can the system of the subject proposed in the first part be maintained otherwise, except with the help of a **Sharp** <term>Sharp: referred to in the text as a 'Diesis,' a symbol that raises a note by a half-step</term> placed before the second-to-last note. For example:

<image-desc>A musical staff showing the motif D, F, E, D. An archaic sharp sign (Diesis) is placed before the third note (E) to alter the semitone placement.</image-desc>
<column-break/>
Which the **Diatonic genus** <term>Diatonic genus: the standard system of music using only the natural notes of the scale without chromatic alterations</term>, in which we are now engaged, by no means allows; but for every sharp,

<sig>O o</sig>
<meta>catchword: sharp,</meta>

<summary>This page explains that a Fugue subject is strictly bound to its Mode because the specific arrangement of tones and semitones must remain identical when the melody is transposed to the fifth. Fux demonstrates that a subject working in one mode (Dorian) fails in another (Aeolian) because the intervals do not align without forbidden chromatic alterations.</summary>
<keywords>Fugue subject, musical modes, Dorian, Aeolian, solmization, tones and semitones, Diatonic genus, Diesis, Johann Joseph Fux</keywords>

<page-num>146</page-num>
<header>Exercise V. Lesson II. On the Two-Part Fugue.</header>

One must abstain from sharps <note>original: "Diesi"</note> and flats <note>original: "b. molli"</note> in the themes; otherwise, we would never truly understand the genuine nature of the **Modes** <term>Modes: the ancient precursors to modern major and minor scales, each with a unique arrangement of whole and half steps</term>.

# EXERCISE V.
## SECOND LESSON,
### On the Two-Part Fugue

Now I will teach you the art of constructing two-part **Fugues** <term>Fugue: a composition where a short melody is introduced and then imitated by other parts</term> using a plain and very simple method. Take a **Subject** <term>Subject: the primary musical theme or "main idea" of a fugue</term> of several notes appropriate to the Mode in which you intend to work; write those notes in the part where you have decided to begin. Once these are finished, provided nothing is hindered, you will give the same notes to the second or following part, entering at the **Fourth** or **Fifth** <term>Fourth/Fifth: intervals of four or five scale degrees above the starting note</term>. Meanwhile, with the part that began the theme, you will construct a **Counterpoint** <term>Counterpoint: the technique of writing a second, independent melody to play against the first</term> above it by modulating with various note shapes, just as you were taught in **Florid Counterpoint** <term>Florid Counterpoint: a style of composition using varied rhythmic patterns rather than just note-against-note</term>.

Having done this, after a bit of melodic movement, by arranging the parts accordingly, you will create the first **Cadence** <term>Cadence: a melodic or harmonic configuration that creates a sense of resolution or "punctuation"</term> on the fifth of the Mode. After this, having placed a rest of a whole or half measure <note>original: "tactus"</note>—or even without a rest if a large leap intervenes—you will resume the theme in roughly the same part where you began, but at a different interval than how it started. The other part, after a short rest, will attempt to enter before the subject is finished in the other part <note>This technique of overlapping subjects is known as "stretto."</note>; then, after some melodic movement, you will build a second cadence on the **Third** of the Mode. Lastly, having placed the subject in either part, you will make the other part enter with the subject immediately on its second beat, if the situation allows; and so, with the parts joined in a very close bond, you will bring the Fugue to an end with a final cadence.

*Joseph.* I remember you said above—

*Aloysius.* It is best to defer your doubt for now, until this obscure matter becomes clear through an example. I will therefore take the subject by which the nature of the Mode was demonstrated just a moment ago, and by conducting it in the prescribed manner, I will create a Fugue; by this model, an easier and more direct path will be laid out for you. For example:

->Two-part Fugue. \hspace{100pt} Cadence on the 5th.<-

<image-desc>A musical score in two staves using C-clefs (soprano/alto). It displays a two-part fugal exercise. The top voice enters with a subject consisting of half notes and quarter notes. After several measures, the lower voice enters with the same subject transposed, while the upper voice continues with a counterpoint. The score concludes with a cadence labeled "Cadence on the 5th."</image-desc>

<header>Exercise V. Lesson II. on the Two-Part Fugue.</header>

<image-desc>Two staves of musical notation in C-clef (Soprano and Alto) showing a two-voice fugal exercise. The lower voice begins with a musical subject, followed by the upper voice imitating the subject a fifth higher.</image-desc>

->*Cadence in the third.* <note>original: "Cadentia in 3tia."</note><-

<image-desc>Two staves of musical notation in C-clef showing a brief musical passage labeled as a cadence in the third.</image-desc>

Look, the **Subject** <term>Subject: the primary melodic theme of the fugue</term> is completed in the Alto, and it is taken up by the Soprano <note>original: "Cantu"</note> at the fifth of the Mode; meanwhile, the Alto wanders in a kind of **Florid Counterpoint** until the subject in the Soprano is finished. *After a brief movement is made* in both parts, a **Cadence** <term>Cadence: a melodic or harmonic configuration that creates a sense of resolution or "closing" a phrase</term> is constructed at the fifth of the mode. *This being done*, you see the subject is repeated by the Alto a fifth lower, indeed without a rest, because it is done by a leap; then, after a rest is placed, the Soprano part enters at the octave of the Mode; but earlier than in the beginning, namely on the third beat <note>original: "tactu"</note> of the subject. While the Alto modulates in the meantime, and the subject is finally finished in the Soprano, after a brief movement of both parts, you see a second cadence constructed in the third of the Mode. *Finally*, you will find that with the subject taken up in the Alto, the same subject is immediately woven into the Soprano on the second beat, and from there the end is imposed by a final cadence. Using this most simple method, and by gradually practicing the other Modes, have no doubt that you will attain a fuller knowledge of Fugues.

**Joseph.** I see in this Fugue that **sharps** <note>original: "Dieses"</note> and **flats** <note>original: "b mollia"</note> are placed in some locations, contrary to the rule about not using them in the **Diatonic Genus** <term>Diatonic Genus: the standard scale of seven notes (like the white keys of a piano) without extra accidentals like sharps or flats</term>.

**Aloysius.** I think it should be understood as referring to the subjects, where the half-steps <note>original: "Semitonia"</note> should occur by natural reasoning, without the help of a sharp or flat, in order to recognize the nature and difference of the **Modes**. There is a different reasoning in the general melodic movement, where the use of a sharp or flat is not only not forbidden, but even sometimes necessary to avoid the harsh relation of *mi* against *fa* <term>mi contra fa: a "forbidden" musical interval, the tritone, which sounds dissonant and was historically nicknamed the "devil in music"</term>. These aforementioned relations arise mostly from the following opposition of *mi* against *fa*.

<sig>O o 2</sig>

<summary>Aloysius demonstrates the structure of a two-part fugue, showing how the subject moves between Alto and Soprano voices, and explains that while accidentals are forbidden in the subjects themselves to preserve modal character, they are necessary in the counterpoint to avoid dissonant intervals like the tritone.</summary>
<keywords>fugue, subject, soprano, alto, cadence, counterpoint, diatonic genus, sharps, flats, mi contra fa, tritone, musical modes</keywords>

*Aloys.* In the first instance, consideration was given to the **Fundamental Note** <term>Fundamental Note: the bass note or root upon which a musical chord or "system" is built</term> and the following system:

<image-desc>A small musical diagram with two staves. Each staff shows three diamond-shaped notes stacked vertically, representing the notes of a triad (D-F-A), illustrating a harmonic structure.</image-desc>

In which the fourth is certainly not present <note>original: "quarta utique non inest"</note>: in the second instance, however, by omitting the fundamental note and considering the relationship of the upper part, I looked toward the following system:
