# Exercise V. Lesson VII. on Double Counterpoint, etc.
### 212

I have ordered you to walk this path: without which, however, you would never have arrived at a perfect knowledge of this art. Now is the time that I should allow you to wander from the thorny labor of this kind into a more pleasant field of study, abounding in little flowers. I shall leave you to yourself, and grant you the faculty of selecting subjects according to your own judgment, of forming ideas, and of ranging through the **mixed genre** <note>original: "genus mixtum"; a style of composition that combines different types of movement or relaxes the strict rules of the purely diatonic style.</note>, which is not circumscribed by such great narrowness. Furthermore, from now on, it will be within your discretion to use all kinds of time signatures—triple or quadruple—at a more relaxed measure, as well as smaller note values, irregular subjects, and **transposed modes** <note>original: "Modis transpositis"; playing a musical mode starting on a different pitch than its usual starting note, often requiring accidentals like sharps or flats.</note>.

*Joseph.* I am wonderfully glad that I will finally be released from these chains: by which it will happen that I may be permitted to test myself and my own talent. But before I proceed, I remember that you promised a little while ago to demonstrate the method by which a **Direct Subject** <note>original: "subjectum Rectum"; the theme in its original melodic direction.</note> is to be held interchangeably with its **Reverse Contrary** <note>original: "contrario Reverso"; the theme played in melodic inversion, where intervals that went up now go down, and vice versa.</note>; I ask you again and again to do this as soon as possible.

*Aloys.* You remind me opportunely. I will not only comply with your request, but I will even provide more of my own accord: by demonstrating that there is also another method for inverting subjects outside of the **Diatonic genre** <note>original: "genus Diatonicum"; the strict style using only the notes of the natural scale.</note>, different from the one proposed not long ago in the **Paradigm** <note>original: "Paradigma"; an earlier model or example provided in the text.</note>. I shall say, moreover, how three disparate subjects are to be combined in a composition. Likewise, to what extent it is permitted by accepted usage to depart from the common laws of counterpoint in the common style through a **figure of variation** <note>original: "figuram variationis"; decorative or melodic changes to a basic theme.</note>. I say, therefore, that to form a Reverse Contrary in the mixed genre, it is sufficient if ***mi* against *fa*** <note>original: "mi contra fa"; in the medieval hexachord system, this refers to the relationship between the notes E and F, or B and F. Here, it refers to ensuring that the semitones (half-steps) in the inversion correspond correctly to the semitones in the original melody.</note>, and conversely *fa* against *mi*, results everywhere, without having paid attention to the rule prescribed above for the diatonic genre. The matter will become clear through examples.

->By the force of the rules given above for the diatonic genre, the present<-
->theme would have to be inverted into its Reverse Contrary in the following way:<-

# Exercise V. Lesson VII. on Double Counterpoint, etc. <note>original Latin: "Exercitii V. Lectio VII. de Contrapuncto duplici &c."</note>
## 215

By thus alternating the subjects—understand, the Direct <note>The "Rectus" or direct motion is the original version of a musical theme.</note> and the Contrary <note>The "Contrarium" refers to melodic inversion, where the intervals of the theme are turned upside down.</note>—you will produce a composition not lacking in that variety so necessary for harmony. You will observe, moreover, the diligence by which, through several small figures, the opportunity is created for the Direct to proceed simultaneously with the Contrary. Sometimes composers, leaving aside the Contrary, proceed with the Direct alone nearly to the middle; and there, for the first time, they introduce the Contrary: which is left to the judgment of anyone.

*Joseph.* It does not appear that the present Fugue is based on Double Counterpoint <note>Double counterpoint is a technique where two musical lines are composed so they can be swapped (top to bottom) and still sound correct.</note>.

*Aloys.* Not at all; for I believe we have treated that matter sufficiently copiously and abundantly. In this place, the intention was merely to demonstrate how the Direct is to be combined with its Contrary. If, however, anyone desires to adorn a composition with several subjects, distinct in nature and value, this can be achieved by no other means than through the benefit of Double Counterpoint: as is to be seen in the following Example <note>original Latin: "Paradigmate"</note>:

->*Fugue built with three subjects.*<-

# Exercise V. Lesson VII. on Double Counterpoint, etc. <note>original Latin: "Exercitii V. Lectio VII. de Contrapuncto duplici &c."</note>
## 217

<note>The following musical notation illustrates a fugue with three distinct subjects. The music is written in four-voice polyphony (Soprano, Alto, Tenor, and Bass) using the diamond-shaped notes common in the 18th century. It demonstrates how these three themes can be layered together harmoniously.</note>

**[Musical Score]**

This, then, is the method of constructing a fugue with three subjects, of which the second is based on counterpoint at the octave <term>an inversion where one voice is moved up or down eight notes</term>, and the third on counterpoint at the twelfth <term>an inversion where a voice is moved by an interval of twelve notes, changing the harmonic relationships between the parts</term>. 

Here, several things should first be observed: each subject is distinguished by the value of its notes <note>meaning some subjects use long notes while others use short, fast notes</note> so that they are made easier to perceive due to their varied motion. Second, one must take care, where the subject enters at the twelfth, that the other parts do not also create a sixth between themselves; otherwise, the inversion <note>original: "translatio"</note> would not be viable <note>In counterpoint at the twelfth, a sixth inverts into a seventh, which is a dissonance that would ruin the harmony</note>. Third, you will notice that—either because of the entry of another subject or for the sake of a better melody—the complete subject is not always preserved and carried through to the end everywhere; this must be tolerated for the reason just stated. However, a composition of this kind with three subjects almost requires a fifth voice <term>a "part" or "voice" in the musical texture</term>; by this means, the alternation is made easier because every voice is not always occupied, and the voices gain a longer duration to rest.

**Joseph.** I observe and marvel at the craftsmanship. But a longer time is needed to consider it well, which I shall do at home, so as not to be a nuisance <note>original: "fastidio," implying a burden or source of boredom</note> to you at present. Meanwhile, allow me to remind you, Master, that you mentioned above the **Figure of Variation**, **irregular fugues**, and **transposed modes**; now, if it does not upset the order of our lessons, please explain what they are.

**Aloys.** I shall not delay. And first, indeed:

### On the Figure of Variation and Anticipation.

**Variation** is nothing other than **irregular diminution** <term>the process of breaking down long, stable notes into many shorter, decorative notes</term>. It is distinguished from **regular diminution** in that the latter only takes place in notes moving by the leap of a third. For example:

This is by no means permitted in strict counterpoint; however, in common composition <note>original: "compositione vulgari," referring to the freer, modern style of the era used in church or theater music</note> it is allowed. ### Exercise V. Lesson VII. on Double Counterpoint, etc. <note>original: "Exercitii V. Lectio VII. de Contrapuncto duplici &c."</note>

## 220

These few examples should suffice. There is no need today for a composer to introduce further variations: for they are already heard all too often—more than is enough—from performing musicians, even to the point of nausea. Indeed, they appear more like people gargling <note>original: "gargarizantibus"</note> than people singing.

*Joseph.* From where did these kinds of variations and aberrations from the rules of counterpoint take their origin?

*Aloys.* From what was just said, it is clear that the singers were the authors. They were not at all content with regular diminutions <term>diminution; the breaking of long notes into shorter, decorative notes</term>, and being eager to boast of the flexibility of their voices, they invented variations of this kind. If only this desire for varying had remained within the limits of modesty, so that musicians would indeed vary the music, but without changing the substance! How fortunate composers would be! But, alas! This insatiable lust and audacity for varying has proceeded so far that not only is the substance of the harmony—which was composed so carefully and with such sweat—turned completely upside down; but the composer even has difficulty recognizing his own harmony. Those musicians are even more worthy of reproach or laughter who, although endowed with no talent, are driven by that same luxury of varying. They twist their mouths into a thousand shapes—so many that even the gestures of Roscius <note>Quintus Roscius Gallus was a famous Roman comic actor known for his expressive delivery; the author is comparing bad singers to over-acting players</note> were not so varied—and in place of one vowel, five are heard at once. Indeed, this perversity has reached such an excess that they take pride in a competition to see who can carry off the palm <note>a symbol of victory</note> by rolling the notes in such a way that neither the music nor the text can be understood. But who can stand against the torrent? It is a vice of the times and of manners that all things well-established in the beginning fall into a worse state over the passage of time.

*Joseph.* Even though it is not permitted for me, still being in my apprenticeship <note>original: "tyrocinio," referring to the early stage of a student's training</note>, to judge such matters, I would still dare to affirm that the ruin of harmony in this manner has never pleased me. In the meantime, I believe I have understood well enough what variations are and how they are to be made. Now it remains to speak of transposed modes and irregular fugues.

*Aloys.* A perfect understanding of the natural modes must first precede any knowledge of transposed modes <term>transposed modes; musical scales that have been moved from their original starting pitch to a new one, often requiring the use of accidentals like flats or sharps</term>. Since I only touched upon this matter briefly above when discussing simple fugues, I shall undertake to treat it here more fully, and in as much detail as will seem sufficient.

---

->Exercise V. Lesson VII. On the Modes.<- <note>original: "Exercitii V. Lectio VII. de Modis."</note>

## On the Modes.

To attempt to treat the subject of the Modes is just like trying to bring order to ancient chaos. For such a great diversity of opinions is found among authors, both ancient and modern, that it seems there are almost as many views as there are heads. I do not hold the Greek authors in such great awe; for it is beyond controversy that their music was quite poor in intervals at its beginning, as Plato testifies in the *Timaeus*. Therefore, it is no wonder that as time passed and more types of tetrachords <term>tetrachord; a scale of four notes spanning a perfect fourth, the building block of ancient Greek scales</term> and octaves were acquired, the number of Modes also increased. 

Although the Modes of the Greeks took their names and number not so much from the types of octaves as from the diversity, character, and style of various peoples: such as the *Dorian*, *Hypodorian*, *Phrygian*, *Lydian*, etc. With these names, they indicated the different character and style of nations, which each people used according to their ancestral custom; just as today we say: *Italian Air*, *French*, *English*, and so on. Since, however, scarcely a shadow of Greek music remains to us anymore, I cannot wonder enough that there are still some who dare to assign these foreign words to the Modes of our modern music, and to obscure a matter already intricate enough with empty names. For our part, leaving aside what is now obsolete or belongs more to the history of music, we must discuss the opinion of the moderns—specifically those who establish twelve Modes: six Authentic <term>authentic; a mode where the melody primarily moves between the final note and its octave above</term> or Principal, and six Plagal <term>plagal; a mode where the melody moves roughly a fourth below and a fifth above the final note</term> or Collateral.

*Joseph.* Do all modern professors hold this opinion of twelve Modes?

*Aloysius.* Not at all. For some are found who, perhaps led by Choral Chant <note>original: "Cantu chorali"; referring to the traditional eight modes of Gregorian chant</note>, see eight; others see more or fewer Modes.

How much their opinions are to be valued will become clear from what is about to be said, and from our own opinion to be added shortly.

At this point, we must call to mind what we asserted above regarding simple fugues: namely, that six different types of octaves are found according to the various placement of the semitone, and consequently, there are just as many principal Modes: namely D, E, F, G, A, and C. <note>Note that 'B' is excluded here because it does not allow for a perfect fifth above the starting note.</note>

<sig>K k k</sig>
<meta>catchword: qua-</meta>

<summary>Aloysius begins a discussion on the musical modes, dismissing the confusing use of ancient Greek terminology in modern music and narrowing the focus to the twelve-mode system used by modern theorists.</summary>
<keywords>modes, Dorian, Phrygian, Lydian, tetrachords, authentic modes, plagal modes, choral chant, octave species, music theory history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 221, where the author dismissed ancient Greek terminology in favor of a practical, octave-based approach to the musical modes. Here, the focus shifts to the specific "species" of the octave and how the placement of semitones defines the six principal modes.</meta>

->Exercise V. Lesson VII. On the Modes.<- <note>original: "Exercitii V. Lectio VII. de Modis."</note>

...although the Diagram [of these modes] has already been presented in the cited place, since the order [of this lesson] requires it, I think it is by no means superfluous to repeat it again.

<note>This diagram illustrates the six species of the octave, which form the basis of the six authentic modes. Each scale starts on a different note, changing the position of the naturally occurring semitones between E-F and B-C.</note>

1.  *re mi fa sol re mi fa sol.* <note>The First Mode, starting on D.</note>
2.  *mi fa sol re mi fa sol la.* <note>The Second Mode, starting on E.</note>
3.  *fa sol re mi fa re mi fa.* <note>The Third Mode, starting on F.</note>
4.  *ut re mi fa re mi fa sol.* <note>The Fourth Mode, starting on G.</note>
5.  *re mi fa re mi fa sol la.* <note>The Fifth Mode, starting on A.</note>
6.  *ut re mi fa sol re mi fa.* <note>The Sixth Mode, starting on C.</note>

Therefore, so many species of different Octaves are found; because the Semitones <term>semitone; the smallest interval in this musical system, occurring naturally between the syllables 'mi' and 'fa'</term> are found in different places that many times when beginning from any first note of the System <term>System; the 'systema' or gamut, the total range of organized pitches available to the composer</term>. Consequently, just as many principal Modes, and no more, must necessarily be established. For any other systems of Octaves that can be imagined must be reduced to one of these Species, as transposed Modes.

### *Transposed Modes pertaining to the first Mode, D.*

<note>Transposition allows a mode to be moved to a higher or lower pitch level using sharps or flats while maintaining the same pattern of whole steps and half steps.</note>

1.  *re mi fa sol re mi fa sol.*
2.  *re mi fa sol re mi fa sol.*

These, therefore, are the transposed Modes of the first Mode on D, because their Semitones <term>semitone; the smallest interval in the musical system, which defines the "flavor" or species of a mode</term> coincide in exactly the same places as those; consequently, they also preserve the same solmization <term>solmization; the practice of assigning syllables (ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la) to notes to help a singer navigate intervals</term>. They differ indeed in their position [on the staff], but they do not differ at all in their species <note>species refers to the specific arrangement of whole tones and semitones within the octave</note>.

->Exercise V. Lesson VII. on the Modes.<- <note>original: "Exercitii V. Lectio VII. de Modis."</note>

And if any others can be produced with the help of a flat or a sharp <note>original: "adminiculo b., vel #"</note>, they are little used.

<meta>This page transitions from the practical application of solfège and transposition (Exercise V) into a theoretical discussion regarding the total number of musical modes. <page-num>226</page-num>
->Exercise V. Lesson VII. on the Modes.<- <note>original: "Exercitii V. Lectio VII. de Modis."</note>

<image-desc>A musical staff with a C-clef (alto) and a flat on the middle line. Eight diamond-shaped notes are printed in an ascending pattern. Beneath the staff are the solfège syllables: ut, re, mi, fa, sol, re, mi, fa.</image-desc>
->*ut* *re* *mi* *fa* *sol* *re* *mi* *fa*<-

*Joseph.* From what has been said and demonstrated so far, I conclude that there are only six natural Modes. <note>original: "Modos naturales." These are the modes in their primary, untransposed positions.</note>

*Aloysius.* Hold your judgment for a little while, Joseph, and recall to your memory what was said in Book One regarding the division of the Octave: namely, that it can be divided in a twofold way, **harmonically** and **arithmetically**. Through **harmonic division**, the Fifth is placed in the lower part <note>original: "parte gravi," the "heavy" or deep side</note> and the Fourth in the upper part <note>original: "parte acuta," the "sharp" or high side</note>. Through **arithmetic division**, the Fourth is obtained in the lower part and the Fifth in the upper part. Because the reason why this twofold division should be established was explained in Book 1, I will refrain from repeating it here.

Now, from this twofold division of the Octave, two systems of octaves differing in species arise; therefore, two different species of Modes are also established. One is produced by harmonic division, which is called **Authentic**, and the other by arithmetic division, which is called **Plagal**. From this it is inferred that because twelve systems differing in the species of octaves arise, just as many Modes are to be established. <note>Aloysius is arguing for the 12-mode system—six authentic and six plagal—rather than the older 8-mode system or Joseph's initial suggestion of six.</note>

## Octaves harmonically divided, and the Modes arising from them. <note>original: "Octavæ harmonicè diviſæ, & Modi inde oriundi."</note>

<image-desc>Six short segments of musical staves, labeled 1. through 6. with Arabic numerals. Each staff segment contains four diamond-shaped notes illustrating different harmonic divisions of the octave.</image-desc>

There is no doubt that these systems are produced from a harmonic division. However, whether the following systems—presented by Gioseffo Zarlino <note>A famous 16th-century Italian music theorist who influentialized the 12-mode system.</note> and most others for the formation of Plagal Modes—are generated from arithmetic division, is a matter of great controversy.

<image-desc>Six short segments of musical staves, labeled 1. through 6. with Arabic numerals. Each staff contains four diamond-shaped notes. Beneath each segment is a letter label: C., D., E., F., G., and A. respectively.</image-desc>

Since Zarlino himself, in Part 4, Chapter 9, *On the Modes*, posits the following system starting from the Octave of C.

<keywords>harmonic division, arithmetic division, authentic modes, plagal modes, Gioseffo Zarlino, music theory, octave systems, twelve modes</keywords>

### Exercise V. Lesson VII. on the Modes.

<image-desc>A small musical staff with a C-clef on the first line, displaying three vertically aligned notes (G3, B3, D4).</image-desc>
Where the lowest note <note>original: "chorda gravissima," literally the heaviest string</note> remains; which is the true product of arithmetic division. If, therefore, the **Plagal Modes** are generated from arithmetic division, as indeed they are, their formation must be expressed by the following systems:

<image-desc>Five short musical staves labeled above with C., D., E., G., and A. Each staff shows two notes representing an octave divided arithmetically (with the smaller interval, the Fourth, on the bottom).</image-desc>

*Joseph.* In this way, only five **Plagal Modes** would exist.

*Aloysius.* Exactly so. Since the **Octave F-f** cannot be divided arithmetically because of the **Tritone** <term>Tritone: An interval of three whole tones (an augmented fourth) that was traditionally avoided in strict counterpoint due to its dissonant, "unstable" sound.</term> arising therefrom; as I have already said elsewhere.

*Joseph.* What then should be embraced in such a great confusion of the matter?

*Aloysius.* I shall tell you, after we have examined the use and the difference of the **Modes**, both **Authentic** and **Plagal**.

It must be presupposed, according to **Zarlino’s Opinion** <note>Gioseffo Zarlino (1517–1590) was an influential music theorist whose writings defined the laws of Renaissance counterpoint.</note>, that the beginning, the end, and the cadences <note>original: "clausulas," the formal closing patterns of a musical phrase</note> shall be common to both **Modes**: so that there is no difference, except that the melody <note>original: "modulatio"</note> of the **Authentic Mode** must have its existence in the high range <note>original: "in acuto"</note>, while the **Plagal** must have it in the low range <note>original: "in gravi"</note>. Nor does the **Author** require here that the subjects of the **Authentic Mode** always proceed by ascending, and the **Plagal** on the contrary by descending: which his **Examples** <note>original: "Paradigmata"</note> demonstrate.

<image-desc>Two systems of musical notation. The first system is a pair of staves labeled "Authenticus." with a melodic line in the upper staff and foundational notes in the lower staff. The second system is a pair of staves labeled "Plagalis." showing a similar structure shifted lower in range.</image-desc>

<page-num>228</page-num>
# Exercise V. Lesson VII. on the Modes.

From these examples it is clear that there is no other distinction between these two Modes, except that the **Plagal** melody is lower <note>original: "gravior," literally heavier or deeper in pitch</note>, and its subject <note>In this context, the "subject" refers to the primary melodic theme or musical idea</note> is terminated in the collateral interval, namely **E** and **B-natural** <term>B-natural: Represented in the text by the square symbol "♮" (B-quadratum), used to distinguish it from B-flat.</term>. Giovanni Maria Bononcini <note>An Italian composer and theorist (1642–1678) known for his work "Musico prattico."</note> maintains that, besides the lower melody and the resulting change of clefs to suit the nature of the **Plagal Mode**, the subjects ought to descend to the Fourth.

Angelo Berardi <note>A significant music theorist (c. 1636–1694) who wrote extensively on counterpoint and the modes.</note>, an author otherwise not to be disregarded, seems to distinguish the **Authentic Mode** from the **Plagal** by name alone: without regard for subjects ascending or descending, or for melodies being high or low. Indeed, he establishes a lower melody in the **Authentic Mode** than in the **Plagal**, as his examples show.

<image-desc>A series of musical examples on five-line staves using white mensural notation. The first line is labeled "*Authentic.*" Below, four musical staves display melodic sequences using different clefs to demonstrate pitch range, each ending with an "etc." mark.</image-desc>

Who, being skilled in music and knowing the opinions of other authors, would attribute this last example <note>original: "Paradigma"</note> to the **Plagal Mode**? Since the nature of the subject and the melody seem more suited to the formation of the **Authentic Mode**. Even more surprising is the following composition <note>original: "concentus," referring to a harmonized piece or a musical "concord"</note> brought forward by the same author as an example of the **Third Mode** (that is, the range from **E** to **e**); I will place it here in its entirety so that you may wonder at it more.

<image-desc>In the bottom right corner, a small musical fragment acts as a catchword, showing two diamond-shaped notes on a staff to indicate the continuation on the next page.</image-desc>

He highlights that Plagal modes are typically characterized by a lower range and specific interval terminations.</summary>

The musical notation is organized into three distinct systems, each consisting of four staves. *   **Diamond-shaped notes:** These represent **semibreves** and **minims** <term>Semibreve and Minim: Rhythmic values in early music, roughly corresponding to the modern whole note and half note.</term>.
*   **Clef variations:** The staves utilize various **C-clefs** and **F-clefs** <term>Clefs: Symbols placed at the beginning of a staff to designate the pitch of the lines; varying these allowed composers to keep different vocal ranges (soprano, alto, tenor, bass) within the five-line staff.</term>. This illustrates the different pitch ranges (high or low) that distinguish the Authentic and Plagal modes.
*   **Phrasing:** The notation includes **ligatures** <note>original: "ligaturae," which are marks connecting notes to be sung to a single syllable or played as a single melodic unit</note>, bar lines, and rests to structure the exercises.

# Exercise V. Lesson VII. on the Modes.

<image-desc>A four-voice musical example in white mensural notation. The system contains four staves. The top three staves use C-clefs (C1, C2, and C3) and the bottom staff uses an F-clef (F4). The music shows a progression of whole notes (semibreves) and half notes (minims) across four measures, ending with a cadence.</image-desc>

Do you see how neither the entering Tenor <term>Tenor: In this period, the "Tenor" was often the structural voice that determined the mode of the piece.</term> nor the final cadence <term>clausula: A musical conclusion or resting point.</term> corresponds to this proposed arrangement? So much so that nothing prevents this harmony from being assigned to A, or the Hyperphrygian <term>Hyperphrygian: A specific musical mode based on the pitch A.</term> as its own proper mode. I could bring forward many examples from Palestrina <note>original: "Aloysio Prenestino," the Latinized name for the Renaissance composer Giovanni Pierluigi da Palestrina.</note> which demonstrate that the matter of the Modes was not of great concern even to this most excellent man. Having <margin>turned</margin> <note>original: "voluta," likely a marginal correction for the word "voluntas" (will), changing the meaning to "having turned [this] over in my mind."</note> this matter over in my mind, I would almost believe—were it not for the weight of authority—that even these famous authors did not know for certain what to decide about the Modes; or if a distinction between the Authentic and Plagal <term>Authentic and Plagal: Historically, "Authentic" modes were those that stayed above the final note, while "Plagal" modes moved both above and below it.</term> modes exists, they certainly considered it of such little importance that they did not think it worth the effort to worry about it. In the past, when hearing was not yet so refined, it could perhaps be contained within these narrow constraints. But in this age of ours, in which the insatiable hunger of our ears hardly allows itself to be limited by rational boundaries, it is easy to judge what <insert>reason</insert> we should have today for this opinion, which restricts the breadth of music.

**Joseph.** What advice then should I follow in such a great discrepancy among authors and such ambiguity of the matter?

**Aloys.** I will tell you, Joseph, not so much by way of a strict rule as by way of friendly advice. Setting aside the Plagal modes, use the six Modes D, E, F, G, A, and C, along with their transpositions, in the way you were taught above concerning simple Fugues <term>Fugue: A composition where a melody is introduced in one part and then taken up by others in imitation.</term> and as you will still be taught: wandering far and wide through the proper intervals and those friendly to the Mode you are working in. Convince yourself that a composition worked out according to the rules of harmony, yet free and filled with charm, is "authentic" enough, while one void of these qualities is all too "plagal." <note>The teacher is making a play on words: "authentic" here means genuine and good, while "plagal" is used to mean inferior or lacking.</note>

<meta>catchword: Joseph.</meta>

He advises the student to focus on harmonic beauty and the six primary modes rather than rigid modal categories.</summary>

<header>Exercise V. Lesson VII. On various Fugue subjects.</header>

**Joseph.** Perhaps in composition for <term>Choral Song: Plainchant or Gregorian chant, as opposed to polyphonic music.</term> there will be a need for the use of <term>Plagal Modes: Musical modes that sit a fourth lower than their 'Authentic' counterparts, sharing the same final note but having a different range.</term>?

**Aloysius.** Not at all. For it is sufficient to take account of the intonations and the <term>Ecclesiastical Modes: The system of modes used by the Church, which you should learn from the choir singers.</term> (whose knowledge you should seek from the chant singers) and, in your composing, follow the advice given a little while ago without the restriction of the Plagal Mode, and you will complete a work that is not to be regretted.

**Joseph.** Since I have understood from you that you only concern yourself with six <term>figural modes: Modes used in polyphonic or 'figured' music, rather than plainchant.</term> it would be useful to know what order you maintain, and which you establish as the first, second, third, and so on?

**Aloysius.** <note>Gioseffo Zarlino (1517–1590) was an influential Italian music theorist who renumbered the modes to begin on C.</note> Gioseffo Zarlino and most others of that era held C, or the <term>Dorian: Traditionally the mode starting on D, but Zarlino reclassified the mode starting on C as the first mode.</term> mode, as the first. Later practical musicians almost all gave the primary place to D, which was formerly called <term>Phrygian: Traditionally the mode starting on E; here Fux notes the shifting terminology of his predecessors.</term>. For us, it is enough to have demonstrated that there exist only six modes that differ in their species; it matters very little what order one maintains. But if you prefer, Joseph, to follow the customary habit, I will not hinder you from calling them by their commonly accepted names.

### On the various Subjects of Fugues.

Not long ago, mention was made of <term>Fugues: A compositional technique where a short melody (the subject) is introduced by one part and then taken up by others.</term> or irregular subjects; however, I want this "irregularity" to be understood in respect to subjects where the <term>consequent: The second voice to enter, which mimics the first.</term> part responds correctly to the starting part without the aid of a natural <note>original: "♮"</note> or a flat <note>original: "♭"</note>, as is the case in subjects adapted to the <term>Diatonic Genus: Music using the standard seven-note scale without chromatic alterations.</term>. Otherwise, setting aside the rigor of the Diatonic Genus, if such subjects are established according to the logic of the Mode, they can nonetheless have their own regularity. It now falls to you, Joseph, to propose various subjects of your own invention, different from those we have used until now.

**Joseph.** I will do so without distinction, as they come to mind.

<image-desc>A single musical staff with five lines. It features an alto clef (C-clef on the third line) and a cut-circle time signature. The musical sequence consists of a half note on middle C, followed by four quarter notes: G, A, B-flat, and A, ending with a quarter note on G.</image-desc>

**Aloysius.** In what manner will you form the responding part?

**Joseph.** In my humble judgment, I think it can be done in two ways. One: where the last note of the responding part ends in a way that is appropriate to the Mode. The other: by following <term>Solmization: A system of attributing a distinct syllable to each note in a scale, similar to modern 'do-re-mi'.</term>. For example:

# Exercise V. Lesson VII. On various Fugue subjects.

->First way. Second way.<-

<image-desc>A single five-line musical staff with a C-clef on the fourth line (tenor clef). It displays two short musical phrases separated by a double bar line. The first phrase, labeled "First way.", consists of six notes: G3, A3, B3, C4, D4, E4. The second phrase, labeled "Second way.", consists of six notes: G3, A3, B-flat 3, C4, D4, E4.</image-desc>

*Aloysius.* Both ways are legitimate, though for different reasons. The first is correct because its last note falls rightly upon the <term>string: In this context, 'chorda' refers to a specific pitch or degree of the scale.</term> of the <term>Mode: The scale or harmonic system governing the piece.</term>. The second is correct because its last note follows the logic of <term>Solmization: The traditional system of assigning syllables like ut, re, mi to notes to understand their melodic function.</term>.

*Joseph.* But which seems more correct to you?

*Aloysius.* The second <term>Way: Or 'method' of answering a fugue subject.</term> of responding is not to be rejected for two reasons. First, because it better serves the requirements of singing. Second, because it does not deviate so much from the character <note>original: "tenore"</note> of the <term>Subject: The primary melodic theme that is imitated throughout a fugue.</term>. Let it be a firm rule for you to refrain from breaking the <term>Subject</term> as much as possible, unless a serious reason demands it; for the requirements of the <term>Song: Referring here to 'Cantus', the melodic integrity of the vocal line.</term> are more important than those of the <term>Mode</term>. This is because a deficiency in the mode can easily be compensated for by harmonizing with the other part, and by holding a note belonging to the mode while the other part moves outside the scale-degree of the mode. For example:

<image-desc>Two five-line musical staves grouped together showing a two-voice counterpoint example. The upper staff uses an alto clef (C-clef on the third line) and the lower staff uses a tenor clef (C-clef on the fourth line). The notation uses open white notes (diamonds and rectangles). Both staves conclude with "etc."</image-desc>

->Let the same judgment apply to the following <term>Subject</term>. For example:<-

<image-desc>Two musical staves showing a two-part counterpoint exercise. The upper staff includes multiple accidental marks, specifically flats and sharps, to indicate chromaticism or specific modal adjustments. The lower staff provides the accompaniment. The notation consists of diamond-shaped notes on five-line staves.</image-desc>

These two subjects can be called regular <gloss>and</gloss> irregular from different perspectives. Irregular, because they are not placed in their own proper <term>Mode</term>, and therefore that <term>Semitone: The smallest interval in traditional Western music, such as the step between B and C.</term> existing in the upper part in both cases had to be produced by the benefit of the <term>b-flat: Known as 'b mollis' or 'soft b', this is the origin of the modern flat symbol.</term>. They can be called regular with respect to the mixed style <note>original: "generis mixti"</note> which is most commonly used today.

# Exercise V. Lesson VII. On various Fugue Subjects.

*Joseph.* What is it that you say—that the <term>Subjects: The primary melodic themes of a fugue.</term> are not placed in their proper <term>Mode: The precursor to the modern 'key' system, based on specific arrangements of whole and half steps.</term>?

*Aloysius.* If I show you a Mode in which that <term>semitone: The smallest interval in Western music, like the distance between B and C.</term> can be had without a <term>B-flat: <note>original: "b. molli"</note> In early music notation, a 'soft B' or B-flat was often added to avoid harsh intervals.</term>, will it not be an evident sign that those Subjects belong there?

*Joseph.* So it indeed seems to me. But what kind of Mode is it?

*Aloysius.* The Mode of A. E. G. <note>Aloysius refers to the Aeolian mode, which is centered on the note A.</note>

<image-desc>Musical notation for two voices in mensural style. The top staff has a C-clef on the first line, starting on A; the bottom staff has a C-clef on the fourth line, starting on E. The example is marked with the number "1." and both staves end with "&c."</image-desc>

<image-desc>Musical notation for two voices, marked with the number "2." The top staff has a C-clef on the first line; the bottom staff has a C-clef on the fourth line. Both staves end with "&c."</image-desc>

*Joseph.* The matter is clearly worthy of observation.

*Aloysius.* However, a <term>B-flat</term> placed at the beginning, and outside the song <note>meaning in the key signature at the start of the staff rather than as an accidental within the melody</note>, will be an indication of a Mode transposed to the <term>fourth above: <note>original: "Quartam altam"</note> Moving a piece of music up by four scale degrees, which usually requires adding a flat to keep the intervals the same.</term>; in which case the Subjects are nonetheless to be called regular and congruent with their Mode. For example: <note>original: "E. G." stands for exempli gratia, meaning 'for example.'</note>

<image-desc>Two short musical subjects on a single staff with a C-clef on the first line. The first subject, marked "1.", has a B-flat in the key signature. The second subject, marked "2.", begins after a double bar line.</image-desc>

But in these modern times, in which the <term>mixed genre: <note>original: "Genus mixtum"</note> A term used by Fux to describe the transition from strict Renaissance modality to the emerging major/minor tonal system.</term> has prevailed with great force, these subtleties—although necessary for a genuine understanding of the Modes—are not much attended to. Proceed now to propose other Subjects.

*Joseph.* Seeing the sign of error placed on the third note,

# Exercise V. Lesson VII. On various Fugue Subjects.

...I find that I hesitated in the response not without reason. What was the cause of the error?

*Aloysius.* The interval between the first and third **Note** of the **Subject**, which consists of a **Seventh**; for the intervening note is not of such strength that it could cover the impropriety of that leap. In subjects of this kind, therefore, where a leap of a <term>Fourth: An interval spanning four scale degrees (e.g., C to F).</term> occurs between the first and second note, the third note must be very carefully watched. Whatever interval separates it from the first note of the **Subject** in the part proposing the theme, it should be separated by that same interval in the responding part. Now, in the starting part, a leap of a <term>Sixth: An interval spanning six scale degrees (e.g., C to A).</term> is found between the said notes; therefore, the same interval must be given to the responding part. **For example:**

<image-desc>Two musical staves showing a fugal subject and its answer. The top staff, using a C-clef, presents the subject starting with an upward leap of a fourth, followed by a scalar ascent and a further leap. The bottom staff shows the respondent voice entering two measures later, imitating the subject at a different pitch level. Both staves conclude with the notation "etc."</image-desc>

*Joseph.* It seems that both the subject and the <term>melody: <note>original: "modulatio"</note> The melodic progression or flow of the musical line.</term> go beyond the limits of the <term>Mode: The specific scale or tonal center governing the piece.</term> in this manner.

*Aloysius.* Not at all. For since in the proposing part the subject does not end on the <term>Octave: The interval between one musical pitch and another with double its frequency.</term> of the Mode, how could the responding part end on the <term>Fifth: The five-note interval that serves as the most stable point in a mode after the octave.</term> of that same Mode? Furthermore, are not all the intervals of the subject and the melody contained within the Octave of the Mode by natural reason? But if, by introducing a <term>sharp: <note>original: "Diesi"</note> A symbol indicating a note is raised by a semitone.</term>, it is turned into a transposed Mode, the response will have to be established a little differently. **For example:**

<image-desc>A second musical example with two staves, similar to the first but incorporating sharps into the melody. The top staff presents the subject, and the bottom staff displays the answer starting after a short rest. Both lines end with "etc."</image-desc>

Thus sometimes, according to the nature and difference of the Mode, one and the same subject must be formed in different ways regarding the responding part. Come, please propose another subject.

<meta>catchword: In followed by a small musical staff fragment</meta>

<summary>This page explains how the intervals in a fugal "answer" must be adjusted to maintain the melodic character of the "subject," particularly concerning leaps of a fourth and sixth. Aloysius demonstrates how transposition and the use of sharps (diesis) affect how a second voice imitates the first.</summary>

### Exercise V. Lesson VII. On various Fugue Subjects

<image-desc>Two systems of musical notation, each with two staves (G-clef and F-clef). The music is set in 6/4 time. **Aloysius.** These subjects are suited to the <note>original: "incontinentiæ"</note> unrestraint of the modern style. But beware, Joseph, of using similar subjects—extended to such immense length and breadth—when writing a <term>four-part composition: <note>original: "Quatricinio"</note> A piece written for four voices or instruments.</term>. For it can hardly be managed with such extravagant and abstruse subjects without one part obstructing another. In the meantime, the <term>answer: <note>original: "responsum"</note> The second entry of a fugue theme, usually starting on a different pitch.</term>, as far as the subject allows, has been quite appropriately established. This is because in place of the <term>leap of a fifth: <note>original: "saltûs in Quintam"</note> An interval spanning five notes of the scale.</term> present in the first part, you used a <term>leap of a fourth: <note>original: "saltum in Quartam"</note> An interval spanning four notes.</term>; and in place of a progression of a <term>second: <note>original: "Secundam"</note> Movement to the very next note in the scale.</term>, you have sometimes assigned a leap of a <term>third: <note>original: "Tertiam"</note> An interval spanning three notes.</term> to the answering part in certain places, according to the nature of the <term>Mode: <note>original: "Modi"</note> The specific scale or tonal framework of the piece.</term>. Because of this, I trust that you will not hesitate much in finding answers for any kind of subjects, provided you pay attention to what has been said so far. However, much depends on the choice of subjects. For not all subjects, without distinction, can be developed equally well; this must be weighed beforehand with careful selection and judgment.

It was mentioned to you not long ago, Joseph—now that you have emerged from the troublesome <note>original: "palæstra"</note> *wrestling-school* <note>The author uses the metaphor of an ancient Greek gymnasium to describe the rigorous, "sweaty" work of learning counterpoint.</note> of these lessons—that you have the power to use every kind of time and proportion. Now the matter requires that I demonstrate to you by what method notes of different shape and value are made to be of the same value under a different measure of the <term>beat: <note>original: "tactûs"</note> The "pulse" or steady hand-motion used to keep time in music.</term>.

### Binary Time.

<image-desc>Six staves of musical notation in mensural style using diamond-shaped notes. The staves are grouped in pairs. The first pair has a 'C' time signature (common time). *Joseph.* I see this unequal equality of the values of the figures very clearly; but for what purpose do you bring this up?

Under the time, *Slowly* <note>original: "Adagio"</note>, in every quarter. Let the examples be seen.

<image-desc>Two musical staves with the label "Binary Time" centered between them, illustrating a simple rhythmic example in common time.</image-desc>

One must take care that the <term>ligatures <note>original: "Ligaturæ"; musical ties used to create suspensions or syncopations</note></term> established in the style of the last example are not performed in too hurried a measure; if this were done, they would never have the hoped-for effect.

Since I believe nothing has been overlooked, but that you have been taught everything by which means any thoughts, Ideas, and Concepts formed in the mind can be committed to paper according to the rules of the art, it remains to speak of the choice by which, in embracing one Idea over another, we obtain the praise of good taste.

## On Taste.

<margin>to each</margin>
Hardly any conversation is heard more frequently than that concerning Taste. From this, we hear the Italian saying: *He has good taste* <note>original: "Egli è di buon gusto"</note>. The Frenchman: *He has perfectly good taste* <note>original: "Il a le goût parfaitement bon"</note>. The Latin: *He is a man of exquisite taste* <note>original: "Est homo exquisiti gustûs"</note>. Would that there were the same ease in defining Taste as there is in affirming it. Although Taste is properly attributed only to the palate, in a broader sense, it can apply to anything in which there is a right and select harmony of order. Leaving other matters to others, for our purpose Taste must be restricted to Music, and must be established as twofold: *Active* and *Passive*. *Active* is that which the Musician possesses in singing or playing, about which

### Exercise V. Lesson VII. On Taste.

...was discussed extensively above. The passive [taste] is that which the listener perceives from the <term>melody <note>original: "modulamine"; the melodic flow or arrangement of notes</note></term>. But whom shall we appoint as judge of good and depraved Taste? Since, according to the well-worn proverb:
> My things delight me, yours delight you, and everyone’s own delight them. <note>original: "Me mea delectant, te tua, quemque sua."</note>

Likewise: 
> There is no disputing about tastes, and no one should be a judge in his own case. <note>original: "de Gustibus non est disputandum, nemo sit judex in propria causa."</note>

From this it follows that a rustic fellow is more delighted by his <term>liripipium <note>original: "Liripipium"; usually refers to the long tail of a scholar's hood, used here metaphorically for something simple, familiar, or perhaps a crude folk instrument</note></term> than by any artistic musical harmony. By the same token, Monsieur Bellegarde <note>Jean-Baptiste Morvan de Bellegarde (1648–1734), a French author who wrote extensively on manners and taste</note> relates: there existed a man of no mean birth who, offended by the song of nightingales, was on the contrary wonderfully delighted by the croaking of frogs. He had a house built for himself by a far-hidden pond, surrounded by no trees, so that day and night he might be granted an abundance of such exquisite music (in his judgment); conversely, due to the lack of trees, there would be no place for nightingales to linger and weary him with their song. What do you think, **Joseph**, of the "sublimity" of such Taste?

**Joseph.** I think it could not unfairly be called into judgment.

**Aloys.** Listen further to one or two examples of both rashness and stupid judgment, and the punishment that followed. Marsyas, the proud <term>pipe-player <note>original: "Tibicen"</note></term>, dared to challenge Apollo to a contest out of too much self-esteem, and paid the penalty for his rashness by having his skin stripped off. To King Midas of Phrygia, because he had awarded the first prize to the god Pan when he competed in song against Apollo, his ears were stretched into the length and shape of a donkey's. These are the fabled inventions of poets. But if the punishment for such a crime were as certain in our own age, truly, how many rash and depraved judges of Taste would we see walking about without their skin, or likewise with donkey ears! I do not deny, according to that saying—
> My things delight me —

that everyone has free judgment to find pleasure in whatever they wish: provided they do not take on the role of a judge. For that belongs only to the intelligent person, who knows how to distinguish common and trite things from higher and more sublime ones, while the order of things and nature remains. Hear **Cicero** speaking on this matter: 
> "Others," he says, "when they read good speeches or poems, approve of the orators and poets, but they do not understand why they are moved to approve; they cannot know where it is, what it is, or how that which delights them most was made." 

There are also found some modern composers who, under the guise of "Taste" and novelty, depart from the customary use of <term>consonances <note>original: "Consonantia"; intervals that sound stable and pleasing</note></term> and <term>dissonances <note>original: "Dissonantia"; intervals that create tension</note></term>, inverting all the rights and laws of the Art, and attempting to *create* (which is for Almighty God alone)

<summary>This page discusses the subjective nature of "Taste" in music, warning that while everyone is free to enjoy what they like, only those with true understanding should act as judges. It uses classical myths (Marsyas and Midas) and citations from Cicero to argue against modern composers who ignore traditional musical rules in favor of novelty.</summary>

<keywords>musical taste, subjectivity, Marsyas, Apollo, King Midas, Cicero, modern composers, musical judgment, harmony, dissonance</keywords>

### Exercise V. Lesson VII. On Taste.

...of divine <note>original: "Dei )"; likely completing a thought from the previous page regarding "divine ideas"</note> ideas] they flatter themselves to possess; while in the meantime, the novelty of our limited intellect is nothing other than the arrangement of the same <term>Consonances<note>Combinations of notes that sound stable and pleasant on their own</note></term> and <term>Dissonances<note>Combinations of notes that sound tense or "clashing" and require resolution</note></term> in various ways according to right reason. I say, therefore, that a composition rightly claims the privilege of good Taste if it is built upon <term>Rules<note>original: "Præceptis"; the fundamental laws of counterpoint and harmony</note></term>, avoids trivialities and extravagant eccentricity, strives for higher things, yet proceeds with natural logic, and possesses the power to provide delight even to those skilled in the art.

**Joseph.** How many varied and difficult things this definition of yours requires!

**Aloysius.** I do not deny it, Joseph; to achieve this in all respects is not for <term>beginners<note>original: "tyronum"; students or novices in the early stages of training</note></term>. For them, it is not so easy to find even common starting points at first; nor can one arrive at the higher levels unless the common techniques are first ready at hand. For you, Joseph, who have given such splendid proof of your progress so far, it might perhaps be possible; therefore, I will begin to explain the definition piece by piece ahead of schedule.

I said first, that *Composition ought to be built upon Rules*. For a lawless composition, though it may otherwise enjoy an uncommon idea and be fit to tickle the ears of the unlearned, will not quite satisfy the delicate Taste of those skilled in the art, who desire regularity in addition to an exquisite idea. It was further stated and advised that the composer should not adopt lowly and ordinary concepts, which would bring about disgust through their triviality instead of delight; rather, looking toward sublimity, he should strive for novelty. On the other hand, let him not be seduced by the pursuit of novelty into conceiving ideas that exceed nature and the order of things—passages excessively difficult to sing or play—with which neither the performing musicians nor the listeners would be satisfied. Not the musicians, because of the difficulty of performance; nor the listeners, because compositions of this kind, exceeding natural reason, though they may stick in the ears, never penetrate as far as the soul.

**Joseph.** You demand something of no small importance, Master: by forbidding the commonplace, and commanding the sublime and unusual—while requiring them to be natural.

**Aloysius.** Exactly so. For to present something easy and natural to the ears, yet not commonplace, *is not so easy*; and thus it has passed into a common proverb: *The easy is difficult.* <note>original: "Facile difficile est." This refers to the artistic paradox where the most natural-sounding music often requires the greatest skill to compose.</note> Indeed, the excellence and seasoning of good Taste rest upon this "difficult ease." Nor [is it the same] to find something new and unusual...

<sig>P p p</sig>
<meta>coming</meta>

<summary>Aloysius defines "Good Taste" as a balance between following musical rules and achieving a natural yet sublime novelty. He introduces the paradox "Facile difficile est," explaining that making music sound effortless and natural is the hardest task for a composer.</summary>
<keywords>Good Taste, musical rules, novelty, consonance, dissonance, performance difficulty, natural logic, Joseph, Aloysius</keywords>

--- Page 256 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the master Aloysius and his student Joseph. Aloysius is concluding his definition of "Good Taste" in music, arguing that while talent is necessary, it must be governed by the laws of nature and musical order. They then pivot to discuss the most important style of composition: the Church style.</meta>

### Exercise V. Lesson VII. On the Ecclesiastical Style.

...he will struggle with the difficulty of arriving [at his goal], who, through a desire for novelty alone, inverts the order of things and of nature. But how shall he reach the goal of good Taste by this path? I will not deny that a very large part of good Taste depends on the genius and talent of each individual Composer; however, such influences, though not unpleasing in themselves, must be contained within the limits of nature, order, and the laws [of music], so that they may deservedly achieve the standard of Right Taste.

*Joseph.* What if the corruption of the times and the custom of perverted Taste have so prevailed that a Composer cannot go against the current, as it were, without detriment to his fame and profit?

*Aloysius.* He who is blessed by fortune with a patron <note>original: "hero"; literally 'hero' or 'master', referring to a noble benefactor who supports the artist</note> who delights more in the grain than in the chaff <note>original: "grano magìs, quàm ſpicâ"; a metaphor for preferring the substance of the music over its superficial decorations</note> has no cause for concern, nor reason to depart from the path of right reason. Otherwise: let him be content with his own virtue, and be strengthened by the teaching of Seneca, that wisest of men, who discourses on Providence in his own manner—which is almost Christian <note>original: "more ſuo tantùm, non Chriſtiano"; Seneca was a Stoic philosopher whose ethics were often admired by later Christian scholars</note>—as follows: "Just as so many rivers, so many showers of rain falling from above, and such a great force of medicinal springs do not change the saltiness of the sea, nor even dilute it: so the onset of adverse circumstances does not turn the mind of a brave man. He remains in his state, and whatever happens, he draws it into his own color." I am not unaware that these words pertain to the moral life; yet there is a particular virtue in one who preserves the sciences and arts without blemish.

Since, however, different types of Composition and style also require different Taste: we must first deal with the diversity of style, because all our labor is directed chiefly toward that end.

---

## On the Ecclesiastical Style.

Just as sacred things excel the profane in dignity, so too do I believe no one will doubt that Music—devoted to Divine worship and destined to endure eternally—holds by far the most noble and principal place; therefore, the chief effort must be bestowed upon it. And because God is the highest perfection, it is fitting that the <term>Harmony<note>original: "Concentum"; referring to the overall musical setting or the "concord" of voices and instruments</note></term> tending toward His praise be complete in every rigor of the laws and in perfection—as far as human imperfection allows—and be furnished with all means suitable for exciting devotion. And if the expression of the text requires a certain cheerfulness, care must be taken that the Harmony is not stripped of ecclesiastical gravity, modesty, and decorum...

<meta>catchword: tur ;</meta>

<summary>Aloysius argues that a true composer must prioritize musical integrity over popular trends, citing Seneca’s Stoic resolve as a model. He then introduces the Ecclesiastical style as the most noble form of music, demanding the highest level of technical perfection and emotional gravity to suit divine worship.</summary>

<keywords>good taste, musical order, Seneca, patronage, Ecclesiastical style, Church music, divine worship, musical gravity</keywords>

--- Page 257 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on "Good Taste" before transitioning into a detailed study of the Ecclesiastical Style. On the previous page, Aloysius was quoting Seneca to explain that a strong-minded composer remains true to the laws of music regardless of fleeting fashions. He concludes that thought here before starting Lesson VII.</meta>

<page-num>243</page-num>
->**Exercise V. Lesson VII. On the Ecclesiastical Style.**<-

...[is avoided] <note>The text begins mid-sentence, concluding a point about avoiding inappropriate musical novelties</note>; otherwise, the listeners would be distracted into emotions other than those of devotion. Above all, one must strive to ensure that the music applied to the text is fitting, distinct, expressive, and not inconvenient for the singer, but easy to articulate. This is achieved if the composer abstains from applying very small <note>Referring to fast, short-duration notes</note> figures to the words. It would be different if a certain vowel were to be prolonged without pronouncing the word, in which case **diminutions** <term>diminutions: fast melodic passages or "runs" that "divide" a long note into many smaller ones</term> have their place, according to the singer's ability. 

To achieve all this, the composer will imitate a tailor skilled in his art, who exactly measures all the joints of the body, the length, and the width, so that he may make a garment fitting the body as perfectly as possible. In the same way, the text must be "clothed" by the musician; consideration must be given to the meaning and the emotion, so that the music, adapted to the meaning of the words, may seem not only to sing, but even to deliver an oration. 

Furthermore, according to the variety of the text, there are various methods of the **Ecclesiastical Style**: for one must be followed in composing Masses, another in Motets, another in Psalms, another in Hymns, and so on. All these methods can be reduced to two kinds of style: the *Stilo a Cappella* <note>original: "Stylum à Capella"; literally "chapel style"</note>, or full choir style, and the **mixed style**. We shall begin our treatment with the first, namely the *a cappella*, as it is the worthier due to its antiquity.

### On the Stilo a Cappella.

It is well established that in the earliest times the Divine Offices were sung with voices alone. Then, after organs were introduced and over the passage of time, almost every kind of instrument was employed, as current usage clearly demonstrates even now. Thus, two methods of this *Stilo a Cappella* flourish in our present age: without organ and other instruments, for voices only; and with organ and other instruments. 

The first method still prevails in many cathedral churches, and in the Imperial Court <note>original: "Aula Caesarea"; referring to the Habsburg court in Vienna where Fux served</note> during the time of Lent, through the singular piety of our most August Monarch <note>Emperor Charles VI</note> and his observance of Divine worship. In this kind of composition, therefore, first and above all, one must abstain from the **mixed genus** <term>mixed genus: the use of chromaticism or combining different musical scales</term> and from transposed modes filled too heavily with **sharps** <note>original: "Diesibus"</note> or **flats** <note>original: "b mollibus"</note>. One should use only the pure **diatonic genus** <term>diatonic genus: the standard seven-note scale without "extra" chromatic sharps or flats</term>; otherwise, as I have already said elsewhere, the harmony would never achieve the desired effect.

<sig>Ppp 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: Joseph.</meta>

<summary>Fux introduces the Ecclesiastical Style, using the metaphor of a tailor to explain how music must fit the text. He defines the "a cappella" style and mandates the use of the pure diatonic genus for sacred music.</summary>
<keywords>Ecclesiastical Style, Stilo a Cappella, musical tailoring, diatonic genus, liturgical music, Imperial Court, word painting, vocal articulation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Lesson VII from page 243. The dialogue between Joseph (the student) and Aloysius (the master, representing Palestrina) shifts from general aesthetics to the practical requirements of the "Chapel Style" or *stile antico*. Aloysius explains why simplicity is essential for vocal music and introduces his own musical example as a starting point for the student.</meta>

<page-num>244</page-num>
->**Exercise V. Lesson VII. On the Chapel Style.**<-

*Joseph.* For what reason are the mixed genus <note>In this context, Fux refers to mixing different scales or chromaticisms that deviate from the pure diatonic system</note> and transposed modes forbidden from being used?

*Aloys.* I believe the reason was stated in Book I, in that very same place: namely, that for voices unaided by the help of an organ or other instruments, intonation would be extremely difficult. In this kind of composition, great care must be given to both ease and a natural way of singing. For this reason, the **subjects** <term>subject: the primary melodic theme or "point of imitation" upon which a piece is built</term> chosen should be natural and easy, yet not sterile or trivial. And if the brevity of the text and the need to extend the composition to a great length requires frequent repetition of words—as in the *Kyrie* <note>original: "Kyrie" (Lord, have mercy)</note>, *Amen*, and so forth—care must be taken that any boredom arising from this is abundantly compensated for, either by the variety of fertile melodic movement or by the introduction of new subjects. To assist in this matter, the examples of others should be examined. And because you, Joseph, have laid the foundations of composition under my guidance and auspices—and therefore I trust my works will be more familiar to you—I shall present to you as a model the *Kyrie* from my Mass titled *Vicissitudinis* <note>Latin: "Mass of Alternation" or "Mass of Changes"</note>. Thus, you may thereafter progress little by little to examine the more elevated monuments of famous men. Of this style, the undisputed Prince is **Aloysius Praenestinus** <note>Giovanni Pierluigi da Palestrina (c. 1525–1594). Fux viewed Palestrina's works as the ultimate perfection of vocal polyphony</note>, that light of music, whom I recommend to you again and again to imitate if you are driven by a desire for extraordinary progress.

<header>### Exercise V. Lesson VII. on the Chapel Style.</header>

<image-desc>A four-part polyphonic musical score in white mensural notation, illustrating the "Chapel Style" with a setting of the "Kyrie eleison" (Lord, have mercy). The staves use various C-clefs and an F-clef. The text is underlaid across the four voices. In the top staff (Cantus), an "NB." mark is placed above a note in the seventh measure, indicating a specific interval of a seventh reached by a leap, which is discussed in the dialogue below.</image-desc>

Observe, Joseph, the texture of this harmony <note>original: "concentûs," referring to the sounding together of multiple voices</note>, and if you find anything doubtful or beyond your grasp, ask for an explanation.

*Joseph.* I see, and I admire the interweaving of the parts, drawing the subject <term>subject: the primary melodic theme or motif upon which a composition is based</term> so tightly together; where in almost every beat <note>original: "tactus," the basic unit of time or "stroke" in early music</note> the subject is found, now in one part, now in two, with an easy and natural manner of singing and a fullness of harmony. It is so aptly done that the subject itself seems to take the place of the melody. If I had the same faculty for performing as I have for understanding, I could, without vanity, boast of the progress I have made thus far, at least in my own judgment. However, a small doubt was raised in my mind by the last note marked with **NB.** in the seventh beat of the Cantus <note>the highest vocal part, usually the soprano</note> part, where a seventh is reached by a leap. But it has just come to mind that I encountered a similar case in the Lessons: namely, that the diminution <term>diminution: the process of breaking down long notes into shorter, faster notes, often used as ornamentation</term> was omitted there for the sake of the integrity of the subject, for the following reason:

NB.
<image-desc>A short two-staff musical example demonstrating the concept of "Diminution" (ornamental division of a long note). The word "Diminution" is printed between the staves. The top staff shows a melodic line with faster-moving eighth notes as a variation of the simpler, longer notes in the staff below.</image-desc>

*Aloysius.* I admire your attention and your judgment, by which you have pointed out everything worthy of observation in this harmony. I bid you be of good heart. For he who excels in the understanding of things comes closest to the faculty of performing them. Time, which [matures] all things...

<summary>Aloysius and Joseph discuss the technical merits of the previous page's "Kyrie," specifically how the theme (subject) is woven through the voices and why certain melodic leaps are permitted to keep the theme recognizable.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion between the master Aloysius and his student Joseph regarding the "Chapel Style" (stile antico). Following Joseph's observation about a leap of a seventh, Aloysius expands on the structural requirements of interweaving musical themes and how they must adapt to the length of the liturgical text.</meta>

<page-num>247</page-num>

<header>### Exercise V. Lesson VII. on the Chapel Style.</header>

matures and completes [your understanding], and unceasing practice will provide what remains. You have admired the interweaving <note>original: "incatenationem," literally a 'chaining together' of musical lines</note> of the parts, where the subject <term>subject: the principal musical theme or motif used as the basis for a polyphonic composition</term> enters in almost every beat <note>original: "tactu," referring to the pulse or measure of the music</note>. However, you must know that not every subject, without distinction, can be developed in this way. Rather, if your intention is to bind the parts so tightly together, the subject should be tested, modified, or another chosen through careful inquiry before the selection is finalized. Furthermore, you should not overlook the consideration of the union of parts within the limits of the lines <note>referring to the vocal ranges or the boundaries of the musical staff</note>, which adds the greatest power of harmony to the composition.

The composition of the Chapel Style should be established according to the norm of this example, or a similar one, where the brevity of the text requires it—as in the *Kyrie* <note>Lord have mercy</note> and the *Amen*. For a longer text, such as the *Gloria* <note>Glory to God in the highest</note> or the *Credo* <note>the Creed</note>, a different method of composition is required, where a new subject must be adapted to every new phrase <note>original: "periodo," meaning a section of text or a musical period</note>. Moreover, the subject must be meaningful—suited not only to the music but also, as I have said elsewhere, expressing the meaning and the emotion <note>original: "affectum," the specific mood or rhetorical 'affection' of the piece</note> of the text.

*Joseph.* What if a subject that is otherwise suited to both the music and the text cannot be introduced conveniently?

*Aloysius.* It can be, if it is correctly arranged. First, the part that is to introduce a new subject should be preceded by some length of rest. Meanwhile, the other parts will direct the melody in such a way that—once the new subject is conceived in your mind—you will have the opportunity to enter either during the melody itself or at the cadence <term>cadence: a melodic or harmonic formula that concludes a musical phrase; original: "Clauſula"</term>, or more rarely, after the cadence has been completed. And because it would be too long to present an entire Mass <note>original: "Miſſam"</note> here, I will place an Offertory <note>original: "Offertorium," a chant sung during the Liturgy of the Eucharist</note> before your eyes. Its composition—except for the *Kyrie* and *Amen*—almost coincides with the rest of the text of the Mass. From this ensemble, you will be able to learn the art of adapting the subject to the text and entering at the appropriate time.

---

# Exercise V. Lesson VII. on the Chapel Style <note>original: "de Stylo à Capella." This refers to the tradition of vocal music intended for church use, often following strict rules of counterpoint derived from the Renaissance.</note>

<image-desc>Two systems of polyphonic musical notation for four voices: Soprano (Cantus), Alto (Altus), Tenor, and Bass (Bassus). The music is written in white mensural notation. The lyrics in the first system are: "non confundentur" (shall not be confounded). The lyrics in the second system conclude the phrase: "non confundentur." The lines show imitative counterpoint, where each voice enters with the same melodic idea at different times.</image-desc>

[In the first system, the voices continue the previous phrase:]

- **Soprano:** [they] shall not be con-found-ed, [they] shall not be con-found-ed
- **Alto:** [they] shall not be con-found-ed, [they] shall not be con-found-ed, [they] shall not be con-
- **Tenor:** [they] shall not be con-found-ed
- **Bass:** [they] shall not be con-found-ed, [they] shall not be con-

[In the second system, the phrase concludes:]

- **Soprano:** [they] shall not be con-found-ed.
- **Alto:** -found-ed.
- **Tenor:** -ed, [they] shall not be con-found-ed.
- **Bass:** -found-ed.

Look, Joseph, here is a <term>harmony<note>original: "concentum," referring to the sounding together of multiple independent voices.</note></term> bound together by <term>subjects<note>The "subject" is the primary melodic theme that is passed from one voice to another in imitative music.</note></term> linked continuously like a chain. First, consider and penetrate the meaning of the text: *To you, O Lord, have I lifted up my soul.* <note>Psalm 25:1. In the Latin Vulgate, this is "Ad te Domine levavi animam meam."</note> And you will discover that the subject—as the voice grows and always ascends in the manner of one praying with confidence—is perfectly suited to the meaning of the words. Observe then the second part entering before the first part has finished the subject; and how, through a brief melodic movement using a <term>feigned cadence<note>original: "Clausulam fictam." In counterpoint, this is a technique where a musical resolution is expected but the composer "fakes" it or alters it to allow the music to continue seamlessly into a new section or voice entry.</note></term>, the opportunity to enter is given to the third part, namely the Tenor. Once this is done, the Soprano and Alto, in place of that melodic movement and having adopted another secondary theme for the words: *my soul*, continue to play together quite gracefully in the meantime.

<meta>catchword: Furthermore,</meta>

<summary>This page analyzes a polyphonic musical setting of Psalm 25, explaining how the rising melodic "subject" mimics the spiritual act of lifting one's soul to God. It highlights technical aspects of the "Chapel Style," specifically how overlapping voice entries and "feigned cadences" create a continuous, flowing texture.</summary>
<keywords>Chapel Style, polyphony, imitative counterpoint, feigned cadence, Psalm 25, music theory, vocal parts, word painting</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of a polyphonic setting of Psalm 25. The teacher, Aloysius, is explaining how the musical themes (subjects) are introduced in each voice to mirror the meaning of the Latin prayer, while the student, Joseph, identifies a specific chord progression.</meta>

<page-num>255</page-num>

->### Exercise V. Lesson VII. on the Chapel Style<-

Moreover, so that the melody would not be stretched out too long with useless repetition of words, a rest was placed in the middle, and the **Alto** <note>original: "Altus"</note> imitates the subject under the word: *I have lifted up* <note>original: "levavi"</note>; the **Soprano** <note>original: "Cantus"</note> also takes up this imitation shortly after its own rest. From there, the voices, modulating for a while with a sort of subject under the words: *my soul* <note>original: "animam meam"</note>, form a <term>cadence<note>original: "clausulam"; a melodic or harmonic conclusion to a musical phrase.</note></term> in D. In this cadence, the Soprano—following a rest—introduces a new subject with a new period of text. The expressive power of this subject under the words: *my God* <note>original: "Deus meus"</note>, seems to me not unworthy of consideration. Observe, furthermore, how closely the parts press down this subject in every beat, so that it seems almost to be drawn out of the mouth of another; until, with the words: *in you I trust* <note>original: "in te confido"</note>, another subject, not at all foreign to the meaning of the text, is proposed by the **Bass**. This is taken up by the other parts and then mixed with the previous subject, *my God*, and drawn out until the cadence on *B-flat* <note>original: "B, fa." In the hexachord system used in this period, "B-fa" specifically denotes B-flat, while "B-mi" denotes B-natural.</note>.

*Joseph.* I see a progression here that has never occurred to me through the whole course of these lessons: namely, moving from one <term>fifth<note>A musical interval spanning five notes of the scale; in strict counterpoint, two perfect fifths in a row are usually forbidden.</note></term> to another fifth, though by <term>contrary motion<note>When one musical voice moves up while the other moves down.</note></term>. For example:

<image-desc>A musical notation example in two staves showing a transition between two chords. The upper staff contains the notes A followed by D. The lower staff contains the notes D followed by G. This creates two perfect fifths (D to A, then G to D) moving in opposite directions.</image-desc>

*Aloysius.* You have noticed correctly. For although the rules do not strictly forbid that progression, because two perfect <term>consonances<note>Intervals that sound stable or "sweet," such as octaves and fifths.</note></term> of the same type offer very little variety, it should not be used without grave necessity, especially in a <term>four-voice composition<note>original: "Quatricinio."</note></term>. Here, the necessity of introducing the subject, which could not have happened otherwise, excuses that progression. Just as quite often a slight <term>license<note>A deliberate breaking of a strict musical rule for the sake of a higher artistic purpose.</note></term> should by no means be denied for the sake of binding subjects closely together. This is clearly seen in the following subject, where instead of a leap of a third, a leap of a fourth is used; and instead of a step, a leap of a third is frequently employed for the reason mentioned above: all for the sake of introducing the subject sooner. Consider next the subject with the following phrase: *let me not be ashamed* <note>original: "non erubescam"</note>, entering in the Bass under the aforementioned cadence, repeated in tight succession by the other parts, and drawn out to a cadence in F. There, the Bass again produces a new subject with the phrase: *nor let my enemies mock me* <note>original: "neque irrideant me inimici mei"</note>, which is perfectly suited to the sense of the words, and which then continues in a long stretch with the parts tightly joined.

*Joseph.* In the development of the first subject, I have [per]ceived the hypothesis... <note>The sentence ends abruptly as it reaches the bottom of the page.</note>
