<meta>catchword: ceived,</meta>

<summary>This page describes the technical construction of a four-part choral work, explaining how melodic subjects are adapted to fit the text of Psalm 25. Aloysius justifies using a "license"—the forbidden movement between two fifths—because it allows the musical themes to enter more effectively.</summary>

...I have seen, where two **fifths** <note>original: "Quintæ"</note> are found following one another immediately in this manner:

[Four musical staves showing short melodic fragments. The second and third examples display vertical intervals of a fifth, marked with the number '5' between the staves. The fourth example uses an F-clef on the bottom staff.]

<column-break/>

Where the **Tenor** seems to take the place of the **Bass**.

*Aloysius.* Several authors judge it so. However, the lower range of the **Tenor** <note>original: "vox remissior Tenoris"</note> easily demonstrates to the ears that the second fifth, which the **Tenor** creates, does not result from the preceding fifth, but from a **third** <note>original: "Tertia"</note>; nor is it a matter of such importance that the <term>development<note>original: "deductio"; the way a musical theme is led or unfolded through the different voices.</note></term> of the <term>subject<note>original: "subjecti"; the primary musical theme or idea.</note></term> should suffer or be developed less beautifully. From what has been said so far, you will easily conduct an examination of the rest of the composition <note>original: "Concentûs"</note>,

and furthermore, you will notice that words which by themselves signify nothing are by no means to be repeated. It remains for me to show you a composition in this style with the requirement of a <term>Cantus Firmus<note>original: "Cantûs firmi"; a pre-existing "fixed song" melody used as the basis of a polyphonic composition.</note></term> and the application of the text, from which you will see the manner of introducing the subjects.

->### Exercise V. Lesson VII. on the Chapel Style<-

You have therefore, *Joseph*, a formula for composition elaborated according to the obligation of the <term>Cantus firmus</term> <note>literally "fixed song"; a pre-existing melody, usually from Gregorian plainchant, that serves as the foundation for a polyphonic musical work.</note>, or Gregorian chant. If the subjects <note>musical themes or melodic ideas used as the basis for a section of music.</note> are less singable, and not as expressive as in the previous examples, attribute this to the restriction of the *Cantus firmus*: in this genre of composition, one does not have the free choice to select just any subjects, but only those that can be joined to the Choral Chant; besides the fact that the subjects are mostly extracted from the Gregorian Chant itself, or established in imitation of it. As for the words, which are perhaps repeated more often than is necessary, attribute this to the pace and brevity of the Choral Chant, which must nevertheless last for the entire <term>Offertory</term> <note>the part of the Mass where bread and wine are offered; here referring to the musical setting for that liturgical moment.</note>. Although on one hand such obligations take away no small amount of ornament from the composition, I know not what the sound of the *Cantus firmus* adds on the other hand—soothing and breathing devotion—that immediately seizes the attention of the listeners.

*Joseph.* I remember that you warned a little while ago that subjects should be introduced during the <term>cadence</term> <note>original: "Clausulam"; a musical conclusion or "punctuation mark" at the end of a phrase.</note> itself, but rarely after the cadence has finished; however, I see the opposite in this composition.

*Aloysius.* And I say that this departure must be granted to the restriction of the *Cantus firmus*: inasmuch as subjects drawn from the source of the Gregorian Chant itself cannot be woven in everywhere at will. You will easily observe, however, that the perpetual motion (for the sake of which this rule was given) is by no means interrupted, but is sustained all the way to the end. Let these examples of the <term>Chapel Style</term> <note>original: "stylo à Capella"; church music for voices alone, characterized by a strict, old-fashioned polyphonic texture.</note> without organ and instruments suffice. Once these have been surveyed and exactly weighed, the works of other famous authors of greater artifice should be taken in hand for consideration.

*Joseph.* But what should be observed in composing <term>Psalms</term> and <term>Hymns</term> in this style?

*Aloysius.* Nothing peculiar indeed, except that for those—especially Hymns, being versified—you should apply music that savors somewhat of a song-like melody; so much so that it is not worth the effort to hand down anything specific about them. Let us proceed, therefore, to the Chapel Style equipped with the organ and other instruments, which enjoys a greater freedom both of melody, of singing, and of wandering. Let the *Kyrie* from my <term>Mass</term> named *Solace in Weeping* <note>original: "in Fletu solatium." This is one of Fux's most famous masses, composed in 1714 after the death of his patron, Emperor Joseph I.</note> serve as an example for you.

<meta>catchword: Tutti, [indicating the entry of the full choir/orchestra]</meta>

<summary>Aloysius explains how the constraints of using a pre-existing Gregorian chant (Cantus firmus) dictate the musical structure and theme selection in the Chapel Style. The lesson concludes by transitioning from purely vocal music to the accompanied style, citing Fux's own "Missa in Fletu Solatium."</summary>
<keywords>Cantus firmus, Chapel Style, polyphony, composition rules, musical subjects, cadences, Johann Joseph Fux, Missa in Fletu Solatium, liturgical music</keywords>

### Exercise V. Lesson VII. on the Chapel Style <note>original: "de Stylo à Capella"</note>

<note>The following text refers to the musical score of the "Kyrie eleison" (Lord, have mercy) displayed on the page, which features three vocal parts and a figured bass for the organist.</note>

Consider first, Joseph <note>Joseph is the student addressed throughout this manual</note>, the natural and harmonious way the singing parts move; although they are joined by a tight bond, one part is in no way an obstacle to another. Observe furthermore a melodic movement <note>original: "modulationem"</note> that is not at all common, but flows naturally into related musical modes. Notice also how the voices rest at the appropriate times, so that the composition is not stuffed with a superfluous and useless entanglement of parts; and how, when all these things are observed, nothing is lost from the fullness of the harmony. 

Since this style can also be used in a three-voice composition <term>Tricinio: a musical work written specifically for three independent parts</term> (which I have used not infrequently and with some success), I will also provide here as an example the *Christe* <note>The "Christe eleison" (Christ, have mercy) is the second movement of the Mass</note> of this Mass, written for three voices.

# Exercise V. Lesson VII. on the Chapel Style <note>original: "de Stylo à Capella"</note>

<page-num>267</page-num>

->**[Musical Score]**<-
<note>The image shows two systems of musical notation for three voices and a figured bass (basso continuo). The lyrics are "Christe eleison" (Christ, have mercy). The diamond-shaped notes and the numerals under the bottom staff—such as 6, 5 6, and 7 6 5—guide the organist on which harmonies to play.</note>

This three-voice piece <term>Tricinium</term> <note>a composition specifically for three independent parts</note> in a freer chapel style was composed more for the sake of melodic flow <note>original: "modulationis"</note> and imitation than for the strict requirement <note>original: "obligationem"</note> of sticking to a specific subject, except at the very beginning. In this style, after a cadence <note>original: "Clausulam"</note> and a brief rest, a new musical idea <term>phantasia</term> is always introduced; when this idea is taken up and imitated by the other parts, it presents a melody that is quite pleasing to the ear. This melody is all the more perceptible because the parts generally move following one another through oblique motion <term>motum obliquum</term> <note>a technique where one voice stays on the same note while another moves, helping the listener distinguish the different parts</note>. 

However, one must take care that a composition of this proportion is not sung with too hurried a beat <note>original: "concitatiori mensurâ"</note>. And because this style closely approaches the preceding "chapel style without organ" in its sacred solemnity <note>original: "gravitate Ecclesiastica"</note>, and is more commonly accepted in modern practice, it will not be out of place to offer you one more example to consider: specifically the *Amen* from my Mass: *I believe in one God* <note>original: "Credo in unum Deum"</note>.

<sig>Xxx 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: All together <note>original: "Tutti"</note></meta>

### Exercise V. Lesson VII. On the Chapel Style <note>original: "de Stylo à Capella"</note>

<page-num>271</page-num>

<header>Exercise V. Lesson VII. On the Chapel Style.</header>

->**[Musical Score]**<-

<note>This score concludes the polyphonic "Amen" for five voices. The upper four staves end on a long, final note (longa) on the syllable "men." The bass staff (bottom) shows a "9 8" suspension—a common musical device where a dissonant ninth is held over before resolving down to a stable octave—over the final syllable.</note>

**Aloys.** Consider, Joseph, the movement of the parts joined with the subject, governed by the correct principle of consonances <note>original: "Concordantiarum"</note> and a perpetual chain of dissonances. Observe the melodic flow <note>original: "modulationem"</note>, which, though natural and easy, is by no means ordinary. Furthermore, weigh carefully the connection of the parts, in which the greatest power of harmony resides, and the never-interrupted motion maintained until the very end.

**Joseph.** I see and admire everything you mention; however, it seems that the dissonances in this ensemble <note>original: "concentu"</note> are resolved by ascending, which is contrary to your prohibition.

**Aloys.** What you touch upon here, Joseph, was also raised as an objection to me by a certain excellent musician regarding this *Amen*. He did not notice that that note—namely, the eighth note <term>fusa</term> <note>A "fusa" is an eighth note in the notation of this period, characterized by a flag or a beam.</note> ascending after the dissonance—is a figure of variation <note>a melodic ornament or "diminution" used to decorate a simpler line</note>; it was added for the sake of rhythmic movement and a better melody. Look at the underlying substance:

->**[Musical Score]**<-

<note>This simplified example shows the "substance" Aloys refers to. It strips away the decorative eighth notes to show that the structural resolution of the "9" (the dissonance) actually moves downward to the "8" (the consonance), following the strict rules. The ascending note Joseph saw was merely a decorative leap between the dissonance and its true resolution.</note>

**Joseph.** I am annoyed with myself for overlooking such a clear matter; therefore, I shall henceforth take greater care in identifying figures of variation before I pass judgment.

<sig>Y y y 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: One (original: Unum)</meta>

<summary>Aloys explains that apparent violations of counterpoint rules, such as ascending dissonance resolutions, are often decorative "figures of variation" that do not change the underlying structural correctness of the harmony. This highlights the distinction between the simple structural foundation and the embellished "Chapel Style" surface.</summary>

<page-num>272</page-num>

### Exercise V. Lesson VII. On the Chapel Style.

With your permission, Master, I will propose one more doubt.

**Aloys.** Speak out.

**Joseph.** I remember you forbade that accidental sharps <term>dieses</term> <note>original: "Dieſes accidentaliter." In this period, a "diesis" typically refers to a sharp sign (#) used to raise a note by a half-step.</note> should be doubled. Yet in the ninth measure of this *Amen*, I see a sharp placed in the Alto and doubled in the Tenor. For example:

->**[Musical Example]**<-
<note>The notation shows a four-part texture where both the second voice (Alto) and third voice (Tenor) are playing a note marked with a sharp sign, indicated by small 'X' marks to highlight the overlap.</note>

**Aloys.** I certainly said that. Because a sharp, having great force in itself and no small amount of harshness, when doubled, overpowers the other parts in strength and destroys the equilibrium of the harmony. The prohibition therefore holds where the doubling of the sharp dwells for a long time. However, that Tenor note marked with the sharp is of such short duration <note>literally "so small a value"</note> that the inconvenience, which would otherwise result from a longer delay, is not of such great importance that the elegant order of the texture—required by both the melodic flow and the subject—should be interrupted for its sake.

**Joseph.** Since in this style instruments are also collaborators, what should be done with them? I am not ignorant of the fact that trombones <term>tubas ductiles</term> <note>Literally "flexible tubes," referring to sackbuts or early trombones commonly used to double inner vocal parts.</note> are usually accustomed to sounding in unison with the Alto and Tenor; but what should be assigned to the strings <term>fidibus</term>?

**Aloys.** I affirm that in this Chapel Style, both the first and second violins <note>original: "primis, & ſecundis Fidibus"</note> must proceed in unison with the Soprano <term>cantu</term>.

**Joseph.** Yet I often hear and see the strings playing something else in compositions.

**Aloys.** That is true. But what are they doing? Making disproportionate, awkward leaps that have no singability; so much so that, wandering improperly up and down, they confuse the harmony they were brought in to supplement rather than promote it. For if a four-part composition <term>quatricinium</term> is perfected according to the rules of the art, no space can remain for a fifth part to introduce a subject. Understand the same regarding a three-part composition <term>tricinio</term>. For the rest, it all consists of a very poor method of singing.

**Joseph.** What if the first violins, as is the custom for many, sounded in the octave with the Alto?

**Aloys.** If the doubling of the voices, with the trombone added, is so great that it has the power to cover the sequence of octaves, it can be tolerated; but if, due to the fewness of the voices, the octaves are per-
<meta>catchword: -ceived [cipiun-]</meta>

<summary>Aloys and Joseph discuss the technicalities of doubling accidentals and the proper role of instruments in the "Chapel Style," emphasizing that instruments should reinforce the vocal lines rather than play independent, unmelodic parts.</summary>

<keywords>accidentals, diesis, Chapel Style, trombones, sackbuts, violins, instrumental doubling, polyphony, four-part composition, musical texture</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the teacher Aloys and the student Joseph. Having discussed the use of trombones to support vocal parts on the previous page, Aloys now moves to the use of violins and the overall goals of sacred composition.</meta>

<page-num>273</page-num>

### Exercise V. Lesson VII. On the Mixed Style.

[...if they are] perceived <note>original: "cipiuntur," likely a continuation of *percipiuntur* from the previous page.</note> alone, they certainly smack of a crude style. For this reason, I think you should take the safer advice of joining both violins <term>ambas Fides</term> <note>While "fides" can mean "faith," in this musical context it refers to stringed instruments, specifically the two violin parts common in church trios.</note> to the vocal part. Regarding the other parts of the Mass, as well as the Offertories, Psalms, and Hymns, etc., the music must be directed according to the variety of the text, as was said before, yet with a greater liberty of wandering further afield than in the style without Organ <note>The *a cappella* or "strict" style.</note>.

**Joseph.** What should a composer observe when he is about to furnish a composition with several subjects <term>subiectis</term> <note>In counterpoint, a "subject" is the primary melodic theme used as the building block for a piece.</note>?

**Aloys.** As far as the music is concerned, I demonstrated above that this must be achieved with the help of double counterpoint. Furthermore, you must take care—besides the fact that the subjects must be distinct in their rhythmic values, suited to the text, and expressive—that phrases having contrary meanings are not joined together. For example: *He was crucified* <note>original: "Crucifixus"</note> and *He rose again* <note>original: "Resurrexit"</note>. From this, an entanglement and confusion of meanings would arise. This must be avoided with great attention and judgment. Now let us proceed to discuss:

---

### On the Mixed Style.

By "mixed style," I mean a composition that sometimes concerts with one, two, three, or even more voices with instruments mixed in, and sometimes is furnished with a full choir. This is the style most especially in use in churches today. Since it is common and very frequently heard, I do not think it is worth the effort to discuss it at length or illustrate it with examples—except to warn you, Joseph, that you should not forget the end and goal of Church music. Namely, its purpose is to stir up devotion and serve the Divine worship. Do not confuse this style with the theatrical or dance-like style, as is the habit of many.

On the other hand, do not fall into a tiresome gloominess by adopting sterile ideas that have no "juice" <note>original: "succum," meaning vitality or creative energy.</note> or life, under the impression that this is "sacred" music; such a style produces boredom rather than devotion. Instead, take care that the harmony is pleasing to the ear and flows into the minds of the listeners with delight. Thus, finally, mindful of everything that has been said in warning throughout this long course, you will establish your composition so that it may be meritorious before God and not lack praise among men.

<sig>Z z z</sig>
<meta>catchword: On</meta>

<summary>Aloys explains the "Mixed Style" of church music, which combines voices and instruments. He emphasizes that sacred music should be expressive and pleasing to the ear to inspire devotion, avoiding both theatrical dance rhythms and sterile, boring academicism.</summary>
<keywords>Mixed Style, Church Music, Sacred Music, Violins, Double Counterpoint, Liturgical Decorum, Joseph, Aloys</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Lesson VII from the previous page. While the previous section focused on the "Mixed Style" of sacred music, this page introduces the principles of "Recitative Style" and how musical composition should mirror the emotional delivery of a public speaker.</meta>

<page-num>274</page-num>
<header>Exercise V. Lesson VII. On the Recitative Style</header>

### On the Recitative Style.

The **Recitative style** <note>original: "Stylus recitativus"</note> is nothing other than speech expressed through musical measures, or **oratorical delivery** <note>original: "Oratoria elocutio"</note>. For just as a **Speaker** <note>original: "Declamator"</note> varies his voice according to the different types of speech—sometimes urging on, sometimes relaxing, now raising the voice, now lowering it—he strives to take on the character of the emotion <note>original: "affectûs"</note> which he has conceived in his mind to express. A musical Composer must do the same, according to the variety of the text.

In recitative that is close to daily speech, the music should be set with a somewhat relaxed voice. In moments of tension <term>contentione</term> <note>Referring to conflict, dispute, or intense dramatic action in the text.</note>, a sharp voice, sometimes similar to shouting, should be employed, with frequent changes in the bass <note>original: "crebra Bassi mutatione." In this context, frequent harmonic shifts or movements in the accompaniment emphasize the agitation of the text.</note>. For a text breathing dignity, music full of solemnity <note>original: "gravitate"</note> should be applied, with rare changes in the bass; this is especially suited to the **Church style**, which is often used with instruments added. For example:

[A musical score illustrates the **Recitative style**. It consists of five musical staves. The top three staves contain melody lines for instruments. The fourth staff is the vocal line, featuring the lyrics: *O Lord, do not rebuke me in your anger...* <note>original Latin: "Domine ne in furore tuo arguas me, ne-" from Psalm 6.</note>. The fifth staff is the **basso continuo** line with **figured bass** notation <note>A shorthand system using numbers and symbols above the bass line to tell the keyboard player which chords to improvise.</note> including flats, sharps, and the numbers 6, 4, 7, 2, and 3.]

<meta>musical catchword at bottom right</meta>

<summary>This page defines recitative style as musical oratory, explaining how a composer must vary the vocal delivery and bass movement to match the emotional content of the text, from casual speech to solemn church music.</summary>
<keywords>recitative style, oratorical delivery, musical expression, basso continuo, church style, vocal declamation, Psalm 6</keywords>

The composer uses the **Recitative style** to emphasize the pleading nature of the prayer, allowing the rhythm of the words to dictate the musical flow.</note>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Joseph and Aloysius from page 275. They are concluding the discussion on the sacred Recitative style (using the text of Psalm 6) and transitioning into how Recitative is used in secular contexts like opera and chamber music.</meta>

<page-num>276</page-num>
<header>Exercise V. Lesson VII. On the Recitative Style.</header>

<image-desc>Five systems of musical notation printed with movable type. The first three systems show three-part instrumental or organ accompaniment using C-clefs and F-clefs with diamond-shaped notes. The fourth system includes a vocal line with a G-clef and lyrics underneath, accompanied by a bass line. The lyrics read: "Do mi ne quo ni am con tur ba ta sunt os sa me - a." The final system shows a two-part continuation of the accompaniment.</image-desc>

*Joseph.* What do I see? It appears that the harmony of the instruments, especially in the handling of dissonances <note>original: "dissonantiarum Hypothesibus"</note>, departs entirely from the rules of **Counterpoint** <note>The rules governing how different musical lines interact harmonically.</note>.

*Aloys.* I say that this departure must be granted to the nature of the **Recitative** <term>Recitative: a style of singing that imitates the natural rhythms and inflections of speech</term>, in which, because the Bass does not move in the same way that dissonances are usually resolved, the treatment cannot occur in the customary manner. Furthermore, in this style, one does not look for a strict account of harmony as much as for the expression of the emotions <note>original: "sensuum expressionis"</note>, for the sake of which this style was introduced. In roughly this way—or slightly differently depending on the variety of the text—the Recitative can be established when we pour out our feelings toward God in humble prayer. As for **secular music** <note>original: "Musica profana"</note>, namely Chamber and Theatrical music, reason teaches that it must be done differently, because the purpose is different.

Thus, secular music is established for the sake of refreshing the minds of the listeners and drawing them into various **affects** <note>The "Doctrine of the Affections" was a Baroque theory that music could systematically trigger specific emotions in the listener.</note>. The emotions to be expressed in recitative are generally the following: Anger, Pity, Fear, Force, Distress, Pleasure, and Love.

Anger should be expressed by a kind of incited voice, tending toward the high notes, and if it is more vehement, with a certain cry expressed with a broken voice. This is almost always achieved with smaller note shapes <note>shorter rhythmic values</note> ascending toward the high range, and with frequent changes in the Bass

<meta>The catchword "tione" completes the word "mutatione" (change) from the previous line.</meta>

<vocab>Recitative, Counterpoint, Secular Music, Affects, Chamber Music, Theatrical Music, Joseph, Aloysius</vocab>

<summary>In this lesson, the teacher Aloysius explains that the emotional expression of Recitative justifies breaking traditional rules of counterpoint, and he begins to categorize how different human emotions (the "affects") should be depicted in secular music.</summary>
<keywords>Recitative style, counterpoint rules, doctrine of the affections, musical expression, secular music, Baroque music theory, vocal technique</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the speech by Aloysius (the teacher) from page 276. He is explaining how a singer's delivery must change based on the specific emotion (affect) of the text and the social status of the character being portrayed. He also begins a technical explanation of how musical cadences in recitative should correspond to grammatical punctuation marks.</meta>

<page-num>277</page-num>

### Exercise V. Lesson VII. On the Recitative Style.

...is achieved through delivery <note>original: "pronuntiatione"</note>. In this matter, it is of the greatest importance to consider the social standing of the person who is angry. For if it is a King: he shall by no means descend into feminine shrieking <note>original: "vociferationem muliebrem"</note>; rather, while preserving the dignity of his Majesty, he will manifest his indignation with royal gravity. This principle must be observed in all other emotions <term>Affects: the various emotional states, such as anger or joy, that Baroque music was intended to represent or provoke</term> as well.

**Compassion** <note>original: "Commiseratio"</note> requires a mournful voice, sometimes interrupted, which is seen to be expressed with broader musical figures and more frequent dissonances, while the **Bass** <note>The lowest musical part that provides the harmonic foundation</note> remains on the same note for a longer period of time.

**Fear** demands a low, hesitant voice.

**Strength** is displayed with a vehement, intense voice, supported by a certain gravity.

**Pleasure** uses a type of voice that is effusive, yet smooth and relaxed.

The emotion of **Love** is expressed with a flattering, tender, and affectionate voice. Since, however, love is often accompanied by other emotions, these must also be given the highest consideration.

Furthermore, the following parts of Speech must be exactly observed: the Comma, Colon, Semicolon, Period, Question Mark, Exclamation Point, and Parenthesis. A specific musical ending <note>original: "definentia"</note> should be assigned to nearly every one of these.

If a **Comma** requires a cadence, it should be done in the following way. For example:

<image-desc>A musical score fragment with two staves. The top staff uses a C-clef (soprano) and the bottom staff uses an F-clef (bass). The notation shows a simple melodic line in diamonds and oval notes ending with a decorative flourish above a bass note, illustrating the musical setting of a "Comma".</image-desc>

The **Semicolon** is noted with almost the same music. A Comma is very often continued without a break, as when the meaning of the text must be delivered with a certain haste.

The **Colon** generally ends in the following way:

<image-desc>A second musical score fragment with two staves, similar to the first. It illustrates the musical cadence for a "Colon". The top staff shows melodic movement over a static bass note on the bottom staff, ending with a cadence.</image-desc>

In the same way, the **Period** <note>original: "Punctum"</note> is noted if the sentence and the thought are indeed finished, but the discourse nevertheless continues on the same subject. If, however, an entirely different speech is introduced, a formal cadence <term>clausula formalis: a standard harmonic conclusion that signals the end of a musical section</term> should be used, though it is usually truncated <note>original: "truncata"</note>. For example:

->The **Exclamation Point** <note>original: "Signum Admirationis, vel Exclamationis"</note> is almost always indicated in the following manner.<-

<image-desc>Musical notation in two staves showing a specific melodic and harmonic gesture for an exclamation, involving a sharp accidental.</image-desc>

A phrase separated by **Parentheses**, by which the speech is usually intended to be directed only to the audience so as not to be heard by the other actors <note>this describes the theatrical device known as an "aside"</note>, must be delivered with a softer voice. However, all these things are learned more through judgment, practice, and the study of works by proven authors than by formal rules.

*Joseph.* So much for the Recitative. What rule do you give for composing **Arias**, as we commonly call them?

*Aloys.* What certainty could I establish regarding "free music" <term>Musica arbitraria: music governed by the composer's taste and current fashion rather than strict, unchanging rules of counterpoint</term>, which is subject to change almost every five years? This pursuit of novelty I do not criticize at all; rather, I praise it most highly. Indeed, if a man of middle age, in the fashion of...

<meta>catchword: quin- (likely "quinquaginta" or "quinquennium")</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the technical rules for setting punctuation marks in recitative and begins a dialogue on the nature of arias and the ever-changing fashions of "free music."</summary>

<keywords>recitative style, punctuation in music, period, question mark, exclamation, parentheses, arias, free music, musical fashion, Aloysius Fux</keywords>

--- Page 293 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dialogue between Aloysius and Joseph. The text begins mid-sentence, concluding an analogy comparing musical style to changing fashions in clothing.</meta>

<page-num>279</page-num>

<header>Exercise V. Lesson VII. on the Recitative Style.</header>

...used fifty or sixty years ago were to walk about today, he would certainly expose himself to the risk of being laughed at. In the same way, ***Music*** must also be adapted to the times. But I have not yet seen a ***Tailor*** <note>original: "Sartor"</note>, however much he was poured into the pursuit of novelty, who would have attached the sleeves of a tunic to the thigh or the knees; nor has any ***Architect*** ever been found so foolish as to have placed the foundation of a building upon the roof. Yet we see and hear this everywhere in ***Music***, not without the grief of the intelligent and the disgrace of the art: where, by inverting the precepts of nature and art, the foundation is pushed out of its proper place and forced into the heights, while the other parts are depressed, with no regard given to the foundation. For this reason, you indeed shall strive, ***Joseph***, in your own time and with all your strength, toward novelty and invention; but by no means by overthrowing the rules of art, which imitates and perfects nature, but does not destroy it.

And so, ***Joseph***, once these things have been reduced to a habit and ease through diligent practice, I hope that nothing will be lacking for you to attain the fame of an excellent ***Composer***.

***Joseph.*** Do you seem to me, venerable ***Master***, to be about to put an end to the ***Work***?

***Aloysius.*** Indeed I am. Do you not notice the numbness and lethargy—the usual heralds of my imminent infirmity, namely ***Gout*** <note>original: "Podagra"; a form of inflammatory arthritis, common in the 18th century, that caused Fux great suffering in his later years</note>? You know, moreover, that I am now so broken by both years and nearly intermittent poor health, that if the disease were to attack me with its usual bitterness and hold me in its customary way for the space of six months, a not unfounded fear enters my mind that this time I might not be able to struggle out of it. Therefore, lest this ***Little Work*** <note>original: "Opusculum"</note>, besides its other imperfections, also lack an ending, I place the end to it here.

***Joseph.*** Will you then hand down nothing regarding ***Composition*** for many voices?

***Aloysius.*** It was certainly my intention to insert a treatise on ***Composition*** for many voices into this very work: but interrupted, as you see, by ill health and now confined to my bed, I shall perform that hereafter by publishing a specific ***Treatise***, if it should seem right to God, the Thrice Best and Greatest, to prolong my life and grant me strength. By His benefit, you will be able to be taught the remaining things worth knowing without the living voice of a ***Master***. In the meantime, I would wish you to be persuaded that for one who has the power to complete a perfect ***Four-part piece*** <term>quatricinium: a composition for four voices, which Fux considered the foundation of all polyphony</term>, the way is already paved for the ***Composition*** of many parts; for as the abundance of voices increases, the rigor of the rules is somewhat relaxed. Farewell, and pray to God for me.

---

->F I N I S.<-

<summary>Aloysius concludes the musical treatise by warning Joseph to respect the foundational rules of music even when seeking novelty. He ends the lessons early due to his failing health and a looming bout of gout, noting that mastery of four-part writing is the gateway to all larger compositions.</summary>

<keywords>musical foundation, music theory, composition rules, gout, Johann Joseph Fux, polyphony, four-part writing, Baroque aesthetics</keywords>
